Uncertainty visualization: literature and API proposal

A literature review of IEEE VIS 2024 to 2026 and an API proposal with rendered specs, for GoFish issue #980 (distribution-valued data). Compiled 2026-10-30.

Summary Proposal ggdist specs ggdibbler specs ggdibbler side by side Theory Scales and densities Blur as a limit Stable layouts One limit GoFish over draws Catalog Papers A specs Papers B specs Types and schema Mark definitions 2024 triage 2025 triage 2026 triage

This page reviews the uncertainty visualization papers at IEEE VIS 2024, 2025 and 2026. It covers every paper accepted at the Workshop on Uncertainty Visualization in those three years and the uncertainty papers in the main conference. Each paper is judged against #980 (distribution-valued data) and the design note internals/design/distributions.md. The adjacent issues are #1231, #635, #1091, #971, #256 and #48.

The ggdibbler side by side tab puts ggdibbler code beside the proposed GoFish spec, with renders of both, for Harriet Mason. The Theory tab asks how its continuous mapping theorem relates to Kindlmann and Scheidegger's algebraic design and to GoFish's type checks.

Three tabs from 2026-11-04 follow up. Scales and densities works out how a scale changes a density's height. Blur as a limit proposes sample({ n: Infinity }) with an averaging group, so that blur and fades are the limit of overlaid draws. Stable layouts gives a type rule for sorted bars and treemaps under draws.

On 2026-11-06, One limit works out what overlaid and partitioned draws tend to as their number grows. Both tend to the expected picture, what survives depends on whether the channel is linear, and sorting draws within a cell gives the ggdist marks. GoFish over draws then shows that every chart in ggdist and ggdibbler is an ordinary GoFish flow over a draw key, and compares real renders from both packages with the GoFish spec for each. Catalog lists the 218 charts used in that check, with each package render, its GoFish spec, its fit class and its verdict under the rules.

The design note already covers ggdist, ggdibbler, R distributional, posterior::rvar, Uncertain<T>, probability monads, Vega-Lite and No More, No Less. This page reports only what is new beyond those. It is a map of the design space and does not pick a design.

Terms used on this page

Distribution
A description of which values an uncertain quantity could take and how likely each one is, e.g., "vote share is about 52%, give or take 3 points". The design note writes Dist<T> for a distribution over values of type T.
Draw
One value picked at random from a distribution. If a poll's vote share is a normal distribution with mean 52 and standard deviation 1.5, one draw might be 51.2 and another 53.9. Most draws land near 52. A histogram of many draws looks like the distribution's curve.
Sample
A set of draws, e.g., 100 draws from the vote share distribution.
Draw key
A column that numbers the draws (draw 1, draw 2, ...). A chart can use it like any other by key, e.g., to spread draws into small multiples, animate over them or overlay them.
Independent cells
Each uncertain value in a table is drawn on its own. Draw 1 for Boston has no connection to draw 1 for Chicago.
Shared draws
Draw 1 is one complete version of the whole table, with every value picked together, so values can rise and fall together across draws. A line through uncertain points needs this, because each line must be a whole path from one draw.
Lift (uncertainty as noise)
Make the ordinary chart once per draw and show all the results together. Where the results agree the picture is crisp, and where they disagree it is blurry. ggdibbler works this way.
Consume (uncertainty as signal)
Draw the distribution itself as the mark, e.g., an interval or a density curve. ggdist works this way.
Slab
A mark whose thickness shows a density curve, as in ggdist's half-eye plot. A violin is two slabs back to back.
HOP (hypothetical outcome plot)
An animation that shows one draw per frame.
Bootstrap (resample)
Make a new dataset of the same size by picking rows from the data at random, with repeats allowed. Each new dataset is one draw. Because a whole dataset is picked at once, bootstrap draws are shared draws.
Coverage
How often an interval contains the true value. A 95% interval should contain it 95 times in 100.
Posterior draws
Draws produced by Bayesian model-fitting software such as Stan. Each draw is one plausible set of values for the model's unknowns.
Gaussian, mixture and joint Gaussian
A Gaussian is the normal (bell curve) distribution. A mixture is a weighted sum of several Gaussians, which can have more than one peak. A joint Gaussian covers several values at once and includes a covariance matrix, which says how each pair of values moves together.
Data depth
A way to order whole draws, e.g., whole curves, from the most typical to the most unusual. The deepest draws are the most central ones.
VSUP (value-suppressing uncertainty palette)
A color scale that merges nearby colors when the uncertainty is high, so readers cannot tell apart values the data cannot tell apart.
104papers triaged
36workshop papers, all three years
7rated High
18rated Medium

How much was read. The seven High papers were read in full or by their method sections, except where noted. Most Medium ratings rest on the abstract only. Three items could not be read and are the best ones to read by hand:

  • UASTL (Krake, Klötzl, Hägele, Weiskopf, TVCG 2024). IEEE refused the download, and there is no arXiv copy. It would likely be rated High.
  • Designing for Disclosure (Mehta, Kindlmann, Kale, VIS 2025). Its taxonomy may give names for the #1091 lint rules.
  • A Priest, a Rabbi, and an Atheist Walk into an Error Bar (Correll and Harrison, alt.VIS 2025). It argues that the field reduces too many kinds of uncertainty to error bars.

The seven papers rated High

PaperVenueWhat is newBears on
VMC: A Grammar for Visualizing Statistical Model Checks
Guo, Kale, Kay, Hullman		VIS 2024 fullA declarative grammar over posterior draws. It has four ways to group draws, which are collapse, individualize, animate and aggregate. Each draw is a full replicate of the dataset, and the number of draws is part of the sampling step.Uses of Dist, Q2, Q10
The Impact of Vertical Scaling on Normal PDF Plots
Fygenson, Padilla		VIS 2024 fullTwo preregistered studies (n = 600 and n = 401). Rescaling each density curve's height leads readers to wrong comparisons. Equal area under every curve gives the most accurate reading.Slab marks, #971, #1091
Evaluating judgements of spatial correlation in visual displays of scalar field distributions
Zhao, Berger		VIS 2025 fullAnimated draws were better for locating a region that varies together across draws. Small multiples were better for seeing whether the region is there. The task exists only when cells share draws.Q2, Q9
wGMM-UAPCA
Klötzl, Tastekin, Hägele, Evers, Weiskopf		Workshop 2025Gaussian mixtures pass exactly through a linear map such as PCA. The output is drawn as highest-density contours at chosen probability levels.Q1
A General Approach to Visualizing Uncertainty in Statistical Graphics
Petek, Nabergoj, Štrumbelj		TVCG at VIS 2026The plain chart is rerun on bootstrap resamples of the rows, and the images are averaged. With n draws, the range of the drawn positions is an interval with coverage (n − 1)/(n + 1), so 39 draws give 95%. Every draw must share one axis domain. The paper compares itself to ggdibbler by name.Q2, Q5, Q9, Q10, #635, #1231, #1091

What the papers say about the open questions

The question numbers refer to section 8 of distributions.md.

Q2 Default dependence: independent cells or shared draws

This is the strongest theme across the three years. ggdibbler treats separate cells as independent, and several papers show charts that need draws shared across cells.

  • Zhao and Berger (2025) ask viewers to find a region whose cells move together across draws. If every cell is an independent distribution, that region does not exist in the picture.
  • In VMC (2024), the sample type is <predictors, response, draw_id>[], and each draw is a whole replicate of the dataset.
  • Petek et al. (2026) make draws by resampling rows. Every statistic computed inside one resample belongs to the same joint outcome, so lines, regressions and stacks within a draw are valid.
  • UASTL (2024), UADAPy (2024 and 2026), MAGIC (2026) and Uncertainty-Aware Jacobi Sets (2026) model correlation as a joint Gaussian with a covariance matrix.

The papers suggest four options. They can be combined.

  1. The input shape fixes the dependence. A long table with a declared draw key means shared draws. A column of per-cell distribution objects means independent cells. With this option there is no default to choose.
  2. Shared draws are the base case. Independent cells are a special case of shared draws.
  3. A row bootstrap is a second source of draws. A flow step such as resample({ n }) adds a draw key whose draws are shared by construction. It covers uncertainty of statistics computed from rows, which is the case in #635 (smoother bands) and #1231 (the quantile rows shortcut). It needs no distribution column.
  4. A joint Gaussian is a constructor. A mean column plus a covariance over a set of cells is a joint distribution with no stored draws. Sampling it gives shared draws.

Q1 Representation: what a distribution is stored as

The design note follows ggdibbler and treats samples as the one representation that always works. The papers add these representations:

  • Gaussian mixtures (wGMM-UAPCA, 2025). An affine map takes a mixture to a mixture exactly. A mixture is also what bind over a discrete variable produces, so a monadic Dist already makes them.
  • A joint Gaussian (UASTL, UADAPy). Loess is a linear smoother, ŷ = Ly. For data with covariance Σ, the band is diag(LΣLᵀ) exactly, with no bootstrap. This is directly useful for #635.
  • Weighted samples (Zou et al., 2026). Their study shows that nine forecast lines, weighted by opacity or line width, keep the perceived distribution.
  • Samples of a family's parameters (jk-plots, 2026). One draw is a pair (μ, σ), not one value.
  • Histograms and quantiles, with an entropy check (Sisneros et al., workshop 2024). The authors compare the entropy of each model with the entropy of the ensemble. The same check could be a unit test for "did converting samples to quantiles lose information", which is the #1231 question.

Evers and Weiskopf (2025) show the limit. A Gaussian pushed through a Fourier transform and squared becomes a mixture of non-central chi-squared distributions, which is no longer Gaussian. So the fallback to draws is still needed for nonlinear steps. Their suggestion is that marks that draw the distribution itself should read it through quantile, cdf and density, so that the mark does not depend on the representation.

Two kinds of uncertainty in the papers do not fit Dist<T>:

  • Tominski et al. (CG&A, presented 2024) name a level between certain and quantified, where a value is known to be uncertain but by an unknown amount. Real data often has this, e.g., a "provisional" flag. There is no spread to draw, so it would be a separate small type or a flag on the cell.
  • Fygenson, Padilla and Matzen (2026) study a qualitative expert confidence (low, medium, high) shown beside an interval. It is an ordinal field next to the Dist and should not be folded into it.

§5 Ways a chart uses a distribution

The design note has three uses, which are lift (uncertainty as noise), consume (uncertainty as signal) and a summary that returns a plain value. The papers add two more.

  • Aggregate per draw (VMC). The chart folds the rows inside each draw and then shows the distribution of the folded values, e.g., a histogram of the mean of each replicated dataset. In Dist terms it is map of a row fold over a whole-table draw, followed by consume. It needs shared draws, because folding rows within draw i means nothing if cells are independent.
  • Nested distributions (jk-plots). The chart lifts over the outer draws and consumes each inner distribution. It is a good worked example for the monad section of the note.

VMC also lists four comparison layouts between model draws and observed data. They are superposition, juxtaposition, nested juxtaposition and explicit encoding (residuals and Q-Q plots). GoFish can already express all four with layer, spread and derive, so they are a good story set for #980 rather than new primitives.

Q9 Where draws go: the transparency direction and its rivals

  • Transparency and subdivision are equally valid. Mason et al. (2026) found no difference between overlaid draws and subdivided regions, as long as draws have equal weight. They also say transparency is the only valid placement when several values are lifted at once. On this evidence, the subdivide operator can wait.
  • Opacity 1/n is not enough. Petek et al. (2026) found that with plain averaging, small marks over a wide range fade as n grows, so more draws look like less uncertainty. They apply a monotone transform to each pixel's frequency. Without such a transform, a convergence test could pass because marks fade, not because the chart is right.
  • Weights. With weighted samples, the overlay opacity or stroke width should follow the weight (Zou et al., 2026).
  • The best direction depends on the task. In Zhao and Berger (2025), animation was better for locating a region and small multiples for detecting it. This argues for keeping the draw key an ordinary by key that spread, animation or the overlay can use.
  • Draw order changes decisions. Matzen et al. (workshop 2024) found that an extreme first forecast leads to worse decisions when viewers stop early. An animation of draws should use a seeded shuffle and not the input order.
  • Radial placement. The jk pie spreads draws on the radial axis in polar coordinates and stacks categories within each ring. This is legal under the existing rules, because it stacks within a draw and spreads across draws. It would make a good test story.
  • Interval-valued time. Potter et al. (2025) found that readers judge uncertain start and end times on a timeline more accurately with transparency than with dashes. That is a rect with fading ends for a Dist<Instant>, which ties to #256.
  • Blur. Fygenson, Padilla and Matzen (2026) found that blur changes the perceived extent of a band, so they drew the blur outside the band's edge. Any blur effect in GoFish should stay outside the bounds that layout and axes read.

There is a tension here. Mason et al. support transparency as the default placement, while Zhao and Berger support no default at all. The two results answer different tasks. Mason et al. test whether a false signal is hidden, and Zhao and Berger test where a true signal is.

Q5, Q10 How many draws, the axis domain and the convergence test

  • A meaning for n. Petek et al. prove that the range of n draws is an interval with coverage (n − 1)/(n + 1) for any distribution. So 9, 19 and 39 draws give 80%, 90% and 95% intervals. GoFish could accept coverage: 0.95 as a second way to give n. Zou et al.'s nine lines would then be an 80% picture, so both spellings would be needed.
  • Where n is set. VMC puts the draw count in the sampling step and not in the chart options. That answers the second half of Q10.
  • The axis domain. Petek et al. require one domain shared by all draws. In GoFish that means domain inference runs over the union of the draws, which the current single-pass inference already does. The axis extent is then the extreme draws, which is the same (n − 1)/(n + 1) interval.
  • Depth for whole curves. ggdibbler rejects quantiles because they need an order and do not extend past one dimension. Data depth orders whole draws from the center outward (Inclusion Depth, TVCG at VIS 2024, and the surface boxplot workshop papers). A depth band is the envelope of the deepest k% of draws. For line ensembles it is an alternative to the pointwise quantile ribbon. The two show different things, because the ribbon shows the spread at each x and the depth band shows the shape of the most typical paths.
  • Convergence needs continuity. ggdibbler's convergence result assumes the chart is a continuous function of the data. Friesecke et al. (2025) show that PCA is not continuous. Some GoFish operators are not continuous either, e.g., sorting by value, bin edges, packing and label placement. For each such operator there are two ways to lift it. It can fix its structure from the point estimate and place every draw with that structure, or it can rebuild its structure for each draw. The two give visibly different charts.
  • Convergence in data size. jk-plots converge to the empirical plot as the number of observations grows. A story pair with n = 10 and n = 1000 would test that.
  • Convergence as an admission rule. Mason et al. allowed into their study only maps whose zero-variance limit is the plain map. This supports the capture-diff convergence test planned in the design note.

Chart types and idioms to support

IdiomSourceNotes for GoFish
Density slabs with one shared scaleFygenson and Padilla 2024Use one shared scale by default, and make per-group normalization an option a user asks for. ggdist's default is also one scale. This applies to violins and ridgelines (#971).
Depth band for line or contour ensemblesInclusion Depth 2024, surface boxplotsA consume mark over Dist<Curve> with shared draws.
Highest-density contours at chosen levelswGMM-UAPCA 2025The 2D version of a multi-level interval. Sorting grid cells by density and summing mass works for parameters and for a KDE of draws.
Weighted spaghetti of about nine linesZou et al. 2026Beat CI bands for perceiving the shape of the distribution. Weight goes to opacity or width.
jk-plots (CDF band, histogram, box, pie)Piccolotto et al. 2026Three summaries of many reference models, which are a random subset, bands and a gradient. This might be a strategy family.
Probit-scaled CCDF for ordinal outcomesSarma 2026One line per draw of Φ⁻¹ P(y ≥ k). Dist<Ordinal> has a cdf and a median but no mean. It also tests a nonlinear scale on probabilities.
Timeline rect with fading endsPotter et al. 2025Dist<Instant> consumed by a rect (#256).
Regression line with residual segmentsBraun et al. 2024CI bands did not make visual checks of a fit more accurate, and residual segments reduced bias. That is a connect from each point to its fitted value (#635).
Certain and uncertain elements in one chartPetek et al. 2026Elements that do not depend on the draw stay crisp, e.g., a bar from the full data with an uncertain point on top.
Points rather than intervalsMarkant et al. 2025Mean plus points beat mean plus interval for later recall. The path that shows points should be as short to write as an error bar.
Soft boundary for uncertain set membershipTominski et al. 2024A new requirement for enclose when membership is a Dist<Bool>.
Level-of-detail table of distribution cellsSlingsby and Hyde 2024Point, interval, slab and dots are one ordered set of detail levels on a Dist cell, so they could be one mark with a detail option.

Lint rules for #1091

Each rule below has study evidence behind it and could be checked from types.

  • Density slabs side by side with per-group normalization. Readers misjudge them (Fygenson and Padilla 2024). If slab thickness carries a "density of X" measure, mismatched normalizations disagree as units.
  • A value and its sd() on two separate channels, when the intent is noise. This behaves like no uncertainty at all (Mason et al. 2026).
  • A VSUP palette with a local domain. VSUP needs the global extent of value and uncertainty (Mason et al. 2026).
  • A lifted Dist on a continuous color channel under the overlay. Averaging is ambiguous there, because a certain mid-gray looks like an even mix of light and dark (Petek et al. 2026). Two-color palettes are fine.
  • A stack within each draw. This is legal, but the interior segments are hard to read (Petek et al. 2026). This should be a hint, not an error.
  • A discontinuous operator applied to a distribution-valued field (Friesecke et al. 2025).
  • Hue mapped to an uncertainty field. Readers take hue as danger (Fygenson, Padilla, Matzen 2026). The evidence is weak, so this should be a hint.
  • A summary that hides spread. The Designing for Disclosure taxonomy (Mehta et al. 2025) may give the categories. It has not been read yet.

Grammars and systems to look at

  • VMC (R, 2024). A grammar of sampling, transformation, representation and comparative layout. It is the closest prior grammar to the #980 plan.
  • bootplot (Python, github.com/davidnabergoj/bootplot, 2026). It reruns a user's matplotlib code on row resamples and averages the images. Its output is raster only. In GoFish, the overlay of n SVG layers matches it for opaque marks but not where marks overlap within one draw, which is a case for the canvas question (Q11).
  • UADAPy (Python, github.com/UniStuttgart-VISUS/uadapy, 2024 and 2026). A Distribution class that wraps a scipy.stats object or an array of samples and is multivariate. It also has operators that take and return distributions (PCA, MDS, STL). It is the closest precedent for what a GoFish Python user would pass across the bridge (Q1).
  • Visual Decoding Operators (Long, Chang, Wu, Kale, Kay, 2026). It models reading a PDF or CDF chart as a chain of perceptual operators with known error. Their practical advice is to add a reference line at the quantile the reader is asked about.
  • Counterbalance (2026). A declarative grammar for charts under differential privacy, where the user states the goal and not the noise mechanism. The noise is added on purpose, so it is only loosely related.

Decisions these papers raise for you

  1. Should a row bootstrap (resample) be a second source of draws beside distribution fields, or a way to build a Dist?
  2. Which representations go in the first version? Samples only, or also a joint Gaussian, mixtures and weighted samples?
  3. Is "aggregate per draw" a named use of Dist, or only map followed by consume with no new name?
  4. Should the transparency overlay be the default draw placement, as Mason et al. suggest, or should there be no default, as Zhao and Berger suggest?
  5. Are "uncertain but unquantified" values and qualitative confidence in scope? Both papers point to fields beside the Dist and not inside it.
  6. When a discontinuous operator lifts over draws, should it fix its structure from the point estimate or rebuild it for each draw, and which is the default?

Distribution-valued data in GoFish (#980)

Summary. GoFish should let a table cell hold a distribution, and it should treat a group of rows as the distribution it already is. A chart then shows uncertainty in one of two ways. It draws samples and places them with the operators it has today, or it draws the distribution itself with a few new marks such as slab and interval.

Recommendation. Adopt the synthesis worked out in types.md. It is Approach A (a distribution type on fields) with Approach B (draws as rows only) as its plain-column case. One new channel kind, dist, folds a group's rows into one distribution. The draw key is a fact in the column record, schema: { temp: { draws: "draw" } }. Everything is additive for users.

Draws and samples. A draw is one random value taken from a distribution. A sample is a collection of draws. sample({ n }) takes n draws per row and adds a draw key that says which draw each new row is. With shared draws, draw i is one consistent world across rows: draw 3 of every row comes from the same run of a model. With independent draws, each row is drawn on its own, so the shared index is only a label.

Issue #980 asks for distribution-valued data, with ideas from ggdist and ggdibbler. This page is the review copy of the proposal. The work behind it is in this folder.

  • signal.md, noise.md, papers-a.md and papers-b.md hold one section per example, each with its render, today's GoFish code, and the proposed code.
  • types.md and types-sketch.ts give the types and schema changes. The sketch typechecks against the real descriptor types.
  • The prototype stories are in packages/gofish-graphics/stories/proposals/uncertainty/.
  • The design note is apps/docs/docs/internals/design/distributions.md. Its literature review is not repeated here.

Terminology

  • Distribution. A description of the values something could take and how likely each is. A normal with a mean and a standard deviation is one. Dist<T> is a distribution over values of type T, such as a price, a time or a category.
  • Draw. One value picked at random from a distribution. A sample is a set of draws.
  • Draw key. The column that says which draw a row belongs to, usually draw.
  • Empirical distribution. The distribution of a sample, where each draw counts equally (or by its weight). A group of rows is the empirical distribution of its values. .mean() on a group of rows is the mean of that distribution.
  • Shared (joint) draws and independent draws. Draws are shared when draw 3 of one value and draw 3 of another come from one run of a model. Posterior draws and bootstrap resamples are shared. Draws are independent when each value is sampled on its own, as sample does for a dist.normal cell. A line or a whole curve inside one draw is a possible path for shared draws. For independent ones it shows a joint the data lacks.
  • Fold. Combining a group's rows into one value. A size channel folds by summing, a position channel by averaging. The new dist channel folds rows into one distribution, the mixture of the rows' distributions.
  • Lift, or uncertainty as noise. Turning each distribution into draws and drawing each draw as an ordinary mark. The spread of the marks shows the uncertainty. ggdibbler works this way.
  • Consume, or uncertainty as signal. A mark that reads the whole distribution and draws its shape or its summary, such as a density curve or an interval. ggdist works this way.
  • Summary. One value computed from a distribution, such as its mean, median or a quantile.
  • Quantile. The value below which a given share of the distribution lies. The 0.5 quantile is the median.
  • Interval and level. An interval at level 0.95 runs from the 0.025 quantile to the 0.975 quantile, so it holds 95% of the distribution.
  • Coverage. The share of a distribution that the range of n draws covers on average: (n − 1) / (n + 1). So 39 draws cover 95%.
  • Band. An interval drawn along an axis, such as a forecast fan. A pointwise band takes the quantiles at each x on its own. A depth band keeps the most central whole curves and draws their envelope.
  • Overlay, or the z direction. Drawing the draws in the same place at low opacity, so they blend. It is a technique, not an operator. group({ by: "draw" }) splits the rows by the draw key and places nothing, so the draws land on top of each other, and the mark sets the low opacity.
  • HOP (hypothetical outcome plot). An animation that shows one draw per frame.
  • Kernel density estimate. A smooth density curve made from a sample. Its bandwidth, called smoothing in GoFish, sets how smooth it is.

Precedent

The design note's section 3 surveys these in full.

System What you write What it decides
ggdist (R) stat_halfeye(aes(xdist = dist_normal(m, s))), or aes(x = value) over sample rows One stat family draws a slab and an interval. It reads distribution objects and sample rows alike. Its scales transform the distribution.
ggdibbler (R) aes(y = dist_normal(m, se)) with a nested position such as stack_dodge Samples only, 10 draws by default, independent cells. Draws get their own position inside the plot's (x, y, z or time). Stacking draws is wrong.
distributional (R) dist_normal(mu, sigma) in a data frame column One distribution object per cell, independent. mean, quantile, hilo.
posterior rvar (R) rvar(draws_array) in a column The first array dimension indexes draws, so arithmetic matches draw i to draw i and keeps correlation.
Vega-Lite { "mark": "errorband", "encoding": { "y": { "field": "v" } } } with extent: "ci" No distribution type. It computes an extent from rows or takes precomputed bounds.
Observable Plot Plot.lineY(draws, { x, y, z: "draw", strokeOpacity: 0.1 }) No distribution type. Draws are rows, grouped by z, with opacity set by hand.
Uncertain<T> (C#) if (speed > 4) on an Uncertain<double> Values build a lazy network, so a value used twice is one random variable. A conditional becomes a hypothesis test.
PPLs (Stan, PyMC with ArviZ, TFP) posterior draws with chain and draw dimensions; TFP Normal(loc, scale).sample(n) Output is shared draws. TFP splits independent cells (batch shape) from one joint draw (event shape).

Two things recur. A distribution lives in a cell, and the system needs a way to say whether two cells share draws. GoFish follows both. It takes ggdist's marks, ggdibbler's positions for draws, and rvar's shared draws.

The approaches

Approach A, a distribution type on fields

A field expression builds a distribution per row from parameter columns, such as dist.normal({ mean: "mean", sd: "se" }). A signal mark reads it in a dist channel. For noise, sample({ n }) turns each row into n rows with a draw key, and a Dist read on a drawn row reads that draw. The draws are then placed with spread, time.sequence or group. Summaries are methods on the expression, such as d.median(), d.quantile(0.9) and d.interval(0.95).

Half-eye. A density slab per group with a point interval on its baseline.

const value = dist.normal({ mean: "mean", sd: "sd" });

chart(halfEyeGroups, { w: 520, axes: { x: { title: "value" }, y: true } })
  .flow(spread({ by: "group", dir: "y", anchor: "baseline", spacing: 64 }))
  .mark(
    layer({ h: 60 }, [
      slab({ x: value, fill: SLAB }),
      interval({ x: value, levels: [0.66, 0.95], fill: INK }),
    ])
  );

value = dist.normal(mean="mean", sd="sd")

(
    chart(half_eye_groups, w=520, axes={"x": {"title": "value"}, "y": True})
    .flow(spread(by="group", dir="y", anchor="baseline", spacing=64))
    .mark(
        layer(
            [
                slab(x=value, fill=SLAB),
                interval(x=value, levels=[0.66, 0.95], fill=INK),
            ],
            h=60,
        )
    )
)

Uncertain bar chart, dodged. Twenty draws per category, side by side.

const value = dist.normal({ mean: "mean", sd: "se" });
chart(estimates)
  .flow(
    spread({ by: "category", dir: "x" }),
    sample({ n: 20 }),
    spread({ by: "draw", dir: "x", spacing: 0 })
  )
  .mark(rect({ h: value }));

value = dist.normal(mean="mean", sd="se")
chart(estimates).flow(
    spread(by="category", dir="x"), sample(n=20), spread(by="draw", dir="x", spacing=0)
).mark(rect(h=value))

jk-CDF. Fifty posterior draws of a normal model, each drawn as its CDF. The parameter columns are posterior draws, so the normal is a Dist<Dist<number>>. sample lifts the outer level and cdf consumes the inner one exactly.

// posterior: [{ draw, mu, sigma }], one row per posterior draw
const model = dist.normal({ mean: "mu", sd: "sigma" }); // Dist<Dist<number>>

chart(posterior, {
  schema: { mu: { draws: "draw" }, sigma: { draws: "draw" } },
})
  .flow(sample({ n: 50 }), group({ by: "draw" }))
  // new: cdf mark; new: opacity reads a field
  .mark(cdf({ x: model, stroke: "#3182bd", opacity: field("draw").share() }))
  .layer(
    // the empirical CDF of the observed rows
    chart(observations).mark(cdf({ x: "value", stroke: "#111" }))
  );

# posterior: one row per posterior draw, columns draw, mu, sigma
model = dist.normal(mean="mu", sd="sigma")  # Dist[Dist[float]]

chart(posterior, schema={"mu": {"draws": "draw"}, "sigma": {"draws": "draw"}}).flow(
    sample(n=50), group(by="draw")
).mark(
    # new: cdf mark; new: opacity reads a field
    cdf(x=model, stroke="#3182bd", opacity=field("draw").share())
).layer(
    chart(observations).mark(cdf(x="value", stroke="#111"))
)

Approach B, draws as rows only

There is no distribution type. Data is always a long table of draws. A signal mark is a statistic over the rows of each group, as ggdist reads sample rows. Noise is grouping by the draw column. B reads well whenever the data is already a sample.

Raincloud. Penguin body mass per species. The same column feeds the slab, the interval and the raw points.

chart(penguins, { w: 560, axes: { x: { title: "Body mass (g)" }, y: true } })
  .flow(spread({ by: "Species", dir: "y", spacing: 20 }))
  .mark(
    layer([
      slab({ x: "Body Mass (g)", fill: "Species", opacity: 0.7 }),
      interval({ x: "Body Mass (g)", levels: [0.66, 0.95], fill: INK }),
      chart()
        .flow(
          scatter({ x: "Body Mass (g)", overlap: Overlap.jitter() }).translate({
            y: -16,
          })
        )
        .mark(circle({ r: 1.5, fill: "Species", opacity: 0.6 })),
    ])
  );

(
    chart(penguins, w=560, axes={"x": {"title": "Body mass (g)"}, "y": True})
    .flow(spread(by="Species", dir="y", spacing=20))
    .mark(
        layer(
            [
                slab(x="Body Mass (g)", fill="Species", opacity=0.7),
                interval(x="Body Mass (g)", levels=[0.66, 0.95], fill=INK),
                chart()
                .flow(
                    scatter(x="Body Mass (g)", overlap=Overlap.jitter()).translate(
                        y=-16
                    )
                )
                .mark(circle(r=1.5, fill="Species", opacity=0.6)),
            ]
        )
    )
)

Ridgeline of croissants. Each month's daily highs, cut into five slices of equal probability.

chart(seattleWeather, {
  w: 520,
  axes: { x: { title: "Daily high (°C)" }, y: false },
})
  .flow(
    spread({
      by: field("month").sort(months),
      dir: "y",
      anchor: "baseline",
      spacing: 36,
    })
  )

    layer({ h: 34 }, [
      chart().mark(slab({ x: "temp_max", fill: "none" }).name("density")),

        .flow(
          partition({ by: field("temp_max").bin(Bin.quantiles(5)), dir: "x" })

    )
    .mark(
        layer(
            [chart().mark(slab(x="temp_max", fill="none").name("density"))], h=34
        ).relate(
            lambda density: [
                chart()
                .flow(partition(by=field("temp_max").bin(Bin.quantiles(5)), dir="x"))

                        [

                                fill=FILL,
                                stroke=STROKE,

Depth band. Fifty whole curves. The band is the envelope of the deepest half.

const draws = { schema: { y: { draws: "curve" } } };

// (a) pointwise quantile ribbons and the pointwise median
chart(curveDraws, draws)
  .flow(scatter({ by: "x", x: "x" }))
  .mark(
    interval({ y: "y", along: "x", levels: [0.5, 0.9], band: Band.pointwise() })
  );

// (b) depth band: the envelope of the deepest 50% of whole curves, the
// deepest curve as the median, and the outlier curves
chart(curveDraws, draws)
  .flow(scatter({ by: "x", x: "x" }))
  .mark(
    interval({
      y: "y",
      along: "x",
      levels: [0.5],
      band: Band.depth({ outliers: 1.5 }),
    })
  );

schema = {"y": {"draws": "curve"}}

# (a) pointwise quantile ribbons
chart(curve_draws, schema=schema).flow(scatter(by="x", x="x")).mark(
    interval(y="y", along="x", levels=[0.5, 0.9], band=Band.pointwise())
)

# (b) depth band
chart(curve_draws, schema=schema).flow(scatter(by="x", x="x")).mark(
    interval(y="y", along="x", levels=[0.5], band=Band.depth(outliers=1.5))
)

The ridgeline shows where B falls short. The slab smooths the days, but the cuts are quintiles of the days, so the slices do not hold equal shares of the drawn curve. Pure B cannot say "cut the curve", so it keeps today's quantileRows helper. For a known family such as one normal, pure B must first sample it, and the cuts are only as good as the sample. The jk-CDF is worse. B has to turn each inner normal into 500 rows, and the curves become noisy ECDFs over 25,000 rows.

The synthesis, where rows are already a distribution

types.md notices that A and B are one model. A group of rows is the empirical distribution of its values, and today's .mean() and .median() already summarize it. Three rules follow.

  1. One fold. A dist channel folds a group's rows into one Dist, the mixture of the rows' Dists. A plain value is a Dist with all its mass at one point. A plain column in a group gives its empirical Dist, which is B. One row with dist.normal gives that normal, which is A.
  2. One summary. .mean(), .median() and .quantile(p) summarize the folded Dist. On a plain column that is what they do today.
  3. Binning a Dist key truncates it. A cell of d.bin(Bin.quantiles(n)) holds the Dist cut to that cell. For a sample, that is the draws in the cell, which is today's split.

The synthesis changes these spellings from the shared syntax sheet. The group files use the new ones throughout.

  • The draw key moves from a channel expression, rvar("temp", { draw: "draw" }), into the column record, { draws: "draw", weight: "w" }, because which rows are one draw is a fact about the table. Issue #1198 (when a sum over rows is valid, for stocks, flows and per-unit values) argues the same for its entity key. A plain column needs no rvar at all.
  • A smoothed sample is the fold field("temp").kde({ smoothing }), not a dist.kde constructor. smoothing is the word Overlap.noise already uses for a bandwidth.
  • Summaries are methods on the Dist expression, d.quantile(p), not field(d).quantile(p), because field() takes a column name.
  • An interval is a span, so it fills a dims entry, rect({ dims: { x: d.interval(0.95) } }). rect({ x: d.interval(0.95) }) is an error, because x is one position.
  • No overlay step. Draws in one frame use today's group({ by: "draw" }), and the mark sets their opacity. The specs write it as opacity: field("draw").share(), the draw's share of the total weight, so 1/n for n equal draws. Open decision 5 asks whether this stays on the mark or becomes a default.
  • A probit scale belongs on the axis, not on the ccdf mark, because a scale acts on every mark on its axis.
  • B's sample({ value: dist.normal(...), n, as }) is dropped. sample({ n }) with a dist.* channel works in both approaches.
  • Where no group had a spelling, these were chosen. sample does not look for Dist cells. It adds a draw key and a seed, and any Dist read on a drawn row reads that draw. A single-key grid is table({ by, numCols }), not spread({ wrap }), because table already has numCols. A correlated pair is dist.mvnormal({ mean: { x, y }, sd: { x, y }, cor }), read as p.x and p.y. resample takes coverage like sample. summarize takes its columns flat. A record weight with no draws key means weighted support points.

Weighted forecasts. Nine model runs, each line's opacity from its weight. The record says model is the draw key and weight its weight. group splits the rows by model, and line reads the weight as its opacity.

chart(forecasts, { schema: { temp: { draws: "model", weight: "weight" } } })
  .flow(group({ by: "model" }), scatter({ by: "month", x: "month", y: "temp" }))
  // new: opacity reads a field
  .mark(line({ stroke: "#1f4e79", strokeWidth: 2, opacity: "weight" }));

chart(forecasts, schema={"temp": {"draws": "model", "weight": "weight"}}).flow(
    group(by="model"),
    scatter(by="month", x="month", y="temp"),
).mark(
    # new: opacity reads a field
    line(stroke="#1f4e79", stroke_width=2, opacity="weight")
)

VMC aggregate per draw. A histogram of each replicate's mean. Grouping by the draw key and summarizing gives one row per draw.

chart(replicates, { schema: { y: { draws: "draw" } } })
  .flow(
    group({ by: "draw" }),
    summarize({ mean: field("y").mean() }), // new: one row per group
    partition({ by: field("mean").bin(Bin.step(0.05)), dir: "x" })
  )
  .mark(rect({ h: field("mean").count(), fill: "#9ecae1", inset: 0.5 }))
  // new: rule mark
  .layer(
    chart(observed).mark(rule({ x: field("y").mean(), stroke: "#08306b" }))
  );

chart(replicates, schema={"y": {"draws": "draw"}}).flow(
    group(by="draw"),
    summarize(mean=field("y").mean()),  # new: one row per group
    partition(by=field("mean").bin(Bin.step(0.05)), dir="x"),
).mark(rect(h=field("mean").count(), fill="#9ecae1", inset=0.5)).layer(
    chart(observed).mark(rule(x=field("y").mean(), stroke="#08306b"))
)

Probit CCDF. Posterior draws of an ordinal model. By the one-fold rule, the rows of a (condition, draw) group with k weighted by p already fold to the categorical that dist.categorical({ level: "k", p: "p" }) builds. So B's form (below the A form in papers-a.md) is the same distribution, not a second mechanism.

// epred: [{ condition, draw, k, p }], p = P(rating = k) in that draw
const rating = dist.categorical({ level: "k", p: "p" }); // Dist<Dist<Ordinal>>

chart(epred, {
  schema: { p: { draws: "draw" } },
  color: Color.palette({ Control: "#7f7f7f", Treatment: "#d95f02" }),
  axes: { y: { scale: Scale.probit() } }, // new: a scale family, on the axis
})
  .flow(sample({ n: 40 }), group({ by: "condition" }), group({ by: "draw" }))
  // new: ccdf mark; new: rule mark; new: opacity reads a field
  .mark(
    ccdf({ x: rating, stroke: "condition", opacity: field("draw").share() })
  )
  .layer(rule({ y: 0, strokeDasharray: "4 3" }));

# epred: columns condition, draw, k, p with p = P(rating = k) in that draw
rating = dist.categorical(level="k", p="p")  # Dist[Dist[Ordinal]]

chart(
    epred,
    schema={"p": {"draws": "draw"}},
    color=Color.palette({"Control": "#7f7f7f", "Treatment": "#d95f02"}),
    axes={"y": {"scale": Scale.probit()}},  # new: a scale family, on the axis
).flow(sample(n=40), group(by="condition"), group(by="draw")).mark(
    ccdf(x=rating, stroke="condition", opacity=field("draw").share())
).layer(
    rule(y=0, stroke_dasharray="4 3")
)

Trade-offs

A B Synthesis
Known families (one normal per row) exact must sample first exact
Data already a sample needs a declaration reads directly reads directly
Cut a smoothed curve at its quantiles yes no, keeps quantileRows yes, with .kde()
Nested distributions (posterior of a model) exact 500 rows per draw exact
Stack-by-draw and other errors from the type only if the draw key is declared from the record
New concepts type, constructors, rvar, marks, steps marks, steps type, constructors, marks, steps, two record keys

Overlap check

Each new piece either desugars exactly into existing operators or adds a capability that none has. These are the cases.

  • Drawing draws in one frame: group. An earlier draft added an overlay step for this. It is not needed. group({ by: "draw" }) splits the rows by the draw key and places nothing (src/ast/graphicalOperators/group.ts), so every draw lands in the same frame. group builds a Frame, the same node that time.sequence builds for its one frame (src/ast/marks/time.ts). layer also places nothing, over a list of children instead of a split. The earlier workaround, a layer of one chart per draw, failed inside a table because it is not a flow step. group is a flow step, and it works inside a table cell and a spread slot today. What is left is the opacity, which is open decision 5.
  • interval against ribbon, rect and scatter's yMin. The point form is exactly a layer of one rect({ dims: { x: d.interval(p) } }) per level and a circle at d.median(). The along form is a ribbon per level and a line through the medians. d.interval(p) returns an AxisInterval, the one span form the IR already has, so it needs no IR change. interval is shorthand.
  • slab against ribbon. A slab is a ribbon over a density grid that the mark computes. Exact.
  • dots against scatter and stack. A quantile dotplot is sample({ n: 20, at: "quantiles" }) then a scatter by bin and a stack of circles. Exact, except for Wilkinson's dot binning, which is new layout work in any form.
  • sample and resample against derive. A derive can make the same rows, but then the draw key, its seed and whether it is joint are unknown to the checker. The two steps are a derive plus a column type (HasDraws).
  • summarize against group and an aggregate channel. An aggregate channel such as rect({ h: field("y").mean() }) folds a group, but its result cannot feed a later partition. summarize collapses each group to one row of columns. It is general, not specific to uncertainty, and could be its own issue.
  • gradient. No exact desugaring. Today it is many thin cells under a color scale. It needs a gradient paint along a mark, which would also serve legends and areas.
  • cdf and ccdf. A CDF is a curve over a grid of x values, not one value per row, so it is not a line over rows. These are new marks.
  • Band.depth(). After the depth ranking, the rest is the pointwise band on a subset of whole curves. That partial overlap lives inside one strategy family, so the band rule is one option, not two marks.
  • quantileRows. Fully subsumed. Once a cell can hold a Dist, Bin.quantiles cuts it directly, and the helper is deleted. Issue #1231 (replace quantileRows with first-class distributions) asks for exactly this.
  • dist.categorical across rows. Subsumed by a weighted fold, as the probit example shows. It may not need to exist.

Examples gallery

Each example has its today code, notes and Python in the group file named under its heading. The code shown is the reconciled proposed spelling.

Croissant

Croissant chart

A density cut at its quantiles into slices of equal probability, each with a dot at its median (Fygenson, Bertini and Padilla, EuroVis 2026). PR #1229 merged it with the quantileRows helper. See signal.md.

const x = dist.normal({ mean: "mean", sd: "sd" });
const params = [{ mean: 0, sd: 1 }];

layer([chart(params).mark(slab({ x, fill: "none" }).name("density"))]).relate(
  ({ density }) => [
    chart(params)
      .flow(partition({ by: x.bin(Bin.quantiles(10)), dir: "x" }))
      .mark(
        layer([
          intersect({ fill: FILL, stroke: STROKE, strokeWidth: 2 }, [
            density,
            region({ inset: 3 }),
          ]),
          circle({ cx: x.median(), cy: 6, r: 3, fill: STROKE }),
        ])
      ),
  ]
);

Ridgeline of croissants

One croissant per month of Seattle's daily highs, each cut at its own quintiles. See signal.md.

const temp = field("temp_max").kde(); // one smooth Dist per group, from its rows

chart(seattleWeather, {
  w: 520,
  axes: { x: { title: "Daily high (°C)" }, y: false },
})
  .flow(
    spread({
      by: field("month").sort(months),
      dir: "y",
      anchor: "baseline",
      spacing: 36,
    })
  )
  .mark(
    layer({ h: 34 }, [
      chart().mark(slab({ x: temp, fill: "none" }).name("density")),
    ]).relate(({ density }) => [
      chart()
        .flow(partition({ by: temp.bin(Bin.quantiles(5)), dir: "x" }))
        .mark(
          layer([
            intersect({ fill: FILL, stroke: STROKE, strokeWidth: 1.5 }, [
              density,
              region({ inset: 2 }),
            ]),
            circle({ cx: temp.median(), cy: 5, r: 2.25, fill: STROKE }),
          ])
        ),
    ])
  );

Quantile dotplot

Twenty quantiles of a bus's arrival time, so each dot is a 5% chance (Kay et al., CHI 2016). See signal.md.

const minutes = dist.lognormal({ meanlog: "meanlog", sdlog: "sdlog" });

chart([{ meanlog: Math.log(11), sdlog: 0.45 }], {
  w: 480,
  axes: { x: { title: "minutes until the bus arrives" }, y: false },
}).mark(dots({ x: minutes, n: 20, fill: SLAB, stroke: INK }));

Fan chart

Nested forecast bands that widen with the horizon, as in the Bank of England fan chart and ggdist's stat_lineribbon. See signal.md.

const value = dist.normal({ mean: "median", sd: "sd" });

layer([
  chart(forecast)
    .flow(scatter({ by: "t", x: "t" }))
    .mark(
      interval({
        y: value,
        along: "t",
        levels: [0.5, 0.8, 0.95],
        fill: "#3a6ea5",

    .flow(scatter({ by: "t", x: "t", y: "value" }))
