  w: 520,
  h: 300,
  axes: { x: { title: "time step" }, y: { title: "value" } },
});

ggdibbler (uncertainty as noise)

Uncertain bar chart, dodged

Twenty draws per estimate as thin bars side by side (Mason et al. 2026). See noise.md.

const value = dist.normal({ mean: "mean", sd: "se" });
chart(estimates)
  .flow(
    spread({ by: "category", dir: "x" }),
    sample({ n: 20 }),
    spread({ by: "draw", dir: "x", spacing: 0 })
  )
  .mark(rect({ h: value }));

Uncertain stacked bar, dodged

Each draw is a full stack by product, and the draws are dodged within each region, ggdibbler's stack_dodge. Stacking by draw instead is an error. See noise.md.

const sales = dist.normal({ mean: "mean", sd: "se" });
chart(estimates)
  .flow(
    spread({ by: "region", dir: "x" }),
    sample({ n: 20 }),
    spread({ by: "draw", dir: "x", spacing: 0 }),
    stack({ by: "product", dir: "y" })
  )
  .mark(rect({ h: sales, fill: "product" }));

Uncertain stacked bar, overlaid

All draws in one slot at low opacity, so product boundaries blur where they are uncertain. group({ by: "draw" }) puts the draws in one slot. See noise.md.

chart(estimates)
  .flow(
    spread({ by: "region", dir: "x" }),
    sample({ n: 20 }),
    group({ by: "draw" }),
    stack({ by: "product", dir: "y" })
  )
  .mark(rect({ h: sales, fill: "product", opacity: field("draw").share() }));

Uncertain scatter

Thirty draws of each point's x and y, drawn as a cloud. A correlated (x, y) would need dist.mvnormal. See noise.md.

const x = dist.normal({ mean: "x", sd: "xSe" });
const y = dist.normal({ mean: "y", sd: "ySe" });
chart(points)
  .flow(sample({ n: 30 }), scatter({ x, y }))
  .mark(circle({ r: 3, fill: "group", opacity: 0.3 }));

HOPs

One frame per draw. The strip shows six frames as small multiples, and the second image is one frame of the real animation. See noise.md.

const value = dist.normal({ mean: "mean", sd: "se" });
chart(estimates)
  .flow(
    sample({ n: 50 }),
    time.sequence({ by: "draw" }),
    spread({ by: "category", dir: "x" })
  )
  .mark(rect({ h: value }));

Uncertain map

A grid of regions shown three ways: the means, a pixel map of draws, and the draws overlaid ("Colour Blinded by the Noise", Mason et al.). The images are the means, the pixel map and the overlay, in that order, and the code holds all three. See noise.md.

const value = dist.normal({ mean: "mean", sd: "sd" });
const blues = Color.gradient(["#f7fbff", "#6baed6", "#08306b"]);

// (a) means
chart(tiles, { color: blues })
  .flow(table({ by: { x: "col", y: "row" }, spacing: 2 }))
  .mark(rect({ fill: value.mean() }));

// (b) pixel
chart(tiles, { color: blues })
  .flow(
    table({ by: { x: "col", y: "row" }, spacing: 2 }),
    sample({ n: 16 }),
    table({ by: "draw", numCols: 4, spacing: 0 }) // new: single-key wrap in flow form
  )
  .mark(rect({ fill: value }));

// (c) transparency
chart(tiles, { color: blues })
  .flow(
    table({ by: { x: "col", y: "row" }, spacing: 2 }),
    sample({ n: 16 }),
    group({ by: "draw" })
  )
  .mark(rect({ fill: value, opacity: field("draw").share() }));

Papers

Bootstrap regression spaghetti

Thirty-nine regression lines, one per bootstrap resample, over the observed points (Petek, Nabergoj and Štrumbelj, 2025). See papers-a.md.

chart(points)
  .flow(scatter({ by: "id", x: "x", y: "y" }))
  .mark(circle({ r: 3, fill: "#222" }))
  .layer(
    chart(points)
      .flow(
        resample({ n: 39 }), // or resample({ coverage: 0.95 })
        group({ by: "draw" }),
        derive(fitLine), // a user function; runs once per draw
        scatter({ by: "x", x: "x", y: "y" })
      )
      .mark(line({ stroke: "#d62728", opacity: field("draw").share() }))
  );

Weighted forecast lines

Nine forecast lines, each line's opacity from its model weight (Zou et al., 2026). See papers-a.md.

chart(forecasts, { schema: { temp: { draws: "model", weight: "weight" } } })
  .flow(group({ by: "model" }), scatter({ by: "month", x: "month", y: "temp" }))
  // new: opacity reads a field
  .mark(line({ stroke: "#1f4e79", strokeWidth: 2, opacity: "weight" }));

VMC aggregate per draw

A histogram of each replicated dataset's mean, with a rule at the observed mean (Guo, Kale, Kay and Hullman, VMC). See papers-a.md.

chart(replicates, { schema: { y: { draws: "draw" } } })
  .flow(
    group({ by: "draw" }),
    summarize({ mean: field("y").mean() }), // new: one row per group
    partition({ by: field("mean").bin(Bin.step(0.05)), dir: "x" })
  )
  .mark(rect({ h: field("mean").count(), fill: "#9ecae1", inset: 0.5 }))
  // new: rule mark
  .layer(
    chart(observed).mark(rule({ x: field("y").mean(), stroke: "#08306b" }))
  );

jk-CDF

Fifty posterior normal CDFs over the empirical CDF (Piccolotto et al., VIS 2026). See papers-a.md.

// posterior: [{ draw, mu, sigma }], one row per posterior draw
const model = dist.normal({ mean: "mu", sd: "sigma" }); // Dist<Dist<number>>

chart(posterior, {
  schema: { mu: { draws: "draw" }, sigma: { draws: "draw" } },
})
  .flow(sample({ n: 50 }), group({ by: "draw" }))
  // new: cdf mark; new: opacity reads a field
  .mark(cdf({ x: model, stroke: "#3182bd", opacity: field("draw").share() }))
  .layer(
    // the empirical CDF of the observed rows
    chart(observations).mark(cdf({ x: "value", stroke: "#111" }))
  );

jk pie

One ring per posterior draw of three category shares, from a Dirichlet. See papers-a.md.

// counts: [{ category, count }], the observed counts
// new: dist.dirichlet, one joint distribution over the rows
const probs = dist.dirichlet({ alpha: field("count").plus(1) });

chart(counts, { coord: Coord.polar(), axes: false })
  .flow(
    sample({ n: 20 }),
    spread({ by: "draw", dir: "y", spacing: 0 }),
    stack({ by: "category", dir: "x" })
  )
  .mark(rect({ w: probs, h: 6, fill: "category" }));

Probit CCDF

One CCDF line per posterior draw and condition of an ordinal model, on a probit axis (Sarma, 2026). The render shows z values, because GoFish has no probit scale. See papers-a.md.

// epred: [{ condition, draw, k, p }], p = P(rating = k) in that draw
const rating = dist.categorical({ level: "k", p: "p" }); // Dist<Dist<Ordinal>>

chart(epred, {
  schema: { p: { draws: "draw" } },
  color: Color.palette({ Control: "#7f7f7f", Treatment: "#d95f02" }),
  axes: { y: { scale: Scale.probit() } }, // new: a scale family, on the axis
})
  .flow(sample({ n: 40 }), group({ by: "condition" }), group({ by: "draw" }))
  // new: ccdf mark; new: rule mark; new: opacity reads a field
  .mark(
    ccdf({ x: rating, stroke: "condition", opacity: field("draw").share() })
  )
  .layer(rule({ y: 0, strokeDasharray: "4 3" }));

Depth band and pointwise ribbon

Fifty whole curves summarized pointwise and by band depth, the functional boxplot (Sun and Genton, 2011). See papers-b.md.

const draws = { schema: { y: { draws: "curve" } } };

// (a) pointwise quantile ribbons and the pointwise median
chart(curveDraws, draws)
  .flow(scatter({ by: "x", x: "x" }))
  .mark(
    interval({ y: "y", along: "x", levels: [0.5, 0.9], band: Band.pointwise() })
  );

// (b) depth band: the envelope of the deepest 50% of whole curves, the
// deepest curve as the median, and the outlier curves
chart(curveDraws, draws)
  .flow(scatter({ by: "x", x: "x" }))
  .mark(
    interval({
      y: "y",
      along: "x",
      levels: [0.5],
      band: Band.depth({ outliers: 1.5 }),
    })
  );

Correlated field draws

Ten draws of a scalar field with a region that rises and falls together, as small multiples (Zhao and Berger, VIS 2025). See papers-b.md.

const options = {
  schema: { value: { draws: "draw" } },
  color: Color.gradient("viridis"),
};

// small multiples: one panel per draw, five to a row
// new: single-key table with numCols in flow form
chart(fieldDraws, options)
  .flow(
    table({ by: "draw", numCols: 5, spacing: 12 }),
    table({ by: { x: "j", y: "i" } })
  )
  .mark(rect({ fill: "value" }));

// HOP: the same chart with the draw key on time instead of space
chart(fieldDraws, options)
  .flow(time.sequence({ by: "draw" }), table({ by: { x: "j", y: "i" } }))
  .mark(rect({ fill: "value" }));

Density scaling

Three normal densities on one shared scale and rescaled to one peak height (Fygenson and Padilla, VIS 2024). The second form gets a lint warning. See papers-b.md.

// params: [{ curve, mean, sd }], one row per curve
const d = dist.normal({ mean: "mean", sd: "sd" });

// (a) equal area: one density scale for every slab (the default)
chart(params)
  .flow(group({ by: "curve" }))
  .mark(slab({ x: d, fill: "curve", opacity: 0.45, normalize: "all" }));

// (b) equal peak: each slab rescaled to the same height. The lint warns here.
chart(params)
  .flow(group({ by: "curve" }))
  .mark(slab({ x: d, fill: "curve", opacity: 0.45, normalize: "group" }));

Uncertain timeline

Activities with uncertain start and end times, drawn with fading ends and with dashed end ranges (Potter et al., VIS 2025). See papers-b.md.

// activities: [{ activity, start, startSd, end, endSd }]. start and end are
// Dist<Instant>; their sd columns are durations.
const start = dist.normal({ mean: "start", sd: "startSd" });
const end = dist.normal({ mean: "end", sd: "endSd" });

// (a) fade: the opacity at t is P(start <= t <= end)
// new: gradient with two Dist ends
chart(activities)
  .flow(spread({ by: ROWS, dir: "y" }))
  .mark(gradient({ x: start, x2: end, fill: BAR }));

// (a, noise form) the same fade from 50 overlaid draws
chart(activities)
  .flow(
    spread({ by: ROWS, dir: "y" }),
    sample({ n: 50 }),
    group({ by: "draw" })
  )
  .mark(rect({ x: start, x2: end, fill: BAR, opacity: field("draw").share() }));

// (b) dashed: a solid core, and a dashed box over each end's 95% interval
chart(activities)
  .flow(spread({ by: ROWS, dir: "y" }))
  .mark(
    layer([
      rect({ x: start.quantile(0.975), x2: end.quantile(0.025), fill: BAR }),
      // new: a dash option on rect
      rect({
        dims: { x: start.interval(0.95) },
        fill: "none",
        stroke: BAR,
        strokeDasharray: "3 3",
      }),
      rect({
        dims: { x: end.interval(0.95) },
        fill: "none",
        stroke: BAR,
        strokeDasharray: "3 3",
      }),
    ])
  );

Types and schema

The full page is types.md, and the sketch is types-sketch.ts. The sketch imports the real descriptor DSL, and it typechecks. Its six @ts-expect-error lines are each a real error. It builds on PR #1200 (units as a domain plus traits, with CanAdd, and column records) and PR #1237 (one name resolver).

Layer Change Additive or breaking
Value types Dist<T>, held as pure, family, sample, mixture, truncated or smoothed, and marked independent or joint. Each summary asks for a capability of T: quantile needs an order, mean needs values that average, sd returns the difference unit, prob needs levels. additive
Data and schema The column record gets draws and weight. The column type gets HasDraws { joint, n?, seed? } on the draw key and HasSample { draws, weight? } on the value column. DataIR does not change. additive
Internal HasDomain.sorted() becomes HasDomain.quantile(p), so Bin.quantiles cuts a Dist with no new code, and quantileRows is deleted (#1231). internal only
Field expressions A new DistAccessor beside FieldAccessor and StructAccessor, because a normal reads two columns. New ops quantile, cdf, sd, prob and kde, and share on a draw key. .interval(p) adds no op, because it returns an AxisInterval. additive
Descriptors A dist channel kind. Operators sample, resample, summarize. Marks slab, interval, dots, gradient, cdf, ccdf. opacity becomes a channel on marks, so it can read a field (open decision 5). additive
Strategy families Band.pointwise() and Band.depth({ outliers }). The dist.* constructors get their own table in the same shape. additive
Python A DistAccessor class, the new methods, a dist module, and generated wrappers. sample and resample take a seed, and JS and Python share one seeded generator. additive

Everything is additive for users. .mean() and .median() change meaning only on Dist inputs, which do not exist today.

The checker has five rules, each at a place that already reads column types. The error texts are from the sketch and the design note.

  1. A Dist in a one-value channel with no lift. In inferNumeric (channels.ts). Static. 
    rect: channel "h" expects a number of sales, but it holds Dist<sales>. A distribution
is not one number. Choose one of these:
  - summarize it:  rect({ h: field("sales").mean() })
  - draw it:       add sample({ n: 50 }) to the flow and place the draws
  - show it:       use interval({ y: "sales" }) or slab({ y: "sales" })
  2. A stack by a draw key. In spread's split, beside the HasMidpoint read. Static. 
    stack({ by: "draw", dir: "y" }): "draw" is a sample index. The draws of one value are
a sample, and their sum grows with the number of draws, so the stacked total has no
meaning. stack needs a key whose groups add up to a whole.
Place the draws with spread({ by: "draw", dir: "x" }), with time.sequence({ by: "draw" }),
or stack by a category inside each draw.
  3. A sum over several draws (a size channel, .sum(), treemap). Beside checkSummable (measure.ts). Read from the rows. This is #1198's entity check with the draw key as the repeated key, so they should be one function. 
    rect h sums 20 draws of "draw". A sum of draws grows with their number. Split by
"draw" first, or take .mean().
  4. A connecting mark across draws. Where a relational mark picks its path tier (classifyOperator). Static. A line must sit inside a split by the draw key, such as group({ by: "draw" }), so that each line joins the marks of one draw. This holds for shared and independent draws alike. Over independent cells each line is one draw of the whole table with every cell drawn on its own, so the lines show a joint the data does not give (theory.md Section 1.6). That is a caveat, not an error.
    line({ along: "t" }) joins 30 draws of "value" into one path. Draw 3 in one t has no
relation to draw 7 in the next, so the path runs across draws.
Split by the draw key first, with group({ by: "draw" }), so each line stays in one
draw. Or summarize first with field("value").interval(0.8) and draw a ribbon.
  5. A summary that T cannot support. In evalFieldValues (fieldExpr.ts). Static. 
    field("species").quantile(...): quantile needs an order on the values, but
Dist<Category> (species) has none. Use field("species").prob(level) for the
probability of a category, or draw it with sample().

The typed JS surface catches rules 1, 2, 4 and 5 at compile time in the sketch. The engine runs all five, for Python and for untyped columns.

Gaps in today's GoFish

These came up while drawing the examples. None depends on #980.

  • opacity takes only a number, on every mark. Weighted lines need one layer per weight, and option (a) of open decision 5 needs opacity to read a field. Own issue.
  • No rule mark. A full-span reference line is a two-point line, and a rect with a literal height did not span the axis in data units. Own issue.
  • group takes one key. Condition by draw needs a joined key column. Own issue, small.
  • No color domain option. Color.gradient cannot fix its domain, so three plates of one map cannot share a scale. Own issue.
  • A table cell with many rows draws one rect, with no warning. 768 rows gave 48 rects. Own issue, as a bug.
  • No probit scale, and no scale family. Own issue, a design question.
  • No gradient fill along a mark. Fades are stepped cells under a color scale. Own issue, as a paint feature.
  • rect has no strokeDasharray. Fold into the gradient paint issue or a small fix.
  • No single-key grid in flow form. table has numCols, but its flow form requires by: { x, y }. Own issue, small.
  • Raincloud layout workarounds. The rain needs a pixel translate to sit below the baseline, a child with no position then falls to the bottom of the layer, and a stack combinator did not separate the points from the slab. Own issue, to investigate.
  • capture-one Vite startup timeouts under parallel load. Several renders at once timed out while the server started. Own issue, tooling.
  • Smaller ones. spread's label: false does not hide ticks. A layer of table charts lays them out one after another. The color option on layer(...).render does not reach its charts. The gradient legend prints long decimals. table column labels pile up on the left. These can go in one cleanup issue.

Recommendation

Adopt the synthesis. These are the reasons, against the design principles in api-design.md.

  • It desugars into the core. slab, interval and dots are exact shorthands for marks GoFish has. Draws are placed by spread, group and time.sequence, which all exist today. sample and resample are a derive plus a column type. Only gradient, cdf, ccdf and Band.depth add something new, and each is something no operator can do today.
  • One concept is one operator. There is one fold for a distribution channel, not one for rows and one for cells. .mean() has one meaning. quantileRows goes away. B's plain columns are not a second path, they are the same fold.
  • Meaning lives on the data type. Whether rows are draws, and whether those draws are shared, is in the column record, like units after #1200. The stack-by-draw error and the line-across-draws error come from the type, not from the user remembering.
  • Few new terms. The user learns dist.*, sample, two record keys and the signal marks. rvar and dist.kde are gone. The marks keep ggdist's names.
  • The same shape in JS and Python. Every example above has a Python form with the same structure, and the draws cross as plain rows.

The runner-up is pure B, draws as rows only. It is shorter when the data is already a sample, it needs no new value type, and Python users mostly bring draws from ArviZ. It loses on known families, on nested distributions, and on cutting a smoothed curve, which is the croissant chart that started #1231. The pick would change if the one-fold rule broke on a hard example, such as a chart that mixes independent cells and shared draws in one group, or if parameters could not cross to Python with a shared seeded generator. In that case B with a declared draw key is the safe subset, and A could be added later.

Decisions for the maintainer

  1. Should the draw key live in the column record ({ draws: "draw" }, the pick here) or in a channel expression (rvar, the sheet's spelling)?

  2. Should the namespace be dist.normal, like time, or Dist.normal, like the other capitalized factory namespaces, with Dist.Dist as the type?

  3. When a chart mixes a dist.* cell with a joint draws column, are they independent, or is it an error? (Design note question 2.)

  4. Should a group with several dist.* rows fold to their mixture, or should a signal mark draw one shape per row, as ggdist does?

  5. Where does the opacity of draws in one frame come from? group({ by: "draw" }) places the draws, and it sets no opacity. There are two options.

    • (a) On the mark, as a field expression on the draw key: rect({ opacity: field("draw").share() }). share() is the draw's share of the total weight, w / Σw, so 1/n for n equal draws. This needs opacity to read a field, which it cannot today, and a new share op. A weighted ensemble can read its weight column directly instead, line({ opacity: "weight" }), as the weighted forecasts do.
    • (b) A default. A mark under a sample draw key gets opacity 1/n unless it sets its own. This needs nothing new on the mark, but the opacity then depends on a step elsewhere in the flow.

    The specs here use (a). Two findings in theory.md (Q3) hold for both. A floor, such as the earlier draft's max(0.05, w / Σw), stops the picture from converging as n grows, so the fix for faint marks belongs in the renderer. And the blend mode is open: "over" compositing converges to a tone map of the coverage, and additive blending converges to the expected image.

  6. Should slab and interval over a plain column smooth the sample by default, as ggdist does, or read the raw sample unless the user writes .kde()?

  7. What crosses the bridge from Python: dist.* parameters, draws as rows, or also cells of SciPy distributions as an Arrow struct column? And do JS and Python share one seeded generator? (Question 1.)

  8. Which quantile range sets the axis extent of an unbounded distribution? (Question 5.)

  9. Is the draw-sum check the same function as #1198's entity check, so #1198 lands first? (Question 3.)

  10. For categories, is prob() per level a flow step or a mark, and does a by key or a color channel over Dist<Category> need anything beyond sample? (Question 8.)

  11. Should filter on a Dist be an error, a test with a stated threshold, or a per-draw filter after sample? (Question 7.)

  12. Are summarize (flat columns, or a columns record on the wire) and dist.categorical in scope for #980, or does summarize get its own issue and dist.categorical give way to the weighted fold?

Design note questions 4 (interaction with selection), 10 (visual convergence as a test, and whether n is a chart option) and 11 (node count and a canvas path) stay open but do not block the syntax. Question 6 (row folds versus cell summaries) is settled above by the one-summary rule. Question 9 (the z direction) is settled by today's group, which already draws the draws in one frame. Only its opacity stays open, as decision 5.

Uncertainty as signal

These examples draw a distribution itself, as ggdist does. Each one has a prototype in today's GoFish in packages/gofish-graphics/stories/proposals/uncertainty/Signal.stories.tsx, with its data in signal-data.ts next to it. The croissant charts are the merged stories in stories/forwardsyntax/Croissant.stories.tsx. GoFish has no distribution type yet, so every density grid, quantile and interval in the Today code is computed by hand in the data file.

Three patterns repeat in the Today code, and the proposed marks remove all three.

  • One table holds two or three kinds of rows (kind: "slab", "interval", "point"), and each layer picks its rows with filter. A distribution in one cell would replace the extra rows.
  • The point interval is always the same three marks: a thin rect, a thick rect and a circle, read from five precomputed columns.
  • The slab is a ribbon over a grid of { x, pdf } rows.

Croissant chart

A density curve cut at its quantiles into slices of equal probability, each with a dot at its median (Fygenson, Bertini and Padilla, EuroVis 2026). It was merged in #1229, and issue #1231 asks to replace its quantileRows helper with a real distribution.

Today (the merged Croissant story):

// grid = gridOf((x) => normPdf(x), -4, 4, 401): rows { x, pdf }
layer([
  chart(grid)
    .flow(scatter({ x: "x" }))
    .mark(ribbon({ h: "pdf", fill: "none" }).name("density")),
]).relate(({ density }) => [
  chart(grid)
    .flow(
      derive((rows) => quantileRows(rows, { x: "x", density: "pdf", n: 1000 })),
      partition({ by: field("x").bin(Bin.quantiles(10)), dir: "x" })
    )
    .mark(
      layer([
        intersect({ fill: FILL, stroke: STROKE, strokeWidth: 2 }, [
          density,
          region({ inset: 3 }),
        ]),
        circle({ cx: field("x").median(), cy: 6, r: 3, fill: STROKE }),
      ])
    ),
]);

Precomputed by hand: the density grid { x, pdf } on [-4, 4], and then 1000 rows at the curve's quantiles (quantileRows), so that Bin.quantiles and median() read the curve and not the grid. The grid must be wide enough that the tails do not move the outer quantiles.

Proposed (A)

const x = dist.normal({ mean: "mean", sd: "sd" });
const params = [{ mean: 0, sd: 1 }];

layer([chart(params).mark(slab({ x, fill: "none" }).name("density"))]).relate(
  ({ density }) => [
    chart(params)
      .flow(partition({ by: x.bin(Bin.quantiles(10)), dir: "x" }))
      .mark(
        layer([
          intersect({ fill: FILL, stroke: STROKE, strokeWidth: 2 }, [
            density,
            region({ inset: 3 }),
          ]),
          circle({ cx: x.median(), cy: 6, r: 3, fill: STROKE }),
        ])
      ),
  ]
);

x = dist.normal(mean="mean", sd="sd")
params = [{"mean": 0, "sd": 1}]

layer([chart(params).mark(slab(x=x, fill="none").name("density"))]).relate(
    lambda density: [
        chart(params)
        .flow(partition(by=x.bin(Bin.quantiles(10)), dir="x"))
        .mark(
            layer(
                [
                    intersect(
                        [density, region(inset=3)],
                        fill=FILL,
                        stroke=STROKE,
                        stroke_width=2,
                    ),
                    circle(cx=x.median(), cy=6, r=3, fill=STROKE),
                ]
            )
        )
    ]
)

The data is one row with the parameters. partition with Bin.quantiles(10) on a distribution cuts its probability, not its rows. Each of the ten cells holds the same distribution cut down to one slice, so x.median() in a cell is the slice's median. There is no grid and no derive.

Ridgeline of croissants

The same chart, one per month of Seattle's daily highs, each cut at its own quintiles.

Today (the merged Ridgeline story):

chart(monthlyDensity, {
  w: args.w,
  axes: { x: { title: "Daily high (°C)" }, y: false },
})
  .flow(
    spread({
      by: field("month").sort(months),
      dir: "y",
      anchor: "baseline",
      spacing: 36,
    })
  )
  .mark(
    layer({ h: 34 }, [
      chart()
        .flow(scatter({ x: "temp_max" }))
        .mark(ribbon({ h: "pdf", fill: "none" }).name("density")),
    ]).relate(({ density }) => [
      chart()
        .flow(
          derive((rows) =>
            quantileRows(rows, { x: "temp_max", density: "pdf", n: 1000 })
          ),
          partition({ by: field("temp_max").bin(Bin.quantiles(5)), dir: "x" })
        )
        .mark(
          layer([
            intersect({ fill: FILL, stroke: STROKE, strokeWidth: 1.5 }, [
              density,
              region({ inset: 2 }),
            ]),
            circle({
              cx: field("temp_max").median(),
              cy: 5,
              r: 2.25,
              fill: STROKE,
            }),
          ])
        ),
    ])
  );

Precomputed by hand: a kernel density estimate per month, put on a grid of 161 rows from three bandwidths below the month's lowest high to three above its highest.

Proposed (A)

const temp = field("temp_max").kde(); // one smooth Dist per group, from its rows

chart(seattleWeather, {
  w: 520,
  axes: { x: { title: "Daily high (°C)" }, y: false },
})
  .flow(
    spread({
      by: field("month").sort(months),
      dir: "y",
      anchor: "baseline",
      spacing: 36,
    })
  )
  .mark(
    layer({ h: 34 }, [
      chart().mark(slab({ x: temp, fill: "none" }).name("density")),
    ]).relate(({ density }) => [
      chart()
        .flow(partition({ by: temp.bin(Bin.quantiles(5)), dir: "x" }))
        .mark(
          layer([
            intersect({ fill: FILL, stroke: STROKE, strokeWidth: 1.5 }, [
              density,
              region({ inset: 2 }),
            ]),
            circle({ cx: temp.median(), cy: 5, r: 2.25, fill: STROKE }),
          ])
        ),
    ])
  );

temp = field("temp_max").kde()  # one smooth Dist per group, from its rows

(
    chart(seattle_weather, w=520, axes={"x": {"title": "Daily high (°C)"}, "y": False})
    .flow(
        spread(by=field("month").sort(months), dir="y", anchor="baseline", spacing=36)
    )
    .mark(
        layer([chart().mark(slab(x=temp, fill="none").name("density"))], h=34).relate(
            lambda density: [
                chart()
                .flow(partition(by=temp.bin(Bin.quantiles(5)), dir="x"))
                .mark(
                    layer(
                        [
                            intersect(
                                [density, region(inset=2)],
                                fill=FILL,
                                stroke=STROKE,
                                stroke_width=1.5,
                            ),
                            circle(cx=temp.median(), cy=5, r=2.25, fill=STROKE),
                        ]
                    )
                )
            ]
        )
    )
)

The data is the raw daily table. A plain column in a Dist channel is the group's rows as draws (the one-fold rule in types.md), but its quantiles would be the sample's quantiles, while the slab would draw a smoothed curve. The cuts would then not match the drawn curve, which is the exact problem quantileRows solves today. field("temp_max").kde() folds the group's rows into one smoothed distribution whose density and quantiles are both those of the drawn curve. It is a fold like .mean(), and its bandwidth option is smoothing, the word Overlap.noise already uses (.kde({ smoothing: 1.5 })). This replaces the dist.kde("temp_max") first written here.

Proposed (B)

chart(seattleWeather, {
  w: 520,
  axes: { x: { title: "Daily high (°C)" }, y: false },
})
  .flow(
    spread({
      by: field("month").sort(months),
      dir: "y",
      anchor: "baseline",
      spacing: 36,
    })
  )
  .mark(
    layer({ h: 34 }, [
      chart().mark(slab({ x: "temp_max", fill: "none" }).name("density")),
    ]).relate(({ density }) => [
      chart()
        .flow(
          partition({ by: field("temp_max").bin(Bin.quantiles(5)), dir: "x" })
        )
        .mark(
          layer([
            intersect({ fill: FILL, stroke: STROKE, strokeWidth: 1.5 }, [
              density,
              region({ inset: 2 }),
            ]),
            circle({
              cx: field("temp_max").median(),
              cy: 5,
              r: 2.25,
              fill: STROKE,
            }),
          ])
        ),
    ])
  );

B reads more simply, but it draws a different chart. The slab is a density stat over the days, and the cuts are quintiles of the days. The slices then hold equal shares of days, not equal shares of the drawn curve. In the synthesis this B code is legal and means exactly that, and A differs from it by one .kde(). Pure B, with no Dist type, has no way to say "cut the curve", so it would keep quantileRows. For the single normal, pure B also needs draws: a sample of the normal first, and the cuts are then only as good as the sample.

New pieces needed: dist.normal, slab, a Bin.quantiles that cuts a distribution's probability, and a cell median that reads the cut distribution. The ridgeline also needs .kde(). Overlap check: partition and Bin.quantiles already exist, and the proposed form only changes what they read. Today quantileRows stands in for the distribution by turning it into 1000 quantile rows. That is a full subsumption: once a Dist is a cell, quantileRows can be deleted (#1231), and no second path remains. slab over a dist.* is the same picture as ribbon({ h: "pdf" }) over a grid that the library makes itself.

Half-eye

Per group, a density slab on one side of the baseline, with a point at the median, a thick 66% interval and a thin 95% interval on the baseline. This is ggdist's stat_halfeye.

Today

chart(halfEyeRows, { w: 520, axes: { x: { title: "value" }, y: true } })
  .flow(spread({ by: "group", dir: "y", anchor: "baseline", spacing: 64 }))
  .mark(
    layer({ h: 60 }, [
      chart()
        .flow(
          filter((d) => d.kind === "slab"),
          scatter({ x: "x" })
        )
        .mark(ribbon({ h: "pdf", fill: SLAB })),
      chart()
        .flow(filter((d) => d.kind === "interval"))
        .mark(
          layer([
            rect({ x: "lo95", x2: "hi95", h: 1.5, fill: INK }),
            rect({ x: "lo66", x2: "hi66", h: 4, fill: INK }),
            circle({ cx: "median", r: 4, fill: INK }),
          ])
        ),
    ])
  );

Precomputed by hand: a 121-point density grid per group (kind: "slab" rows), and one kind: "interval" row per group with the median and the 66% and 95% bounds from the normal quantile function. The slabs share one density scale, which is ggdist's normalize = "all". The group labels sit at the middle of each row, not at its baseline, because the y axis labels the row's box.

Proposed (A)

const value = dist.normal({ mean: "mean", sd: "sd" });

chart(halfEyeGroups, { w: 520, axes: { x: { title: "value" }, y: true } })
  .flow(spread({ by: "group", dir: "y", anchor: "baseline", spacing: 64 }))
  .mark(
    layer({ h: 60 }, [
      slab({ x: value, fill: SLAB }),
      interval({ x: value, levels: [0.66, 0.95], fill: INK }),
    ])
  );

value = dist.normal(mean="mean", sd="sd")

(
    chart(half_eye_groups, w=520, axes={"x": {"title": "value"}, "y": True})
    .flow(spread(by="group", dir="y", anchor="baseline", spacing=64))
    .mark(
        layer(
            [
                slab(x=value, fill=SLAB),
                interval(x=value, levels=[0.66, 0.95], fill=INK),
            ],
            h=60,
        )
    )
)

The data is four rows, { group, mean, sd }. Under B the data would be a long table of draws and the code would read the same with x: "value", so B is not shown.

New pieces needed: dist.normal, slab and interval. Overlap check: slab is a ribbon over a density grid that the mark computes, and interval is the three marks of the next example. Both desugar exactly into existing marks once the grid and the quantiles exist. There is no partial overlap with another operator.

Multi-level point interval

A point at the median with a thick 66% interval and a thin 95% interval, one row per group. This is ggdist's stat_pointinterval.

Today

chart(pointIntervalRows, {
  w: 480,
  axes: { x: { title: "value" }, y: true },
})
  .flow(spread({ by: "group", dir: "y", spacing: 16 }))
  .mark(
    layer([
      rect({ x: "lo95", x2: "hi95", h: 1.5, fill: INK }),
      rect({ x: "lo66", x2: "hi66", h: 5, fill: INK }),
      circle({ cx: "median", r: 4, fill: INK }),
    ])
  );

Precomputed by hand: the five columns median, lo66, hi66, lo95 and hi95 per group, from the normal quantile function.

Proposed (A)

const value = dist.normal({ mean: "mean", sd: "sd" });

chart(pointIntervalGroups, { w: 480, axes: { x: { title: "value" }, y: true } })
  .flow(spread({ by: "group", dir: "y", spacing: 16 }))

    .flow(spread(by="group", dir="y", spacing=16))
    .mark(interval(x=value, levels=[0.66, 0.95], fill=INK))
)

Without the interval mark, the summary field ops alone give the Today code with no precomputed columns:

layer([
  rect({ dims: { x: value.interval(0.95) }, h: 1.5, fill: INK }),
  rect({ dims: { x: value.interval(0.66) }, h: 5, fill: INK }),
  circle({ cx: value.median(), r: 4, fill: INK }),
]);

New pieces needed: dist.normal and interval, or only the summary ops .interval(p) and .median(). Overlap check: interval desugars exactly into the layer above, one rect per level with widths stepping down and a circle at the median. It is shorthand, not a new kind of mark. value.interval(0.95) is a two-ended span, so it fills a dims entry, the one span form the IR already has. rect({ x: value.interval(0.95) }) is an error, because x is one position. Two calls, x: value.quantile(0.025), x2: value.quantile(0.975), mean the same as the dims form.

Gradient interval

A bar whose color fades with the density, with the point interval on top. This is ggdist's stat_gradientinterval.

Today

chart(gradientRows, {
  w: 520,
  color: Color.gradient(["#ffffff", "#3a6ea5"]),
  legend: false,
  axes: { x: { title: "value" }, y: true },
})
  .flow(spread({ by: "group", dir: "y", spacing: 14 }))
  .mark(
    layer([
      chart()
        .flow(
          filter((d) => d.kind === "cell"),
          scatter({ xMin: "x0", xMax: "x1" })
        )
        .mark(
          rect({
            cy: 0,
            h: 22,
            fill: "density",
            stroke: "density",
            strokeWidth: 0.6,
          })
        ),
      chart()
        .flow(filter((d) => d.kind === "interval"))
        .mark(
          layer([
            rect({ x: "lo95", x2: "hi95", cy: 0, h: 1.5, fill: INK }),
            rect({ x: "lo66", x2: "hi66", cy: 0, h: 4, fill: INK }),
            circle({ cx: "median", cy: 0, r: 4, fill: INK }),
          ])
        ),
    ])
  );

This render is an approximation. GoFish has no fill gradient along one mark, and opacity takes only a number, not a field. So each bar is 80 thin cells, and each cell's fill comes from a white-to-blue color scale on the density at its center. On a white background this looks like opacity. Faint seams between cells still show, and the stroke on each cell only partly hides them. Precomputed by hand: the 80 cells [x0, x1] per group over mean ± 3.5 sd, the density at each cell divided by the largest density of all groups, and the interval row.

Proposed (A)

const value = dist.normal({ mean: "mean", sd: "sd" });

chart(halfEyeGroups, { w: 520, axes: { x: { title: "value" }, y: true } })
  .flow(spread({ by: "group", dir: "y", spacing: 14 }))
  .mark(
    layer([
      gradient({ x: value, h: 22, fill: "#3a6ea5" }),
      interval({ x: value, levels: [0.66, 0.95], fill: INK }),
    ])
  );

value = dist.normal(mean="mean", sd="sd")

(
    chart(half_eye_groups, w=520, axes={"x": {"title": "value"}, "y": True})
    .flow(spread(by="group", dir="y", spacing=14))
    .mark(
        layer(
            [
                gradient(x=value, h=22, fill="#3a6ea5"),
                interval(x=value, levels=[0.66, 0.95], fill=INK),
            ]
        )
    )
)

New pieces needed: gradient, and under it a real fill gradient in the renderer (an SVG linearGradient with one stop per grid point). Overlap check: this is the one mark here with no exact desugaring. The stepped version is a partition of the support into cells plus a color scale, which is close but leaves seams and costs 80 nodes per bar. A fill gradient along a mark is a general paint feature (it would also serve a heatmap legend or a gradient area). It should be a paint option shared with other marks, and gradient would be a rect that uses it. It should not be a one-off inside this mark.

Quantile dotplot

Twenty quantiles of a bus's arrival time, binned and stacked, so each dot is a 5% chance (Kay, Kola, Hullman and Munson, "When (ish) is my bus?", CHI 2016; ggdist stat_dots with quantiles = 20).

Today

chart(busDots, {
  w: 480,
  h: 160,
  axes: { x: { title: "minutes until the bus arrives" }, y: false },
})
  .flow(
    scatter({ by: "bin", x: "bin", alignment: "end" }),
    stack({ dir: "y", spacing: 1 })
  )
  .mark(circle({ r: 9, fill: SLAB, stroke: INK, strokeWidth: 1 }));

Precomputed by hand: the 20 quantiles of a lognormal with a median of 11 minutes, at probabilities (i + 0.5) / 20, and the 1-minute bin of each. The bins are fixed width, not Wilkinson's dot-density bins, and the dot radius is set by hand to about half a bin. The alignment: "end" is what puts the stacks on the axis. With "start" or the default the stacks hang from the top.

Proposed (A)

const minutes = dist.lognormal({ meanlog: "meanlog", sdlog: "sdlog" });

chart([{ meanlog: Math.log(11), sdlog: 0.45 }], {
  w: 480,
  axes: { x: { title: "minutes until the bus arrives" }, y: false },
}).mark(dots({ x: minutes, n: 20, fill: SLAB, stroke: INK }));

minutes = dist.lognormal(meanlog="meanlog", sdlog="sdlog")

chart(
    [{"meanlog": math.log(11), "sdlog": 0.45}],
    w=480,
    axes={"x": {"title": "minutes until the bus arrives"}, "y": False},
).mark(dots(x=minutes, n=20, fill=SLAB, stroke=INK))

dist.lognormal is one of the sheet's "etc." constructors. I named its parameters meanlog and sdlog, as R and distributional do. Under B the code is the same with x: "minutes" over a table of draws, and dots takes 20 quantiles of the sample.

New pieces needed: dist.lognormal and dots. Overlap check: dots desugars into existing operators once the 20 quantiles are rows: a scatter by bin and a stack of circles, which is the Today code. The step that turns one distribution into its n quantile rows is the one missing piece. It is quantileRows again, and it is close to the sheet's sample({ n }), except that it takes quantiles and not random draws. One spelling would be sample({ n: 20, at: "quantiles" }). The dot binning (Wilkinson's method, with the radius from the bin width) is new layout work in either form.

Raincloud

Per species, a density slab above the baseline, the point interval on the baseline, and the raw birds jittered below it (Allen et al., "Raincloud plots", 2021; in ggdist, stat_halfeye plus stat_dots with side = "left").

Today

chart(raincloudRows, {
  w: 560,
  legend: false,
  axes: { x: { title: "Body mass (g)" }, y: true },
})
  .flow(spread({ by: "species", dir: "y", spacing: 20 }))
  .mark(
    layer([
      chart()
        .flow(
          filter((d) => d.kind === "slab"),
          scatter({ x: "mass" })
        )
        .mark(ribbon({ h: "pdf", fill: "species", opacity: 0.7 })),
      chart()
        .flow(
          filter((d) => d.kind === "interval"),
          scatter({ y: 0 })
        )
        .mark(
          layer([
            rect({ x: "lo95", x2: "hi95", h: 1.5, fill: INK }),
            rect({ x: "lo66", x2: "hi66", h: 4, fill: INK }),
            circle({ cx: "median", r: 4, fill: INK }),
          ])
        ),
      chart()
        .flow(
          filter((d) => d.kind === "point"),
          scatter({ x: "mass", overlap: Overlap.jitter() }).translate({
            y: -16,
          })
        )
        .mark(circle({ r: 1.5, fill: "species", opacity: 0.6 })),
    ])
  );

Precomputed by hand: a kernel density estimate per species on a 121-point grid, and the sample's median and 66% and 95% bounds. The raw birds are copied in as kind: "point" rows. Two parts of the layout are workarounds. The points are moved below the baseline with .translate({ y: -16 }). Once they are, the interval no longer sits on the baseline on its own: a child with no position goes to the bottom of the layer. The scatter({ y: 0 }) pins it back to the baseline. A stack combinator of the points under the slab did not separate them, which I did not track down.

Proposed (A)

chart(penguins, { w: 560, axes: { x: { title: "Body mass (g)" }, y: true } })
  .flow(spread({ by: "Species", dir: "y", spacing: 20 }))
  .mark(
    layer([
      slab({ x: "Body Mass (g)", fill: "Species", opacity: 0.7 }),
      interval({ x: "Body Mass (g)", levels: [0.66, 0.95], fill: INK }),
      chart()
        .flow(
          scatter({ x: "Body Mass (g)", overlap: Overlap.jitter() }).translate({
            y: -16,
          })
        )
        .mark(circle({ r: 1.5, fill: "Species", opacity: 0.6 })),
    ])
  );

(
    chart(penguins, w=560, axes={"x": {"title": "Body mass (g)"}, "y": True})
    .flow(spread(by="Species", dir="y", spacing=20))
    .mark(
        layer(
            [
                slab(x="Body Mass (g)", fill="Species", opacity=0.7),
                interval(x="Body Mass (g)", levels=[0.66, 0.95], fill=INK),
                chart()
                .flow(
                    scatter(x="Body Mass (g)", overlap=Overlap.jitter()).translate(
                        y=-16
                    )
                )
                .mark(circle(r=1.5, fill="Species", opacity=0.6)),
            ]
        )
    )
)

The first draft of A named the sample with rvar("Body Mass (g)"), a draw-less rvar. types.md drops that: a plain column in a Dist channel is already the group's rows as draws, by the one-fold rule. So A and B are the same code here, and the same column feeds the slab, the interval and the points. The slab smooths the sample with a default bandwidth. Writing x: field("Body Mass (g)").kde() would make the interval read the smoothed curve instead of the sample; which one the default should be is an open decision.

New pieces needed: slab and interval, which read a plain column as the group's sample. Overlap check: slab and interval desugar as in the half-eye. The rain part is the existing Overlap.jitter(). The placement of the rain below the baseline is a real gap that neither approach fixes. The sheet's slab({ side }) covers the cloud. A side option on the jitter (or a dots mark with side: "start", as in ggdist) would place the rain without a pixel translate.

Fan chart

An observed series, then nested 95%, 80% and 50% forecast bands around the median forecast, widening with the horizon. This is the Bank of England fan chart (Britton, Fisher and Whitley, "The Inflation Report projections: understanding the fan chart", Bank of England Quarterly Bulletin, 1998), and ggdist's stat_lineribbon.

Today

layer([
  chart(fanBands, {
    color: Color.palette({
      "95%": "#dbe6f2",
      "80%": "#a9c3df",
      "50%": "#6f98c6",
    }),
  })
    .flow(
      group({ by: "level" }),
      scatter({ by: "t", x: "t", yMin: "lo", yMax: "hi" })
    )
    .mark(rect({ w: 0, fill: "level" }))
    .layer(ribbon({ fill: "level" })),
  chart(fanMedian)
    .flow(scatter({ by: "t", x: "t", y: "value" }))
    .mark(line({ stroke: INK, strokeWidth: 2 })),
  chart(fanHistory)
    .flow(scatter({ by: "t", x: "t", y: "value" }))
    .mark(line({ stroke: "#333", strokeWidth: 1.5 })),
]).render(container, {
  w: 520,
  h: 300,
  axes: { x: { title: "time step" }, y: { title: "value" } },
});

Precomputed by hand: one band row per step and level with its normal quantile bounds, a median row per step, and the history (a seeded random walk). The bands are zero-width rects at each step, joined by a ribbon. The smooth band curve overshoots a little at step 0, where the band has no width.

Proposed (A)

const value = dist.normal({ mean: "median", sd: "sd" });

layer([
  chart(forecast)
    .flow(scatter({ by: "t", x: "t" }))
    .mark(
      interval({
        y: value,
        along: "t",
        levels: [0.5, 0.8, 0.95],
        fill: "#3a6ea5",
      })
    ),
  chart(history)
    .flow(scatter({ by: "t", x: "t", y: "value" }))
    .mark(line({ stroke: "#333", strokeWidth: 1.5 })),
]).render(container, {
  w: 520,
  h: 300,
  axes: { x: { title: "time step" }, y: { title: "value" } },
});

value = dist.normal(mean="median", sd="sd")

layer(
    [
        chart(forecast)
        .flow(scatter(by="t", x="t"))
        .mark(interval(y=value, along="t", levels=[0.5, 0.8, 0.95], fill="#3a6ea5")),
        chart(history)
        .flow(scatter(by="t", x="t", y="value"))
        .mark(line(stroke="#333", stroke_width=1.5)),
    ]
).render(w=520, h=300, axes={"x": {"title": "time step"}, "y": {"title": "value"}})

The data is one row per step, { t, median, sd }. I assumed that interval with along draws the median as a line, as its point form draws the median as a point, and that one fill gives the nested bands lighter shades of one color, as ggdist does. If it does not, the median is a second chart with y: value.median(). Under B the data would be sample paths, one row per step and draw, declared with schema: { value: { draws: "draw" } }, and the code reads the same with y: "value". Only then does band: Band.depth() mean something, because whole paths exist.

New pieces needed: dist.normal and interval with along. Overlap check: it desugars exactly into the Today code: per level, a zero-width rect from value.quantile(lo) to value.quantile(hi) at each step, joined by a ribbon, plus a line through the medians. The along form is the same mark as the point interval, with ribbon in place of rect and line in place of circle, so it fits the existing rule that along turns a mark into a connector.

Noise: uncertainty as noise

These examples follow ggdibbler, where a chart takes random variables in place of values, draws from each one, and places the draws. Sources: Mason, Cook, Goodwin, VanderPlas, "A Mathematical Framework and Software Implementation for Uncertainty Visualisation" (arXiv 2606.24217), and Mason, Rogers, Kleffner, Cook, "Colour Blinded by the Noise" (arXiv 2608.17976).

Stories: packages/gofish-graphics/stories/proposals/uncertainty/Noise.stories.tsx (title Proposals/Uncertainty/Noise). Data: noise-data.ts in the same folder. Every draw is precomputed there with a seeded RNG (mulberry32 and Box–Muller, seed 980).

Findings that apply to more than one example:

  • Today's group({ by: "draw" }) draws the draws in one frame. It splits the rows by the draw key and places nothing (src/ast/graphicalOperators/group.ts). It builds a Frame, the same node time.sequence builds for its one frame (src/ast/marks/time.ts). So no new operator is needed for the overlay. The mark sets the low opacity.
  • group works inside a spread slot and inside a table cell. An earlier draft used a layer of one chart per draw instead. That worked for the stacked bar, because sibling charts share their scales, but it failed for a table: the layered tables were laid out one after another (15000 px tall), and the color option on layer(...).render did not reach the charts. layer is not a flow step, and group is.
  • Alpha compositing is order-dependent: with opacity a and n layers, the last draw gets weight a and the first gets a(1−a)^(n−1). ggdibbler has the same bias. It is inherent to transparency, not to GoFish.
  • On spread, label: false does not hide the axis ticks. axes: false does.

Uncertain bar chart, dodge

Five estimates, each a normal(mean, se), with 20 draws per category dodged side by side as thin bars. This is the ggdibbler dodge position on a bar chart (Mason et al. 2026, arXiv 2606.24217, section on nested positions).

Today

chart(barDraws, { w: 480, h: 260 })
  .flow(
    spread({ by: "category", dir: "x", spacing: 12 }),
    spread({ by: "draw", dir: "x", spacing: 0, axes: false })
  )
  .mark(rect({ h: "value", fill: "#4e79a7" }))
  .render(container, { axes: true });

Precomputed by hand: the 20 draws of each category, as a long table with a draw column.

Proposed (A)

const value = dist.normal({ mean: "mean", sd: "se" });
chart(estimates)
  .flow(
    spread({ by: "category", dir: "x" }),
    sample({ n: 20 }),
    spread({ by: "draw", dir: "x", spacing: 0 })
  )
  .mark(rect({ h: value }));

value = dist.normal(mean="mean", sd="se")
chart(estimates).flow(
    spread(by="category", dir="x"), sample(n=20), spread(by="draw", dir="x", spacing=0)
).mark(rect(h=value))

Proposed (B) is the Today spec on a long table of draws. The only new piece is where the draws come from: sample of a dist.* channel, or draws data declared with schema: { value: { draws: "draw" } }.

New pieces needed: dist.normal, sample. The placement is the existing spread, so the lift desugars exactly into today's chart over a draw table. This spelling declares the Dist as a constant and first uses it in the mark, after sample in the flow. The reconciled rule (from the sample descriptor in types-sketch.ts) is that sample does not look for Dist cells at all. It turns each row into n rows with a draw key and a seed, and any Dist expression read on a drawn row reads that row's draw. The draw is a function of the seed, the row and the draw index, so two channels that name the same Dist read the same draw, and no channel has to be known before the mark.

Uncertain stacked bar, dodge

Sales by region (x) and product (stacked), each a normal; 20 draws are dodged inside each region, and each draw is a full stack by product. This is ggdibbler's stack_dodge position (Mason et al. 2026, Figure 7c).

Today

chart(salesDraws, { w: 480, h: 260 })
  .flow(
    spread({ by: "region", dir: "x", spacing: 16 }),
    spread({ by: "draw", dir: "x", spacing: 0, axes: false }),
    stack({ by: "product", dir: "y" })
  )
  .mark(rect({ h: "sales", fill: "product" }))
  .render(container, { axes: true });

Precomputed by hand: 20 draws of every (region, product) cell, clamped at 0.

Proposed (A)

const sales = dist.normal({ mean: "mean", sd: "se" });
chart(estimates)
  .flow(
    spread({ by: "region", dir: "x" }),
    sample({ n: 20 }),
    spread({ by: "draw", dir: "x", spacing: 0 }),
    stack({ by: "product", dir: "y" })
  )
  .mark(rect({ h: sales, fill: "product" }));

sales = dist.normal(mean="mean", sd="se")
chart(estimates).flow(
    spread(by="region", dir="x"),
    sample(n=20),
    spread(by="draw", dir="x", spacing=0),
    stack(by="product", dir="y"),
).mark(rect(h=sales, fill="product"))

Writing stack({ by: "draw", dir: "y" }) in place of the draw spread gives this error (text from the design note, section 5):

stack({ by: "draw", dir: "y" }): "draw" is a sample index. The draws of one value are
a sample, and their sum grows with the number of draws, so the stacked total has no
meaning. stack needs a key whose groups add up to a whole.
Place the draws with spread({ by: "draw", dir: "x" }), with time.sequence({ by: "draw" }),
or stack by a category inside each draw.

Proposed (B) reads the same as Today. In B, draw is an ordinary column unless the schema declares it a draw key (schema: { sales: { draws: "draw" } }) or sample made it. If it is only a column, B cannot raise the stack error. With the record, both A and B raise it from the key's type (HasDraws).

New pieces needed: dist.normal, sample, and a capability on the draw key that stack checks. Placement desugars exactly into existing spread and stack.

Uncertain stacked bar, transparency (z direction)

The same data, with all 20 draws of each region drawn in the same slot at low opacity, so the product boundaries blur where they are uncertain. This is ggdibbler's stack_identity position (Mason et al. 2026, Figure 7d).

Today

chart(salesDraws, { w: 480, h: 260 })
  .flow(
    spread({ by: "region", dir: "x", spacing: 16 }),
    group({ by: "draw" }),
    stack({ by: "product", dir: "y" })
  )
  .mark(rect({ h: "sales", fill: "product", opacity: 0.1 }))
  .render(container, { axes: true });

Precomputed by hand: the draws and the opacity (0.1 for 20 draws).

group({ by: "draw" }) splits each region's rows by draw and places nothing, so the 20 stacks of a region land in the same slot.

Proposed (A)

chart(estimates)
  .flow(
    spread({ by: "region", dir: "x" }),
    sample({ n: 20 }),
    group({ by: "draw" }),
    stack({ by: "product", dir: "y" })
  )
  .mark(rect({ h: sales, fill: "product", opacity: field("draw").share() }));

chart(estimates).flow(
    spread(by="region", dir="x"),
    sample(n=20),
    group(by="draw"),
    stack(by="product", dir="y"),
).mark(rect(h=sales, fill="product", opacity=field("draw").share()))

New pieces needed: dist.normal, sample, and the opacity rule. The layout is today's group. The opacity is written as field("draw").share(), the draw's share of the total weight, which is 1/20 = 0.05 here. That needs opacity to read a field. The other option is a default opacity for marks under a draw key. proposal.md lists both as open decision 5.

Uncertain scatter

Twelve points whose x and y are both uncertain; 30 draws of each point are drawn at low opacity, so each point becomes a cloud whose spread shows its uncertainty. This follows the uncertain mtcars scatter in Mason et al. 2026, Figure 10, colored by a three-level group as that figure is colored by cyl.

Today

chart(pointDraws, { w: 400, h: 320 })
  .flow(scatter({ x: "x", y: "y" }))
  .mark(circle({ r: 3, fill: "group", opacity: 0.3 }))
  .render(container, { axes: true });

Precomputed by hand: 30 (x, y) draws per point.

Proposed (A)

const x = dist.normal({ mean: "x", sd: "xSe" });
const y = dist.normal({ mean: "y", sd: "ySe" });
chart(points)
  .flow(sample({ n: 30 }), scatter({ x, y }))
  .mark(circle({ r: 3, fill: "group", opacity: 0.3 }));

x = dist.normal(mean="x", sd="xSe")
y = dist.normal(mean="y", sd="ySe")
chart(points).flow(sample(n=30), scatter(x=x, y=y)).mark(
    circle(r=3, fill="group", opacity=0.3)
)

New pieces needed: dist.normal, sample. scatter overlaps the draws by itself, so no group step is needed. The opacity is set by hand. The share rule, opacity: field("draw").share(), would give 1/30. The two independent normals stand in for one bivariate draw. A correlated (x, y) needs a joint constructor, which the sheet does not have. The spelling chosen here, to keep dist.* regular, is const p = dist.mvnormal({ mean: { x: "x", y: "y" }, sd: { x: "xSe", y: "ySe" }, cor: "rho" }), read as scatter({ x: p.x, y: p.y }). p.x and p.y are the marginals of one joint Dist, so a sample draws them together.

HOPs (hypothetical outcome plots)

Four bars animated over draws, one frame per draw, so the viewer sees the spread of outcomes over time (Hullman, Resnick, Adar 2015; the t direction in the design note). A PNG cannot animate, so the strip shows 6 frames as small multiples, and a second story holds the real animation still on draw 0.

Today (strip)

chart(hopDraws, { w: 720, h: 160 })
  .flow(
    spread({ by: "draw", dir: "x", spacing: 24 }),
    spread({ by: "category", dir: "x", spacing: 4 })
  )
  .mark(rect({ h: "value", fill: "#4e79a7" }))
  .render(container, { axes: true });

Today (animation, time.sequence exists and renders)

chart(hopDraws, { w: 240, h: 160 })
  .flow(
    time.sequence({ by: "draw", duration: 3000, playing: false }),
    spread({ by: "category", dir: "x", spacing: 4 })
  )
  .mark(rect({ h: "value", fill: "#4e79a7" }))
  .render(container, { axes: true });

Precomputed by hand: 6 draws per bar. playing: false only holds the frame for the screenshot. The y axis holds still across frames because time.sequence infers domains over every keyframe.

Proposed (A)

const value = dist.normal({ mean: "mean", sd: "se" });
chart(estimates)
  .flow(
    sample({ n: 50 }),
    time.sequence({ by: "draw" }),
    spread({ by: "category", dir: "x" })
  )
  .mark(rect({ h: value }));

value = dist.normal(mean="mean", sd="se")
chart(estimates).flow(
    sample(n=50), time.sequence(by="draw"), spread(by="category", dir="x")
).mark(rect(h=value))

The strip is the same chart with spread({ by: "draw", dir: "x", spacing: 24 }) in place of time.sequence.

New pieces needed: dist.normal, sample. The animation desugars exactly into the existing time.sequence, which needs a numeric key; sample's draw index is numeric, so it fits.

Uncertain map: means, pixel, transparency

An 8×6 grid of square regions stands in for a map; each region holds a normal whose sd grows to the east. The three plates follow the choropleth comparisons in Mason et al. "Colour Blinded by the Noise" (arXiv 2608.17976): (a) the means, (b) a pixel map where each region is split into a 4×4 grid of draws, (c) the draws of each region drawn on top of each other with transparency. The noisy eastern tiles look grainy in (b) and washed out in (c).

Today (a)

chart(tileEstimates, { w: 320, h: 240, color: MAP_COLOR })
  .flow(table({ by: { x: "col", y: "row" }, spacing: 2 }))
  .mark(rect({ fill: "mean" }))
  .render(container, { axes: false });

Today (b)

chart(tileDraws, { w: 320, h: 240, color: MAP_COLOR })
  .flow(
    table({ by: { x: "col", y: "row" }, spacing: 2 }),
    table({ by: { x: "sx", y: "sy" }, spacing: 0 })
  )
  .mark(rect({ fill: "value" }))
  .render(container, { axes: false });

There is no subdivide operator. The pixel version is a table nested in a table, with the subcell of each draw (sx = draw % 4, sy = floor(draw / 4)) precomputed in the data. The flow form of table needs by: { x, y }; it has no single-key wrap with numCols. On a real map the pixel version needs a way to cut a region's shape into cells, which does not exist.

Today (c)

chart(tileDraws, { w: 320, h: 240, color: MAP_COLOR })
  .flow(
    table({ by: { x: "col", y: "row" }, spacing: 2 }),
    group({ by: "draw" })
  )
  .mark(rect({ fill: "value", opacity: 0.15 }))
  .render(container, { axes: false });

group({ by: "draw" }) splits each cell's 16 rows by draw, so each draw gets its own rect and the 16 rects land on top of each other. Two things failed before group was used: without the split, a table cell with 16 rows draws one rect, not 16 (48 rects for 768 rows, with no warning), and a layer of 16 table charts lays them out one after another.

Precomputed by hand for all three: 16 draws per tile. GoFish has no way to fix a color domain, so (a) uses the range of the means (40 to 75) and (b), (c) use the range of the draws (−21 to 112). The paper's plates share one scale; these do not. The gradient legend also prints long decimals and overlaps its lowest tick label (an existing cosmetic issue). The table column labels pile up at the left when axes are on, so axes are off here.

Proposed (A)

const value = dist.normal({ mean: "mean", sd: "sd" });
const blues = Color.gradient(["#f7fbff", "#6baed6", "#08306b"]);

// (a) means
chart(tiles, { color: blues })
  .flow(table({ by: { x: "col", y: "row" }, spacing: 2 }))
  .mark(rect({ fill: value.mean() }));

// (b) pixel
chart(tiles, { color: blues })
  .flow(
    table({ by: { x: "col", y: "row" }, spacing: 2 }),
    sample({ n: 16 }),
    table({ by: "draw", numCols: 4, spacing: 0 }) // new: single-key wrap in flow form
  )
  .mark(rect({ fill: value }));

// (c) transparency
chart(tiles, { color: blues })
  .flow(
    table({ by: { x: "col", y: "row" }, spacing: 2 }),
    sample({ n: 16 }),
    group({ by: "draw" })
  )
  .mark(rect({ fill: value, opacity: field("draw").share() }));

value = dist.normal(mean="mean", sd="sd")
blues = Color.gradient(["#f7fbff", "#6baed6", "#08306b"])

# (a) means
chart(tiles, color=blues).flow(table(by={"x": "col", "y": "row"}, spacing=2)).mark(
    rect(fill=value.mean())
)

# (b) pixel
chart(tiles, color=blues).flow(
    table(by={"x": "col", "y": "row"}, spacing=2),
    sample(n=16),
    table(by="draw", num_cols=4, spacing=0),  # new: single-key wrap in flow form
).mark(rect(fill=value))

# (c) transparency
chart(tiles, color=blues).flow(
    table(by={"x": "col", "y": "row"}, spacing=2), sample(n=16), group(by="draw")
).mark(rect(fill=value, opacity=field("draw").share()))

New pieces needed: dist.normal, sample, d.mean(), the opacity rule, and a single-key table({ by, numCols }) in flow form (the numCols option exists on the node but the flow form rejects a single by). Papers B needed the same single-key grid for its small multiples and first wrote spread({ wrap }). The reconciled spelling for both is table({ by, numCols }), because table already owns numCols, and a wrap on spread would be a second route to the same grid. For a real map, (b) needs a "cut a shape into cells" operator; that is a new operator with no existing overlap. The three plates should share one color domain, which needs a domain option on Color.gradient (not on the sheet, and not a Dist question). Overlap check: (c) is the same group and opacity rule as the stacked bar. A fill channel drawn this way is a color mix, which is the point of the plate.

Papers A: uncertainty examples from recent IEEE VIS papers

Prototype stories: packages/gofish-graphics/stories/proposals/uncertainty/PapersA.stories.tsx (title Proposals/Uncertainty/Papers A). Data and helpers, with a seeded mulberry32 RNG, Box–Muller normals and a Marsaglia–Tsang gamma for χ² and Dirichlet draws: papers-a-data.ts next to it. Each render below is the story's PNG. The Python blocks were formatted with black (run through uvx black).

// new: marks a spelling that the shared syntax sheet does not cover. The list at the end collects them.

Bootstrap regression spaghetti

Forty observed points drawn crisp, with 39 regression lines, one for each bootstrap resample of the rows, overlaid at low opacity. Thirty-nine draws give a 95% pointwise band by the paper's coverage rule, (n−1)/(n+1). Source: Petek, Nabergoj, Štrumbelj, "A General Approach to Visualizing Uncertainty in Statistical Graphics" (https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.00937, Fig. 7).

Today

chart(bootPoints)
  .flow(scatter({ by: "id", x: "x", y: "y" }))
  .mark(circle({ r: 3, fill: "#222" }))
  .layer(
    chart(bootLines)
      .flow(group({ by: "draw" }), scatter({ by: "x", x: "x", y: "y" }))
      .mark(line({ stroke: "#d62728", strokeWidth: 1.5, opacity: 0.12 }))
  );

Precomputed by hand: the 39 resamples and an OLS fit of each, stored as two endpoints per line. The opacity 0.12 is picked by hand.

Proposed (A)

chart(points)
  .flow(scatter({ by: "id", x: "x", y: "y" }))
  .mark(circle({ r: 3, fill: "#222" }))
  .layer(
    chart(points)
      .flow(
        resample({ n: 39 }), // or resample({ coverage: 0.95 })
        group({ by: "draw" }),
        derive(fitLine), // a user function; runs once per draw
        scatter({ by: "x", x: "x", y: "y" })
      )
      .mark(line({ stroke: "#d62728", opacity: field("draw").share() }))
  );

chart(points).flow(scatter(by="id", x="x", y="y")).mark(circle(r=3, fill="#222")).layer(
    chart(points)
    .flow(
        resample(n=39),  # or resample(coverage=0.95)
        group(by="draw"),
        derive(fit_line),  # a user function; runs once per draw
        scatter(by="x", x="x", y="y"),
    )
    .mark(line(stroke="#d62728", opacity=field("draw").share()))
)

Approach B reads the same: resample already makes draws as rows, so there is no Dist field here in either approach.

New pieces needed

  • resample({ n }) / resample({ coverage }): new. It does not desugar into an existing operator. A derive can do the same with a user function, but then the draw key and its "shared draws" property are not known to the system. coverage is not in the resample descriptor of types-sketch.ts. It is kept here with the same rule as sample's, n = (1 + c) / (1 − c), because both make n draws and the rule is the paper's.
  • group({ by: "draw" }) is today's, as in the Today code. The draw key's type comes from resample, which marks draw as a sample index.
  • opacity: field("draw").share(): new. It is the draw's share of the total weight, here 1/39. It needs opacity to read a field. The other option, a default opacity under a draw key, is open decision 5 in proposal.md.
  • No regression transform exists in GoFish. The spelling uses a user function in derive, which the sheet's rule 2 runs once per draw. A built-in fit helper would be a separate decision.

Weighted multiple-forecast lines

Nine forecast lines of a temperature anomaly over 30 months, each line's opacity set by its model weight (0.1 to 1). Source: Zou et al., "Seeing Through the Forecast Clutter" (https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00433). The paper's stimuli were not visible in the fetched text, so the data and styling are a stand-in that follows the described design (9 lines, opacity weights from 0.1 to 1).

Today

// Line opacity takes a number, not a field, so each model is its own layer.
const [first, ...rest] = forecastWeights.map((weight) =>
  chart(forecasts.filter((d) => d.weight === weight))
    .flow(scatter({ by: "month", x: "month", y: "temp" }))
    .mark(line({ stroke: "#1f4e79", strokeWidth: 2, opacity: weight }))
);
rest.reduce((c, layer) => c.layer(layer), first);

Precomputed by hand: the nine trajectories. The chart has to split the table by model in JavaScript, because line's opacity cannot read a field today.

Proposed (A)

chart(forecasts, { schema: { temp: { draws: "model", weight: "weight" } } })
  .flow(group({ by: "model" }), scatter({ by: "month", x: "month", y: "temp" }))
  // new: opacity reads a field
  .mark(line({ stroke: "#1f4e79", strokeWidth: 2, opacity: "weight" }));

chart(forecasts, schema={"temp": {"draws": "model", "weight": "weight"}}).flow(
    group(by="model"),
    scatter(by="month", x="month", y="temp"),
).mark(
    # new: opacity reads a field
    line(stroke="#1f4e79", stroke_width=2, opacity="weight")
)

Proposed (B) is the same chart without the column record.

New pieces needed

  • The column record's draws and weight keys: new. The first draft wrote rvar("temp", { draw: "model", weight: "weight" }) and a sample() with no n. With the record, the rows are already the draws, so no sample() is needed: sample() with no n keeps every stored draw, which is the identity on a draws column. The record tells the checker that model is a draw key. The opacity does not need it.
  • line({ opacity: "weight" }): new, because opacity takes only a number today. The paper maps the weights linearly into 0.1 to 1, so the heaviest line is opaque, and the weight column holds those values. The share rule, opacity: field("model").share(), would draw each line at w / Σw instead. The nine weights sum to about 5, so the heaviest line would be drawn at about 0.2, fainter than the paper's. Which rule to use is the user's choice on the mark. A linewidth option (the paper's other condition) is also open.
  • Overlap check: the layout is today's group({ by: "model" }), the same as the multiline chart. The only library change is a field-valued opacity.

VMC "aggregate per draw"

A histogram of the mean of each of 200 posterior-predictive replicated datasets (50 values each), with a vertical line at the observed data's mean. Source: Guo, Kale, Kay, Hullman, "VMC: A Grammar for Visualizing Statistical Model Checks" (https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.16702).

Today

chart(replicates)
  .flow(
    // The per-draw fold, by hand: one row per replicate, its mean.
    derive((rows) =>
      Object.values(groupBy(rows, "draw")).map((g) => ({
        mean: meanBy(g, "y"),
      }))
    ),
    partition({ by: field("mean").bin(Bin.step(0.05)), dir: "x" })
  )
  .mark(rect({ h: field("mean").count(), fill: "#9ecae1", inset: 0.5 }))
  .layer(
    chart(observedMeanRule)
      .flow(scatter({ by: "count", x: "mean", y: "count" }))
      .mark(line({ stroke: "#08306b", strokeWidth: 2, curve: Curve.linear() }))
  );

Precomputed by hand: the 200 replicates (a posterior draw of (μ, σ), then 50 values), the observed mean, and the rule as two points from count 0 to 22. GoFish has no rule mark, and a rect with a literal height did not span the y axis in data units, so the rule is a two-point line.

Proposed (A)

chart(replicates, { schema: { y: { draws: "draw" } } })
  .flow(
    group({ by: "draw" }),
    summarize({ mean: field("y").mean() }), // new: one row per group
    partition({ by: field("mean").bin(Bin.step(0.05)), dir: "x" })
  )
  .mark(rect({ h: field("mean").count(), fill: "#9ecae1", inset: 0.5 }))
  // new: rule mark
  .layer(
    chart(observed).mark(rule({ x: field("y").mean(), stroke: "#08306b" }))
  );

chart(replicates, schema={"y": {"draws": "draw"}}).flow(
    group(by="draw"),
    summarize(mean=field("y").mean()),  # new: one row per group
    partition(by=field("mean").bin(Bin.step(0.05)), dir="x"),
).mark(rect(h=field("mean").count(), fill="#9ecae1", inset=0.5)).layer(
    chart(observed).mark(rule(x=field("y").mean(), stroke="#08306b"))
)

Proposed (B)

chart(replicates)
  .flow(
    group({ by: "draw" }),
    summarize({ mean: field("y").mean() }),
    partition({ by: field("mean").bin(Bin.step(0.05)), dir: "x" })
  )
  .mark(rect({ h: field("mean").count(), fill: "#9ecae1", inset: 0.5 }))
  .layer(
    chart(observed).mark(rule({ x: field("y").mean(), stroke: "#08306b" }))
  );

The step choice. VMC's "aggregate" grouping is a fold inside each draw, then a picture of the folded values. I spell it as two ordinary steps: group({ by: "draw" }) then summarize({ ... }), a new general step that collapses each group to one row of aggregates. After it, mean is a plain column with one row per draw, so the histogram is the existing partition plus count. I did not use a Dist-level spelling such as "the row mean of a Dist column is a Dist" (functor map over shared draws). types.md's one-summary rule settles the naming conflict the design note's section 5c flags: .mean() always summarizes the group's folded Dist, so here, inside one draw's group, it is that replicate's mean.

New pieces needed

  • summarize: new, and not uncertainty-specific. GoFish has no step that turns each group into one row; today it is a derive with lodash. It overlaps with aggregate channels (field("y").mean() in a mark), but those cannot feed a later partition, so the overlap is partial.
  • rule: new mark (a full-span line at one x or y). Today it is a two-point line.
  • The fold is only valid with shared draws (a draw is a whole replicate). The record's draws: "draw" declares this, in A and B alike. Without it nothing checks it.
  • A and B differ only by the schema record. The first draft's A also had an rvar and a sample(), which the record makes unneeded.
  • summarize takes its columns flat, summarize({ mean: ... }), as dplyr's summarize(mean = ...) and polars' agg(mean=...) do. The descriptor in types-sketch.ts names the record columns. Whether the wire wraps the flat form is open.

jk-CDF band

Fifty Normal CDF curves, one for each posterior draw of (μ, σ) from a Normal model of 15 observations, drawn faint over the empirical CDF step line. Source: Piccolotto et al., "Adaptive Uncertainty Visualization With jk-Plots", VIS 2026 short paper (https://ieeevis.org/year/2026/program/content/87e9069f-c5dd-411a-9cbf-5730077c7a18, OSF https://osf.io/wzv2g).

Today

chart(jkCdfDraws)
  .flow(group({ by: "draw" }), scatter({ by: "x", x: "x", y: "p" }))
  .mark(line({ stroke: "#3182bd", strokeWidth: 1, opacity: 0.15 }))
  .layer(
    chart(ecdf)
      .flow(scatter({ by: "x", x: "x", y: "p" }))
      .mark(line({ stroke: "#111", strokeWidth: 1.5, curve: Curve.step() }))
  );

Precomputed by hand: the 50 posterior draws of (μ, σ) (flat prior, χ² for σ), each CDF evaluated on a grid of 61 x values, and the empirical CDF as sorted step points.

Proposed (A)

// posterior: [{ draw, mu, sigma }], one row per posterior draw
const model = dist.normal({ mean: "mu", sd: "sigma" }); // Dist<Dist<number>>

chart(posterior, {
  schema: { mu: { draws: "draw" }, sigma: { draws: "draw" } },
})
  .flow(sample({ n: 50 }), group({ by: "draw" }))
  // new: cdf mark; new: opacity reads a field
  .mark(cdf({ x: model, stroke: "#3182bd", opacity: field("draw").share() }))
  .layer(
    // the empirical CDF of the observed rows
    chart(observations).mark(cdf({ x: "value", stroke: "#111" }))
  );

# posterior: one row per posterior draw, columns draw, mu, sigma
model = dist.normal(mean="mu", sd="sigma")  # Dist[Dist[float]]

chart(posterior, schema={"mu": {"draws": "draw"}, "sigma": {"draws": "draw"}}).flow(
    sample(n=50), group(by="draw")
).mark(
    # new: cdf mark; new: opacity reads a field
    cdf(x=model, stroke="#3182bd", opacity=field("draw").share())
).layer(
    chart(observations).mark(cdf(x="value", stroke="#111"))
)

Proposed (B) is not shown. The first draft sampled the inner normal into rows with a constructor form, sample({ value: dist.normal(...), n: 500, as: "x" }). types.md drops that form: a dist.* channel is A's spelling in the synthesis, and sample({ n }) lifts it. Pure B, with no Dist type, would still need 500 rows per draw.

New pieces needed

  • cdf as a mark: new. The sheet says "cdf as line over field(d).cdf() if needed", but a CDF is a curve over a grid of x values, not one value per row, so it does not fit a line over rows. I chose a consume mark cdf({ x: d }). Given a plain column, it draws the empirical CDF of the group's rows as a step line, which also covers the observed layer.
  • Nested dist.normal({ mean: "mu", sd: "sigma" }) over columns declared as draws: the sheet's rvar nesting, with the draw key moved to the column record. Its parameters are Dists, so it is a Dist<Dist>. The outer sample({ n: 50 }) picks 50 of the stored posterior draws and keeps their ids, so they stay joint. The inner Normal stays a Dist that cdf consumes exactly. This is the clearest case for Approach A.
  • Pure B cannot keep the inner Normal, so it has to sample it (500 values per draw). The curves become ECDFs, which are noisy and cost 25,000 rows. This is where A and B read and behave differently.

jk pie

A jk pie: concentric rings in polar coordinates, one ring per posterior draw of three category probabilities (20 draws from Dirichlet(1 + counts), counts 7, 5, 3), stacked by category around each ring. Same source as above.

Today

chart(pieDraws, { coord: Coord.clock(), axes: false })
  .flow(
    spread({ by: "draw", dir: "y", spacing: 1, y: 30 }),
    stack({ by: "category", dir: "x" })
  )
  .mark(rect({ w: "p", h: 6, fill: "category" }));

Precomputed by hand: the 20 Dirichlet draws as a long table. I used Coord.clock() (12 o'clock start, clockwise, like a pie) rather than Coord.polar(); the layout is the same. y: 30 leaves a hole in the middle.

Proposed (A)

// counts: [{ category, count }], the observed counts
// new: dist.dirichlet, one joint distribution over the rows
const probs = dist.dirichlet({ alpha: field("count").plus(1) });

chart(counts, { coord: Coord.polar(), axes: false })
  .flow(
    sample({ n: 20 }),
    spread({ by: "draw", dir: "y", spacing: 0 }),
    stack({ by: "category", dir: "x" })
  )
  .mark(rect({ w: probs, h: 6, fill: "category" }));

# counts: one row per category, columns category, count
probs = dist.dirichlet(alpha=field("count").plus(1))  # new: one joint over the rows

chart(counts, coord=Coord.polar(), axes=False).flow(
    sample(n=20),
    spread(by="draw", dir="y", spacing=0),
    stack(by="category", dir="x"),
).mark(rect(w=probs, h=6, fill="category"))

Proposed (B)

// pieDraws: [{ draw, category, p }], posterior draws as rows
chart(pieDraws, { coord: Coord.polar(), axes: false })
  .flow(
    spread({ by: "draw", dir: "y", spacing: 0 }),
    stack({ by: "category", dir: "x" })
  )
  .mark(rect({ w: "p", h: 6, fill: "category" }));

B is today's chart.

New pieces needed

  • dist.dirichlet: new. It is a joint over the rows of a group (one draw is a whole probability vector), not a distribution per cell. The sheet's constructors are all per cell. A joint constructor says "these rows share draws", which is what makes stack within a draw legal: each ring sums to 1.
  • No new operator. Stack within draw and spread across draws already compose in polar, as the render shows. The rule "stacking by draw on the measured axis is a type error" is not hit, because the stack is by category.
  • The brief calls this Dist<Dist<T>>: a Dirichlet is a distribution over categorical distributions. In A, the w channel lifts the outer level and each ring shows one inner categorical. A prob()-style consume mark is not needed.

Probit-scaled CCDF for ordinal outcomes

Rating levels on x and Φ⁻¹ P(y ≥ k) on y, one line per posterior draw (40) and condition (2), from a cumulative-probit model. The median rating is where the lines cross 0. Source: Sarma, "Adapting CCDF Plots for Visualizing Ordinal Regression Results" (https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01747). The figure images were not in the fetched text, so axis styling is a guess.

Today

chart(ccdfDraws, {
  color: Color.palette({ Control: "#7f7f7f", Treatment: "#d95f02" }),
})
  .flow(group({ by: "line" }), scatter({ by: "k", x: "k", y: "z" }))
  .mark(
    line({
      stroke: "condition",
      strokeWidth: 1,
      opacity: 0.3,
      curve: Curve.linear(),
    })
  )
  .layer(
    chart(probitZero)
      .flow(scatter({ by: "k", x: "k", y: "z" }))
      .mark(
        line({ stroke: "#111", strokeDasharray: "4 3", curve: Curve.linear() })
      )
  );

Precomputed by hand: the posterior draws, the CCDF, the probit transform (z = μc − τ{k−1}), a line key joining condition and draw (a group takes one key), and the zero rule as two points. Approximations: the y axis shows z values, not probabilities, because GoFish has no probit scale. The x axis is continuous, so it has ticks at 2.2, 2.4 and so on. The x axis runs from 2 to 5 because P(y ≥ 1) = 1 is +∞ on the probit scale.

Proposed (A)

// epred: [{ condition, draw, k, p }], p = P(rating = k) in that draw
const rating = dist.categorical({ level: "k", p: "p" }); // Dist<Dist<Ordinal>>

chart(epred, {
  schema: { p: { draws: "draw" } },
  color: Color.palette({ Control: "#7f7f7f", Treatment: "#d95f02" }),
  axes: { y: { scale: Scale.probit() } }, // new: a scale family, on the axis
})
  .flow(sample({ n: 40 }), group({ by: "condition" }), group({ by: "draw" }))
