  // new: ccdf mark; new: rule mark; new: opacity reads a field
  .mark(
    ccdf({ x: rating, stroke: "condition", opacity: field("draw").share() })
  )
  .layer(rule({ y: 0, strokeDasharray: "4 3" }));

# epred: columns condition, draw, k, p with p = P(rating = k) in that draw
rating = dist.categorical(level="k", p="p")  # Dist[Dist[Ordinal]]

chart(
    epred,
    schema={"p": {"draws": "draw"}},
    color=Color.palette({"Control": "#7f7f7f", "Treatment": "#d95f02"}),
    axes={"y": {"scale": Scale.probit()}},  # new: a scale family, on the axis
).flow(sample(n=40), group(by="condition"), group(by="draw")).mark(
    ccdf(x=rating, stroke="condition", opacity=field("draw").share())
).layer(
    rule(y=0, stroke_dasharray="4 3")
)

Proposed (B)

chart(epred, {
  schema: { k: { weight: "p" } }, // new: a weight with no draw key
  color: Color.palette({ Control: "#7f7f7f", Treatment: "#d95f02" }),
  axes: { y: { scale: Scale.probit() } },
})
  .flow(group({ by: "condition" }), group({ by: "draw" }))
  .mark(ccdf({ x: "k", stroke: "condition", opacity: field("draw").share() }))
  .layer(rule({ y: 0, strokeDasharray: "4 3" }));

New pieces needed

  • ccdf mark: new, the complement of the cdf mark above. On an ordinal T it draws one point per level, joined by a line.
  • dist.categorical({ level, p }): new. It builds one categorical from the rows that share every other key (here condition and draw), with level naming the ordered outcome. With p declared as draws it is Dist<Dist<Ordinal>>, like the jk-CDF.
  • By the one-fold rule, a group of rows whose values are weighted is already that categorical: the rows of one (condition, draw) group, with k weighted by p, fold to the same Dist as dist.categorical({ level: "k", p: "p" }). So B's spelling is not a second mechanism, and dist.categorical across rows may not be needed at all. B writes the weight in the column record, as types.md does for draws, and drops the mark's weight option. A weight key with no draws key means the rows are weighted support points. This is a choice made here; types.md shows weight only beside draws.
  • The probit axis: a scale acts on every mark on its axis (design note section 4), so it belongs on the axis, not on ccdf. GoFish has no scale family, so Scale.probit() is a placeholder spelling in the strategy-family form. It would show probability tick labels (0.1, 0.5, 0.9 at their z positions). A scale family is its own issue, not part of #980.
  • rule: the same new mark as in the VMC example.
  • In B, ccdf({ x: "k" }) over weighted rows is a weighted ECDF over the group's rows. It needs no Dist constructor, and it is shorter. A buys only the check that k is ordered and that mean() on it is an error, and the record gives B that check too.

Syntax choices the sheet did not cover

These are the spellings after reconciling with types.md.

  • The draw key and the weight live in the column record: schema: { temp: { draws: "model", weight: "weight" } }. rvar is gone.
  • sample() with no n keeps every stored draw of a draws column, so it is the identity there and the examples leave it out.
  • No overlay step. Draws in one frame use today's group({ by: "draw" }), and the mark sets the opacity: field("draw").share() for the draw's share of the weight, or a column such as "weight" for a per-line opacity. Whether the share should instead be a default is open decision 5 in proposal.md.
  • resample({ coverage }) uses sample's coverage rule.
  • summarize({ name: fieldExpr }): a new general step, one row per group, with the columns flat.
  • rule({ x }) / rule({ y }): a new full-span reference line mark.
  • cdf({ x }) / ccdf({ x }) as consume marks. Given a plain column, they draw the empirical CDF of the rows, weighted if the record has a weight.
  • dist.dirichlet({ alpha }) and dist.categorical({ level, p }): joint constructors over the rows of a group. A weighted fold covers dist.categorical.
  • The probit scale is on the axis, axes: { y: { scale: Scale.probit() } }, a placeholder for a scale family that does not exist.
  • A regression fit is a user function in derive; I did not add a built-in.

Gaps found in today's GoFish

  • opacity takes only a number on every mark, so weighted lines need one layer per weight.
  • There is no rule mark. A rect with a literal h did not span the y axis in data units.
  • There is no probit (or any nonlinear) y scale.
  • group takes one key, so condition × draw needs a joined key column.

Papers B: uncertainty examples from recent IEEE VIS papers

Prototype stories: packages/gofish-graphics/stories/proposals/uncertainty/PapersB.stories.tsx (title Proposals/Uncertainty/Papers B). Data and helpers, with a seeded mulberry32 RNG and Box–Muller normals: papers-b-data.ts next to it. Each render below is the story's PNG.

Depth band vs pointwise ribbon for a line ensemble

Fifty whole-curve draws summarized two ways: pointwise quantile ribbons, and a functional boxplot whose band is the envelope of the deepest half of the curves under modified band depth. Sources: Chaves-de-Plaza et al., "Inclusion Depth for Contour Ensembles", TVCG 2024 (doi 10.1109/TVCG.2024.3350076), and the functional boxplot of Sun and Genton (JCGS 2011).

Today

// Today, panel (b). Panel (a) is the same with pointwiseBands and
// pointwiseMedian, and no outlier layer.
layer([
  chart(curveDraws)
    .flow(group({ by: "curve" }), scatter({ x: "x", y: "y" }))
    .mark(line({ stroke: "#999", strokeWidth: 0.5, opacity: 0.4 })),
  chart(depthBands, { color: BLUES })
    .flow(group({ by: "level" }), scatter({ by: "x", x: "x" }))
    .mark(rect({ w: 0, y: "lo", y2: "hi", fill: "level" }))
    .layer(ribbon({ along: "x", fill: "level" })),
  chart(depthMedian)
    .flow(scatter({ x: "x", y: "y" }))
    .mark(line({ stroke: "#08306b", strokeWidth: 2 })),
  chart(depthOutliers)
    .flow(group({ by: "curve" }), scatter({ x: "x", y: "y" }))
    .mark(line({ stroke: "#d62728", strokeWidth: 1, strokeDasharray: "4 2" })),
]);

Precomputed by hand: the pointwise quantiles and median at each x, the modified band depth of each curve, the envelope of the deepest 25 curves, the deepest curve, the 1.5-times-band outlier rule, and the envelope of the non-outliers. The planted shape outliers (two curves with a fast wiggle) stay inside the 1.5-times band, so the rule flags only the curve that jumps. This is how the functional boxplot behaves, not a rendering issue. Panel (b) shows that the deepest curve is a real smooth member, while the pointwise median in (a) is bumpy and is no member at all.

Proposed (A)

const draws = { schema: { y: { draws: "curve" } } };

// (a) pointwise quantile ribbons and the pointwise median
chart(curveDraws, draws)
  .flow(scatter({ by: "x", x: "x" }))
  .mark(
    interval({ y: "y", along: "x", levels: [0.5, 0.9], band: Band.pointwise() })
  );

// (b) depth band: the envelope of the deepest 50% of whole curves, the
// deepest curve as the median, and the outlier curves
chart(curveDraws, draws)
  .flow(scatter({ by: "x", x: "x" }))
  .mark(
    interval({
      y: "y",
      along: "x",
      levels: [0.5],
      band: Band.depth({ outliers: 1.5 }),
    })
  );

schema = {"y": {"draws": "curve"}}

# (a) pointwise quantile ribbons
chart(curve_draws, schema=schema).flow(scatter(by="x", x="x")).mark(
    interval(y="y", along="x", levels=[0.5, 0.9], band=Band.pointwise())
)

# (b) depth band
chart(curve_draws, schema=schema).flow(scatter(by="x", x="x")).mark(
    interval(y="y", along="x", levels=[0.5], band=Band.depth(outliers=1.5))
)

Proposed (B) is the same code. The first draft of B put the draw key on the mark, interval({ y: "y", draw: "curve", ... }), because B had no rvar. With the draw key in the column record, neither approach needs a per-mark draw option.

New pieces needed

  • The record's draws key, interval with along, and the Band family with pointwise() and depth(). Band.depth({ outliers }) (a number, default 1.5, or false) is the spelling types.md adopted from this example.
  • Band.depth() also changes what the median line is: the deepest whole draw, not the pointwise median. The strategy owns that choice.
  • Overlap: Band.pointwise() desugars exactly into today's scatter({ by: "x" }) + rect({ y, y2 }) + ribbon({ along: "x" }), with per-x quantiles as the rect's y and y2. Band.depth() does not desugar into per-x summaries. It needs one aggregation over the draw key across all x (the depth of each curve), then a filter to the deepest k%, and only then a pointwise min and max. So there is a large partial overlap: after the depth ranking, the rest is the pointwise path on a subset of draws. One could expose the ranking as a field op (field(y).depth()) and build the band from it, but then the user writes the filter and the envelope by hand.
  • Depth needs to know which rows form one curve. The column record says so, in A and B alike. The checker can use it too: Band.depth() over independent draws has no whole curves to rank.

Correlated field draws, small multiples

Ten draws of a 20 by 20 scalar field with a planted elliptical region that rises and falls together across draws, shown as a 2 by 5 small multiple in viridis. Source: Zhao and Berger, "Evaluating judgements of spatial correlation in visual displays of scalar field distributions", VIS 2025 (arXiv 2507.17997).

Today

chart(fieldDraws, { color: Color.gradient("viridis"), w: 748, h: 292 })
  .flow(
    table({ by: { x: "col", y: "row" }, spacing: 12 }),
    table({ by: { x: "j", y: "i" }, spacing: 0 })
  )
  .mark(rect({ fill: "value" }))
  .render(container, { axes: false });

Precomputed by hand: the draws themselves (white noise smoothed by a Gaussian blur with sigma 1.5 cells, plus one shared normal offset per draw inside a soft-edged ellipse, which stands in for the paper's Gaussian process), and each draw's grid position (row, col). The legend's end labels show raw decimals; that is a legend formatting issue, not part of this example. The HOP version also works today: replace the outer table with time.sequence({ by: "draw" }) (not rendered here, since the capture is a still image).

Proposed (A)

const options = {
  schema: { value: { draws: "draw" } },
  color: Color.gradient("viridis"),
};

// small multiples: one panel per draw, five to a row
// new: single-key table with numCols in flow form
chart(fieldDraws, options)
  .flow(
    table({ by: "draw", numCols: 5, spacing: 12 }),
    table({ by: { x: "j", y: "i" } })
  )
  .mark(rect({ fill: "value" }));

// HOP: the same chart with the draw key on time instead of space
chart(fieldDraws, options)
  .flow(time.sequence({ by: "draw" }), table({ by: { x: "j", y: "i" } }))
  .mark(rect({ fill: "value" }));

schema = {"value": {"draws": "draw"}}
viridis = Color.gradient("viridis")

# small multiples
# new: single-key table with num_cols in flow form
chart(field_draws, schema=schema, color=viridis).flow(
    table(by="draw", num_cols=5, spacing=12), table(by={"x": "j", "y": "i"})
).mark(rect(fill="value"))

# HOP
chart(field_draws, schema=schema, color=viridis).flow(
    time.sequence(by="draw"), table(by={"x": "j", "y": "i"})
).mark(rect(fill="value"))

Proposed (B) is the same chart without the schema record, since the data is already draws.

New pieces needed

  • The record's draws key only, and here it changes nothing in the drawing: when the data is a long table of draws and the flow places the draw key, A and B are the same chart. The record matters when a signal mark consumes value, and for the checks.
  • A single-key table({ by, numCols }) in flow form is new (numCols: 5 for five panels a row). The first draft wrote spread({ wrap: 5 }). The noise map needs the same grid, and table already has numCols, so both use table. It is a layout option, not an uncertainty option. Today the grid position is data.
  • This example is the strongest case for shared draws. If each cell were an independent dist.normal and the chart called sample({ n: 10 }), the correlated region would vanish. sample keeps the draw ids of a draws column, so they stay joint (HasDraws { joint: true }), and the docs should say that sampling independent cells cannot show this.
  • Overlap: exact. Small multiples and HOPs are existing operators over the draw key. The design point is only that the draw key is an ordinary by key, so the choice of space or time (which the paper shows changes which judgment is easy) stays with the user.

Density slabs: shared scale vs per-curve rescaling

Three normal densities with standard deviations 0.6, 1 and 1.8, drawn with one shared density scale (equal area), and with each curve rescaled to the same peak height. Source: Fygenson and Padilla, "The Impact of Vertical Scaling on Normal Probability Density Function Plots", VIS 2024 (OSF). Rescaling makes the wide curve C look like it holds far more probability than it does.

Today

const densities = (rows) =>
  chart(rows, { w: 340, h: 200 })
    .flow(group({ by: "curve" }), scatter({ by: "x", x: "x" }))
    .mark(ribbon({ h: "density", fill: "curve", opacity: 0.45 }));
densities(equalArea).render(a, { axes: true });
densities(equalPeak).render(b, { axes: true });

Precomputed by hand: each density on a 241-point grid, and in panel (b) each curve multiplied by the tallest peak over its own peak.

Proposed (A)

// params: [{ curve, mean, sd }], one row per curve
const d = dist.normal({ mean: "mean", sd: "sd" });

// (a) equal area: one density scale for every slab (the default)
chart(params)
  .flow(group({ by: "curve" }))
  .mark(slab({ x: d, fill: "curve", opacity: 0.45, normalize: "all" }));

// (b) equal peak: each slab rescaled to the same height. The lint warns here.
chart(params)
  .flow(group({ by: "curve" }))
  .mark(slab({ x: d, fill: "curve", opacity: 0.45, normalize: "group" }));

d = dist.normal(mean="mean", sd="sd")

# (a) equal area
chart(params).flow(group(by="curve")).mark(
    slab(x=d, fill="curve", opacity=0.45, normalize="all")
)

# (b) equal peak (the lint warns)
chart(params).flow(group(by="curve")).mark(
    slab(x=d, fill="curve", opacity=0.45, normalize="group")
)

Proposed (B)

// B: the data is samples, and the slab is a density estimate over each group's rows.
chart(samples)
  .flow(group({ by: "curve" }))
  .mark(slab({ x: "value", fill: "curve", opacity: 0.45, normalize: "all" }));

The lint on panel (b), proposed text: "These slabs share a layout but use normalize: "group", so their heights do not compare. Readers misjudge which curve holds more probability in a range (Fygenson and Padilla 2024). Use normalize: "all", or split the slabs into panels."

New pieces needed

  • dist.normal, slab with normalize, and the lint.
  • Overlap: slab({ x: d }) with normalize: "all" desugars exactly into today's density grid + scatter({ by: "x", x: "x" }) + ribbon({ h }), because a shared h scale is what GoFish already does. normalize: "group" is a per-group rescale in data. So the slab mark itself adds the grid evaluation of the density and nothing else.
  • The lint is the new part. It needs the slab's thickness to carry a measure type, "density of x", so that a per-group rescale gives a different unit per group and the shared-axis check catches it. This ties to the measure types work (#1197, tracked in #1199).
  • In B the density is a kernel estimate from samples, so it needs a bandwidth. types.md puts it on the fold, not on slab: slab({ x: field("value").kde({ smoothing: 0.3 }) }). smoothing is Overlap.noise's word for the same bandwidth. A, with a known family, does not need one.

Timeline with uncertain start and end times

Seven activities on a day timeline whose start and end times are uncertain, drawn with fading ends (a) and with dashed ends over each end's 95% range (b). Source: Potter et al., "An Evaluation of Temporal and Categorical Uncertainty on Timelines", VIS 2025 short (OSF), which found transparency read more accurately than dashing. I could not open the paper's figures (the OSF page did not load as text), so the exact styling is my guess from the abstract.

Today

// (a) each bar cut into slices with a precomputed P(on), shaded white to blue
chart(activitySlices, {
  w: 420,
  h: 240,
  color: Color.gradient(["#ffffff", BAR]),
})
  .flow(spread({ by: ROWS, dir: "y", spacing: 8 }), group({ by: "slice" }))
  .mark(rect({ x: "x", x2: "x2", fill: "alpha" }))
  .render(a, { axes: true, legend: false });

// (b) thick lines: solid on the core, dashed across each end's 95% range
layer({ w: 420, h: 240 }, [
  chart(activityCores)
    .flow(
      spread({ by: ROWS, dir: "y", spacing: 34 }),
      scatter({ by: "x", x: "x" })
    )
    .mark(line({ along: "x", stroke: BAR, strokeWidth: 26 })),
  chart(activityEnds)
    .flow(
      spread({ by: ROWS, dir: "y", spacing: 34 }),
      group({ by: "end" }),
      scatter({ by: "x", x: "x" })
    )
    .mark(
      line({ along: "x", stroke: BAR, strokeWidth: 26, strokeDasharray: "3 3" })
    ),
]).render(b, { axes: true });

Precomputed by hand: each fading end cut into 12 slices, each with P(started) × P(not yet ended) at its middle, under normal start and end times; the solid core from start + 2 sd to end − 2 sd; and each end's 95% range. Two approximations: the fade is stepped, not a smooth gradient, and it is a white-to-blue color scale, not opacity. A rect's opacity is a constant, not a channel, so it cannot follow a column, and on a white page the color scale looks the same. Panel (b) draws bars as thick lines, because rect has no dash option. The two panels' rows do not line up exactly, and the last row in (b) sits on the axis.

Proposed (A)

// activities: [{ activity, start, startSd, end, endSd }]. start and end are
// Dist<Instant>; their sd columns are durations.
const start = dist.normal({ mean: "start", sd: "startSd" });
const end = dist.normal({ mean: "end", sd: "endSd" });

// (a) fade: the opacity at t is P(start <= t <= end)
// new: gradient with two Dist ends
chart(activities)
  .flow(spread({ by: ROWS, dir: "y" }))
  .mark(gradient({ x: start, x2: end, fill: BAR }));

// (a, noise form) the same fade from 50 overlaid draws
chart(activities)
  .flow(
    spread({ by: ROWS, dir: "y" }),
    sample({ n: 50 }),
    group({ by: "draw" })
  )
  .mark(rect({ x: start, x2: end, fill: BAR, opacity: field("draw").share() }));

// (b) dashed: a solid core, and a dashed box over each end's 95% interval
chart(activities)
  .flow(spread({ by: ROWS, dir: "y" }))
  .mark(
    layer([
      rect({ x: start.quantile(0.975), x2: end.quantile(0.025), fill: BAR }),
      // new: a dash option on rect
      rect({
        dims: { x: start.interval(0.95) },
        fill: "none",
        stroke: BAR,
        strokeDasharray: "3 3",
      }),
      rect({
        dims: { x: end.interval(0.95) },
        fill: "none",
        stroke: BAR,
        strokeDasharray: "3 3",
      }),
    ])
  );

start = dist.normal(mean="start", sd="startSd")
end = dist.normal(mean="end", sd="endSd")

# (a) fade
chart(activities).flow(spread(by=ROWS, dir="y")).mark(
    gradient(x=start, x2=end, fill=BAR)
)

# (a, noise form)
chart(activities).flow(spread(by=ROWS, dir="y"), sample(n=50), group(by="draw")).mark(
    rect(x=start, x2=end, fill=BAR, opacity=field("draw").share())
)

# (b) dashed
chart(activities).flow(spread(by=ROWS, dir="y")).mark(
    layer(
        [
            rect(x=start.quantile(0.975), x2=end.quantile(0.025), fill=BAR),
            # new: a dash option on rect
            rect(
                dims={"x": start.interval(0.95)},
                fill="none",
                stroke=BAR,
                stroke_dasharray="3 3",
            ),
            rect(
                dims={"x": end.interval(0.95)},
                fill="none",
                stroke=BAR,
                stroke_dasharray="3 3",
            ),
        ]
    )
)

Proposed (B)

// B: the data is draws, one row per (activity, draw), and the fade is a stat
// over each group's rows.
chart(activityDraws)
  .flow(spread({ by: ROWS, dir: "y" }))
  .mark(gradient({ x: "start", x2: "end", fill: BAR }));

New pieces needed

  • dist.normal over a time measure, which makes start a Dist<Instant> with a duration sd. The unit check should require sd to be a duration when mean is an instant. This touches the time datatype work (#256).
  • gradient with two Dist ends (x and x2). types.md keeps both forms: gradient takes rect's box channels, and any of them may hold a Dist. One Dist end follows its density. Two ends give P(start ≤ t ≤ end).
  • The noise form (sample + group + plain rect) gives the same fade from draws, and needs no new mark. Each draw is drawn at opacity: field("draw").share(), so 1/50. It shows that one opacity mechanism can serve both modes, as the notes on this paper suggest. Stacked alpha is not a linear sum (see noise.md), so its fade is close to P(start ≤ t ≤ end), not equal to it.
  • For (b): d.interval(0.95) fills a dims entry, the one span form the IR already has. rect({ x: d.interval(0.95) }) is an error, because x is one position. rect also needs strokeDasharray.
  • Overlap: gradient with two Dist ends desugars into today's slices with a computed color, up to the stepping. The lift form desugars into sample + group over existing marks. Both forms need a real opacity channel on rect (or a gradient fill) for a smooth fade.

Types and schema changes for distributions (#980)

This page lists the type and schema changes the proposed API needs. It is grounded in the code on main after #1200 and #1237. The TypeScript sketch is types-sketch.ts, beside this page. It imports the real descriptor DSL and IR types from gofish-ir, and it typechecks:

packages/gofish-graphics/node_modules/.bin/tsc --noEmit --strict --target es2022 \
  --moduleResolution bundler --module esnext --skipLibCheck --noUnusedLocals \
  proposals/uncertainty-specs/types-sketch.ts

It exits 0. It has six @ts-expect-error lines. With the directives removed, the same command reports exactly six errors, so each one is a real error.

The idea that keeps the change small

A group of rows is already a distribution: the empirical distribution of its values. Today's .mean() and .median() fold a group's rows, and that is the mean and median of that empirical distribution. Bin.quantiles cuts a column's empirical quantiles. So the change does not add a second world. It lets a cell hold a distribution that is not made of rows (a normal from two parameter columns), and it records when rows are draws.

Three rules follow, and they settle several spelling conflicts below.

  1. One fold. A distribution channel folds a group's rows into one Dist: the mixture of the rows' Dists, where a plain value is a Dist with all its mass at that value. So a plain column in a group gives its empirical Dist (Approach B), and one row with dist.normal gives that normal (Approach A). B is A with plain columns.
  2. One summary. .mean(), .median() and .quantile(p) summarize the folded Dist. On a plain column that is exactly what they do today. Design note question 6 (row folds versus cell summaries) needs no second name.
  3. Binning a Dist key truncates it. A cell of field(d).bin(Bin.quantiles(n)) holds the Dist cut to the cell. For a sample, that is the draws that fall in the cell, which is today's row split.

What exists today

  • Units and column classes. Unit(name?, { domain, traits }) (packages/gofish-graphics/src/ast/schema.ts:360). A unit stands for the column classes HasUnit, HasLevels, HasOrder and HasMidpoint (schema.ts:310). Domain.discrete and Domain.ordered are in src/families/domain.ts:58 and :65. Trait.summable() and Trait.diverging() are in src/families/trait.ts. CanAdd lives on the unit (schema.ts:152, HasUnit.classes).
  • Column records. A schema entry is { name?, unit? }, a Unit, or a unit's name (schema.ts:496 and :507). Unknown keys throw (schema.ts:532). The record of classes is the wire form (schemaToWire, schema.ts:559), and the types ride the data array under COLUMN_TYPES (schema.ts:641). A split leaf gets them with copyColumnTypes (src/ast/marks/createOperator.ts:1276).
  • The CanAdd check. checkSummable (src/ast/measure.ts:142) runs at every sum: a size channel (src/ast/channels.ts:213), .sum() (src/ast/fieldExpr.ts:460), treemap, and a stack's free parts through Units.sum (src/ast/constraints/distribute.ts:361). It reads the unit, never the rows.
  • Where a stack reads its key's type. spread's split already reads columnType(d, by) when glue is set, for HasMidpoint (src/ast/graphicalOperators/spread.tsx:253 to :259). A draw-key check goes there.
  • Field expressions. FieldOp (fieldExpr.ts:64) and its IR mirror FieldOpIR (packages/gofish-ir/src/frontend/schema.ts:961). field(name) takes one column name (src/ast/data.ts:198). A key over two columns is its own accessor, struct({ x, y }) (src/ast/structExpr.ts, IR StructAccessor at schema.ts:950).
  • Spans. A channel value is one value (ChannelValue, IR schema.ts:895). The one span form is a dims entry, an AxisInterval { min, center, max, size } (IR schema.ts:906). An untagged plain object is an interval (isAxisInterval, gofish-ir/src/frontend/validate.ts:587). scatter's yMin/yMax are two position channels (descriptors.ts:1596).
  • Channel kinds. ChannelInfer is size | pos | color | raw (descriptors.ts:48). It drives markChannels.generated.ts.
  • Strategy families. STRATEGIES (descriptors.ts:627) holds Tile, Overlap, Curve, Bin, Calendar, Shape, Place, each a StrategyFamily (descriptors.ts:544). Overlap.noise already spells a kernel bandwidth as smoothing: number | "silverman" (descriptors.ts:673).
  • Quantiles. Bin.quantiles reads HasDomain.sorted() (src/ast/cells.ts:619 and :636). quantileRows (src/ast/quantileRows.ts) is the declared shortcut that #1231 removes.
  • Python. FieldAccessor and field() are at packages/gofish-python/gofish/ast.py:3036 and :3245, Unit at ast.py:2578, and the marks and operators are generated into _generated.py.

The changes by layer

Value types (no code today)

Dist<T> is a type over the #1200 value types. Each summary asks for a capability of T:

Operation Needs from T In #1200 terms Result
quantile, median, cdf, interval an order HasUnit amounts, or HasLevels with HasOrder T, or an interval of T
mean averages HasUnit amounts (instants and °C too) T
sd differences HasUnit amounts T's difference unit (HasUnit.difference)
prob(level) discrete values HasLevels a share
density (slab, gradient) length HasUnit amounts density
sum over draws never none error

In the sketch the summaries take a this type, so the wrong T is a compile error:

mean(this: DistExpr<AffineValue, J>): ValueExpr<T>;
quantile(this: DistExpr<OrderedValue, J>, p: number): ValueExpr<T>;
sd<U extends UnitTag>(this: DistExpr<Amount<U>, J>): ValueExpr<Amount<DiffUnit<U>>>;

// @ts-expect-error .mean() on Dist<Ordinal>: an ordinal level has no average.
rating.mean();

A Dist is held at run time in one of six forms (DistRep in the sketch): pure, family (named parameters, which may be Dists, so nesting is free), sample (draws with ids and weights, and a shared key when joint), mixture, truncated and smoothed. Its type carries its dependence, independent or joint.

Data and schema

Additive. The column record gets two keys, and the column type gets two classes:

chart(draws, {
  schema: { temp: { unit: Celsius, draws: "draw", weight: "w" } },
});
// wire:
// temp: { HasUnit: {...}, HasSample: { draws: "draw", weight: "w" } }
// draw: { HasDraws: { joint: true } }
  • draws names the column that says which draw each row is. It is a fact about the table, so it sits in the record and not on the unit. This is the same reason #1198 puts entity in the record.
  • HasDraws { joint, n?, seed? } is on the draw-key column. The schema writes it from a record's draws. sample writes it with joint: false for dist.* cells. resample and dist.dirichlet write it with joint: true.
  • HasSample { draws, weight? } is on the value column.
  • RECORD_KEYS (schema.ts:499) grows to name, unit, draws, weight.

Internal change. HasDomain.sorted() becomes HasDomain.quantile(p). For a plain column it is the type-7 quantile of the sorted values, as now. For a Dist key it is the Dist's own quantile. quantileEdges then cuts a Dist with no new code, and quantileRows is deleted (#1231). No user writes HasDomain, so nothing public breaks.

DataIR does not change. A dist.* value is a field expression over plain columns, and draws are plain rows.

Field-expression IR

Additive. A new tagged accessor, beside FieldAccessor and StructAccessor, because a normal reads two columns:

interface DistAccessor {
  type: "dist";
  family: DistFamilyName; // normal, lognormal, student, beta, dirichlet, categorical
  params: Record<
    string,
    number | string | FieldAccessor | DistAccessor | DatumValue
  >;
  ops?: FieldOpIR2[];
}
type ChannelValue2 = ChannelValue | DistAccessor;

New ops, added to FieldOpIR and FieldOp: quantile { p }, cdf { at }, sd, prob { level }, kde { smoothing? }, and share on a draw key (the row's draw's share of the total weight, read by opacity). mean and median keep their wire form. .interval(p) adds no op: it returns a plain { min: q((1 − p) / 2), max: q((1 + p) / 2) }, which is an AxisInterval.

Construct descriptors

Additive. One new channel kind, dist, in ChannelInfer. It folds a group's rows into one Dist, as size sums and pos averages. Signal marks use it:

const distAxis = {
  x: chDist("The distribution, along x. Give x or y, not both."),
  y: chDist("The distribution, along y. Give x or y, not both."),
};
interval: { type: "interval", kind: "leaf-mark", include: [paint],
  fields: { ...distAxis, levels, along: {...}, band: { type: t.ref("Band") } } },

New operators: sample { n?, coverage?, at, seed? }, resample { n, seed? } and summarize { columns }. Draws in one frame use today's group. New leaf marks: slab { x | y, side, normalize }, interval { x | y, levels, along?, band? }, dots { x | y, n }, gradient (rect's box channels, each of which may be a Dist), cdf and ccdf. The full entries are in the sketch, in the shape of the OPERATORS and LEAF_MARKS tables.

Strategy families

Additive. Band joins STRATEGIES: Band.pointwise() (default) and Band.depth({ outliers }), with outliers a number (default 1.5) or false. The dist.* constructors get a table in the same StrategyFamily shape (DIST_FAMILIES), so the validator, the JSON Schema and the Python generator read it the way they read STRATEGIES. It is a separate table because a dist is a channel's value, not one choice for an option.

Python wire

Additive. Python gets a DistAccessor(dict) class beside FieldAccessor (ast.py:3036), the new methods on both, a dist module, and generated wrappers for the new marks and operators. The schema's new classes cross as they are, like HasUnit. sample and resample take a seed, and JS and Python must share one seeded generator so a story draws the same values on both sides (design note question 1). Python can send draws as rows with no new transport.

The checker

Four checks, each at a place that already reads column types:

Rule Where Kind
A Dist in a T channel with no lift inferNumeric, channels.ts:248 static
stack by a draw key spread's split, spread.tsx:259 static
A sum over several draws (size channel, .sum(), treemap) beside checkSummable, measure.ts:142 from the rows
A connecting mark across draws (no split by the draw key) the path-tier choice, classifyOperator, chartBuilder.ts:210 static
quantile on a nominal T, mean on an ordinal T evalFieldValues, fieldExpr.ts:488 static

The error texts are the design note's, word for word where the note has one. The fluent TS surface catches the first, second, fourth and fifth at compile time in the sketch. The engine runs all of them, for Python and for untyped columns:

chart([])
  .flow(spread({ by: "region", dir: "x" }))
  // @ts-expect-error rect h holds Dist<USD> and nothing draws it.
  .mark(rect({ h: sales }));
// @ts-expect-error stack by "draw" after sample: the sum of a sample's draws has no meaning.
chart([]).flow(sample({ n: 20 }), stack({ by: "draw", dir: "y" }));

The sum check over several draws is #1198's entity check with the draw key as the repeated key. They should be one function.

Spelling conflicts reconciled

  • .interval(p) as a span. Chosen: rect({ dims: { x: d.interval(0.95) } }) and scatter({ dims: { y: d.interval(0.95) } }). rect({ x: d.interval(p) }) is an error, because x is one position. The IR already has one span form, the dims interval, and a plain object of two quantiles is one. So the span needs no IR change. Signal's two .quantile(p) calls stay valid and mean the same.
  • field(d).quantile(p) on a Dist expression. Chosen: d.quantile(p). field() takes a column name. A dist.* value is already an expression.
  • rvar(field, { draw }) and B's interval({ draw }). Chosen: the column record, { draws: "draw", weight: "w" }. Which rows are one draw is a fact about the table, as #1198 argues for entity. With it, A and B need no per-mark draw option, and field("draw").share() reads the weight.
  • rvar(field) with no draw key. Dropped. A plain column in a Dist channel is the group's rows as draws, by the one-fold rule.
  • dist.kde(field). Chosen: field("temp").kde({ smoothing }). It folds a group's rows, like .mean(), while dist.* builds a Dist per row from parameters. smoothing is Overlap.noise's word for the same bandwidth (descriptors.ts:673). This also gives B's slab its bandwidth option.
  • gradient({ y: d }) and gradient({ x: start, x2: end }). Both. gradient takes rect's box channels, and any of them may hold a Dist. One Dist end follows its density. Two ends give P(start ≤ t ≤ end).
  • Overlay opacity. No overlay operator: group({ by: "draw" }) places the draws, and the mark sets the opacity. The specs write opacity: field("draw").share(), which is w / Σw, so 1/n for equal weights, as the sheet says. A per-line look such as Papers A's reads a column, opacity: "weight". Both need opacity to read a field. A default opacity under a draw key is the other option (proposal.md, decision 5).
  • sample() with no n. Kept: every stored draw of a draws column. For a dist.* cell, which stores none, it is an error.
  • B's sample({ value: dist.*, n, as }). Not needed. A's sample({ n }) with a dist.* channel works in both, by the one-fold rule.
  • sample({ at: "quantiles" }). Kept, as an enum beside "random". It has no parameters, so it is not a strategy family.
  • dist.normal over an Instant. Its sd is in the difference unit of mean's unit, so a duration. The sketch types it with DiffUnit<U>.
  • scale: "probit" on ccdf. Not a mark option. A scale acts on every mark on its axis (design note section 4), so it belongs to the axis. GoFish has no scale family yet. That is a separate issue.

Not types, but met along the way: field(x).plus(1) (papers A), rule as a reference-line mark, strokeDasharray on rect, spread({ wrap }), an opacity channel on line, and a group over two keys.

Additive or breaking

Everything is additive for users. Two changes are internal: HasDomain.sorted() becomes quantile(p), and quantileRows is deleted (#1231 asks for that). mean and median change meaning only on Dist inputs, which do not exist today.

Open decisions

  1. Should the draw key live in the column record ({ draws }, this page's pick) or in a channel expression (rvar, the sheet's spelling)?
  2. Should the namespace be dist.normal (like time) or Dist.normal (CLAUDE.md capitalizes factory namespaces, and Dist.Dist would be the type, like Curve.Curve)?
  3. Should a mixed chart (a dist.* cell and a joint draws column) treat the two as independent, or is it an error?
  4. Should a group with several dist.* rows fold to their mixture, or should a signal mark draw one shape per row, as ggdist does?
  5. Is the draw-sum check the same function as #1198's entity check, so #1198 lands first?
  6. Should Python send dist.* parameters only, or also cells of SciPy distributions as an Arrow struct column (a HasDist class)? This page leaves cells out.
  7. Is the probability bar prob() per level a flow step (one row per level, like sample({ at: "quantiles" })), or a mark?
  8. What sets the axis extent of an unbounded Dist: a quantile range, and which?

What slab, interval and dots elaborate into (#980)

This page defines the three signal marks as code. Each definition is real TypeScript that runs today, in packages/gofish-graphics/stories/proposals/uncertainty/elaborations.ts. It uses only marks and operators that GoFish exports now. The story file ElaborationsCheck.stories.tsx beside it draws the prototypes with these definitions, from normal and lognormal parameters. No grid, quantile or interval is precomputed.

How a signal mark is written

In the library, each mark would be one line:

export const slab = createMark(slabBody, { x: "dist", y: "dist" }, "slab");

Here "dist" is the proposed channel kind. It folds a group's rows into one Dist before the body runs, as size sums and pos averages. A story cannot add a channel kind, and createMark gives its body the inferred channels, not the rows. So each mark here is a plain Mark<T[]>, which is what a createMark mark is underneath. It takes the group's rows and calls foldDist itself. That is the same work the dist channel would do.

Dist does not exist yet, so the file has a small stand-in:

export interface Dist {
  quantile(p: number): number;
  pdf(x: number): number;
  cdf(x: number): number;
  support(): [number, number];
}

It has one more method than the brief's minimal interface. The quantile of a mixture has no closed form, so it is found by inverting the mixture's CDF. support() is a display range. A normal's true support is the whole line, so normal returns mean ± 4 sd. This is types.md's open decision 8.

The constructors are normal(mean, sd), lognormal(meanlog, sdlog), empirical(values, weights?, smoothing?), mixture(dists, weights?) and pure(x). empirical has the sample's own quantiles (type 7) and CDF, and a Gaussian KDE for its density. Its bandwidth is Silverman's rule, 1.06 · sd · n^(−1/5), as in Croissant.stories.tsx. A dist stand-in namespace builds one Dist per row from parameter columns: dist.normal({ mean: "mean", sd: "sd" }).

The one-fold rule from types.md is foldDist:

export function foldDist<T>(
  channel: DistChannel<T>,
  rows: T[],
  smoothing: Smoothing = "silverman"
): Dist {
  if (isDist(channel)) return channel;
  const cells = rows.map((r) =>
    typeof channel === "string" ? (r as any)[channel] : channel(r)
  ) as (Dist | number)[];
  if (cells.every((c) => typeof c === "number"))
    return empirical(cells as number[], undefined, smoothing);
  return mixture(cells.map((c) => (isDist(c) ? c : pure(c))));
}

Rows with Dist cells fold to their mixture, with equal weights. One row gives its own Dist. Plain values fold to their empirical Dist. A mix of both is a mixture in which a plain value is pure of itself. A pure value has no density, so a slab of such a mix draws only the Dist cells.

Slab

Definition

Let D be the group's folded Dist, f its density, v the value axis and s the cross axis. The slab is the region

  • side end: { (v, s) : 0 ≤ s ≤ f(v) / Z },
  • side start: { (v, s) : −f(v) / Z ≤ s ≤ 0 },
  • side both: { (v, s) : |s| ≤ f(v) / Z },

for v in D's display range. For normalize: "all", Z is 1, so every slab is in density units on one size scale. For normalize: "group", Z is the group's peak max f(v), so every slab's peak is 1. The chart's size scale then maps the largest s to the space the slab is given.

The region, the side rule and what each normalize divides by are decided semantics. These are defaults: the grid of k = 121 even points over support(), the display range of an unbounded Dist, the Silverman bandwidth, and the curve through the grid (ribbon's automatic monotone curve).

Code

export const slab = <T>(o: SlabOptions<T>): Mark<T[]> =>
  signalMark((rows: T[]) => {
    const { axis, cross, channel } = distAxis(o, "slab");
    const side = o.side ?? "end";
    const k = o.k ?? 121;
    const grid = (rows: T[]) => {
      const d = foldDist(channel, rows, o.smoothing);
      const [lo, hi] = d.support();
      const pts = Array.from(
        { length: k },
        (_, i) => lo + ((hi - lo) * i) / (k - 1)
      );
      const Z =
        (o.normalize ?? "all") === "group"
          ? Math.max(...pts.map((p) => d.pdf(p)))
          : 1;
      return pts.map((p) => ({ v: p, s: d.pdf(p) / Z, neg: -d.pdf(p) / Z }));
    };
    const size = cross === "y" ? "h" : "w";
    const paint = { fill: o.fill, stroke: o.stroke, opacity: o.opacity };
    const half = (sign: 1 | -1) =>
      chart(rows)
        .flow(derive(grid), scatter({ [axis]: "v" }))
        .mark(ribbon({ ...paint, dir: axis, [size]: sign > 0 ? "s" : "neg" }));
    if (side === "end") return half(1);
    if (side === "start") return half(-1);
    return layer([half(1), half(-1)]);
  });

signalMark only widens the declared type. Mark<T> says a mark returns a node or a chart builder, but the runtime also takes a LayerBuilder and a combinator mark such as layer([...]).

Elaborates into

A slab is a derive of k grid rows { v, s } for one group, a scatter along v, and a ribbon through anchors of size s. That is side end. Side start is the same ribbon with size −s. It is a signed extent (#773), so it hangs from the line. Side both is a layer of the two halves. A slab along y swaps the axes: the scatter places by y, the ribbon runs along y, and its anchors have width s.

Render

The elaboration:

The prototype, with its precomputed grid:

The two are the same picture. The check story SlabOptions draws side both with normalize: "group" (light) and side start (dark) on two groups:

Design questions

  • Where normalize: "all" gets Z. "All" needs one value shared by every slab, which is outside each group. This definition gets it for free. The heights are raw densities, and the chart's one size scale maps them all, so the tallest slab fills its space and the rest are in proportion. This is how the prototype worked too. The catch is that the scale is shared with every other size channel on that axis. So "all" means "every mark on this scale", not "every slab". A chart that puts a bar and a slab on one size scale would mix their units. A unit check (density is a unit, #1200) would catch that.
  • The line the slab sits on. The line s = 0 is the slab's own origin. Two slabs in one layer share it, so a violin and a start half line up, as the check story shows. An interval beside a start or both slab does not. The layer puts the interval's box at the layer's bottom, not at the slab's origin. A literal cy: 0 on the interval did not pin it there either. So the half-eye works only because side end's origin is its bottom. This could not be expressed with today's layout. It needs a layer that aligns its children by their origins, which is a layout question and not a slab question.
  • Grid size k. 121 points look smooth at chart sizes. A narrow peak in a wide range can fall between points. An adaptive grid (denser where f changes fast) is a default to decide, not a semantic.
  • The display range. Each slab draws over its own support(). For a normal that is mean ± 4 sd. The axis extent of an unbounded Dist is still open (types.md decision 8).
  • Horizontal against vertical. The dist channel's name picks the value axis, and everything else swaps. Nothing else needs a flag.

Interval

Definition

Let D be the group's folded Dist, Q its quantile function, and p₁ > p₂ > … > pₙ the levels, widest first. The point form is

  • for each level pᵢ, the segment [Q((1 − pᵢ)/2), Q((1 + pᵢ)/2)] on the value axis, with cross-axis thickness tᵢ, where t₁ < t₂ < … < tₙ;
  • the point Q(0.5).

With along: a and Band.pointwise(), let D_a be the Dist of the rows at step a of the tier. For each level p, the band is the region between the curves a ↦ Q_a((1 − p)/2) and a ↦ Q_a((1 + p)/2). The median is the curve a ↦ Q_a(0.5).

Band.depth({ outliers }) is defined here but not implemented. It needs joint draws c₁ … c_m, each a whole curve over the tier. The modified band depth of a curve c is the mean, over pairs j < k, of the share of steps a where min(c_j(a), c_k(a)) ≤ c(a) ≤ max(c_j(a), c_k(a)). The band of level p is the pointwise envelope of the ⌈p · m⌉ deepest curves. The median is the deepest curve. A curve is an outlier when it leaves the 0.5 envelope, widened by outliers times its width, at some step. It would elaborate into the same ribbons plus one line per outlier curve. Only the derive changes.

Equal-tailed central intervals, the median as the point, thickness that decreases in p, and pointwise bands by default are decided semantics. These are defaults: levels [0.66, 0.95], thickness from 1.5 to 4 px, a 4 px point, and in the band form an opacity of 1 − 0.3^(1/n) per band. With that opacity, the innermost of n nested bands reaches 0.7 where they overlap.

Code

const ladder = (n: number, i: number, thin = 1.5, thick = 4) =>
  n === 1 ? thick : thin + ((thick - thin) * i) / (n - 1);

const central = (d: Dist, p: number) => ({
  lo: d.quantile((1 - p) / 2),
  hi: d.quantile((1 + p) / 2),
});

export const interval = <T>(o: IntervalOptions<T>): Mark<T[]> =>
  signalMark((rows: T[]) => {
    const { axis, cross, channel } = distAxis(o, "interval");
    const levels = [...(o.levels ?? [0.66, 0.95])].sort((a, b) => b - a);
    const fill = o.fill ?? "#1f3b5c";
    if (o.along !== undefined) return intervalAlong(o, rows, levels, fill);
    const d = foldDist(channel, rows, o.smoothing);
    const thick = cross === "y" ? "h" : "w";
    return layer([
      ...levels.map((p, i) => {
        const { lo, hi } = central(d, p);
        return rect({
          [axis]: v(lo),
          [`${axis}2`]: v(hi),
          [thick]: ladder(levels.length, i),
          fill,
        });
      }),
      circle({ [`c${axis}`]: v(d.quantile(0.5)), r: o.r ?? 4, fill }),
    ]);
  });

const intervalAlong = <T>(
  o: IntervalOptions<T>,
  rows: T[],
  levels: number[],
  fill: string
) => {
  const { axis, cross, channel } = distAxis(o, "interval");
  const along = o.along!;
  const steps = (rows: T[]) => {
    const keys = [...new Set(rows.map((r) => (r as any)[along]))];
    return keys.map((key) => ({
      key,
      d: foldDist(
        channel,
        rows.filter((r) => (r as any)[along] === key),
        o.smoothing
      ),
    }));
  };
  const bands = (rows: T[]) =>
    levels.flatMap((p) =>
      steps(rows).map(({ key, d }) => ({
        [along]: key,
        level: p,
        ...central(d, p),
      }))
    );
  const medians = (rows: T[]) =>
    steps(rows).map(({ key, d }) => ({
      [along]: key,
      median: d.quantile(0.5),
    }));
  const opacity = o.opacity ?? 1 - 0.3 ** (1 / levels.length);
  const thin = cross === "x" ? "w" : "h";
  return layer([
    chart(rows)
      .flow(
        derive(bands),
        group({ by: "level" }),
        scatter({
          by: along,
          [cross]: along,
          [`${axis}Min`]: "lo",
          [`${axis}Max`]: "hi",
        })
      )
      .mark(rect({ [thin]: 0 }))
      .layer(ribbon({ fill, opacity, dir: cross })),
    chart(rows)
      .flow(
        derive(medians),
        scatter({ by: along, [cross]: along, [axis]: "median" })
      )
      .mark(
        line({ stroke: o.stroke ?? "#1f3b5c", strokeWidth: 2, dir: cross })
      ),
  ]);
};

Elaborates into

The point form is a layer of one rect per level, from v(lo) to v(hi) with a fixed thickness, and a circle at v(median). The band form is a layer of two charts, each over a derive that folds every step. The first has one rect of width 0 per step and level, joined by a ribbon per level (group by level). The second is a line through the medians. So along turns an interval into a ribbon per level and a median line, the same marks the fan-chart prototype drew by hand.

Render

Point interval, the elaboration:

The prototype:

They are the same picture.

Fan chart, the elaboration:

The prototype:

The bands are the same. The prototype colors each level from a palette and has a legend. The elaboration uses one fill and nested opacity, so it has no legend.

Design questions

  • along needs every step. The band form reads all the steps of the tier, so it cannot be a mark that sees one group. In the proposal it sits after the user's scatter({ by: "t" }), as line and ribbon do. Those are relational marks. The chart builder rewrites .mark(R) into .mark(blank).layer(R) (tagRelationalFusable, which is internal). That rewrite makes one blank per group, but a band needs one anchor per step and level. So createRelationalMark, which is exported, cannot express it either. Here the band form is a mark at the root, over all rows, and it splits by along itself. The story writes chart(forecast).mark(interval({ y, along: "t", ... })), with no scatter in the flow. The proposal's spelling, with the scatter, needs a chart-level rewrite that the exported API does not offer.
  • Band.depth needs the draw key. Depth ranks whole curves, so it needs the joint draw id across every step. That is outside every step's group. It reads the draws record from types.md.
  • The thickness ladder. 1.5 to 4 px, linear in the level's rank, is ggdist's look in pixels. It is a default. A thickness in data units would need the cross-axis scale.
  • Band color. Nested opacity needs no scale and no legend. A palette by level (the prototype) needs a color scale and gives a legend. Which one is the default is open.
  • Horizontal against vertical. As with the slab, the channel's name picks the value axis. In the band form, along is placed on the other axis.

Dots

Definition

Let D be the group's folded Dist and Q its quantile function. The dots are the n quantiles qᵢ = Q((i − 0.5)/n), for i = 1 … n. Each dot is a probability of 1/n. The dots are binned on the value axis with bin width w and stacked on the cross axis from 0, one dot per quantile.

The binning is Wilkinson's dot-density rule (1999) by default. A bin starts at the smallest quantile not yet binned and takes every quantile below its start plus w. Its stack sits at the midpoint of its first and last quantile. binning: { kind: "fixed" } is the prototype's rule instead: bins [kw, (k + 1)w), each stack at the bin's center.

The n quantiles at (i − 0.5)/n and a stack that counts quantiles are decided semantics. These are defaults: n = 20, Wilkinson binning, the bin width w and the 9 px radius.

Code

const binQuantiles = (qs: number[], kind: "wilkinson" | "fixed", w: number) => {
  if (kind === "fixed")
    return qs.map((q) => {
      const b = Math.floor(q / w);
      return { bin: b, center: (b + 0.5) * w };
    });
  const out: { bin: number; center: number }[] = [];
  let b = 0;
  for (let i = 0; i < qs.length; b++) {
    let j = i;
    while (j + 1 < qs.length && qs[j + 1] < qs[i] + w) j++;
    for (let m = i; m <= j; m++)
      out.push({ bin: b, center: (qs[i] + qs[j]) / 2 });
    i = j + 1;
  }
  return out;
};

export const dots = <T>(o: DotsOptions<T>): Mark<T[]> =>
  signalMark((rows: T[]) => {
    const { axis, cross, channel } = distAxis(o, "dots");
    const n = o.n ?? 20;
    const kind = o.binning?.kind ?? "wilkinson";
    const quantileRows = (rows: T[]) => {
      const d = foldDist(channel, rows, o.smoothing);
      const qs = Array.from({ length: n }, (_, i) => d.quantile((i + 0.5) / n));
      const w = o.binning?.width ?? (qs[n - 1] - qs[0]) / Math.ceil(n / 2);
      return binQuantiles(qs, kind, w).map((b, i) => ({ ...b, q: qs[i] }));
    };
    return chart(rows)
      .flow(
        derive(quantileRows),
        scatter({ by: "bin", [axis]: "center", alignment: "end" }),
        stack({ dir: cross })
      )
      .mark(
        circle({
          r: o.r ?? 9,
          fill: o.fill,
          stroke: o.stroke,
          strokeWidth: o.strokeWidth ?? 1,
          opacity: o.opacity,
        })
      );
  });

Elaborates into

Dots are a derive of n rows { bin, center }, a scatter by bin at the bin's center, aligned so the stacks rise from the baseline, and a stack of circles along the cross axis. This is sample({ n, at: "quantiles" }) from the proposal, followed by the prototype's scatter and stack.

Render

The elaboration, with Wilkinson binning and w = 1 minute:

The prototype, with fixed 1-minute bins:

The quantiles are the same. The stacks differ because the binning differs. Wilkinson's bins start at a quantile, so they follow the data. Fixed bins start at multiples of w, so they follow the axis.

Design questions

  • The bin width needs the scale. A dotplot is only right when a dot's diameter equals the bin width on screen: w times the pixels per unit equals 2r. The pixels per unit come from the value axis's scale, which belongs to the whole chart and is outside the group. This definition takes w (in data units) and r (in pixels) as two options, and the author must match them. The story sets w = 1 and r = 9 by hand, as the prototype did. The right default picks w from the scale, as ggdist's automatic bin width does. No mark body gets layout information like that today. The placeholder default is w = (q_n − q_1) / ⌈n / 2⌉.
  • Which binning. Wilkinson's rule is the dot-density standard. Fixed bins line up across groups, which helps when several dotplots share an axis. The descriptor in types-sketch.ts has only n, so binning is a new option to decide.
  • No gap in a stack. stack drops spacing, so the prototype's spacing: 1 did nothing. Dots touch. A gap would need spread with a spacing, which is not glued.
  • Horizontal against vertical. With y, the bins are on y and the stacks grow along x.

How they compose

A half-eye is a layer of a slab and an interval. This is the HalfEye check story:

const value = dist.normal({ mean: "mean", sd: "sd" });

chart(halfEyeGroups, { w: 520, axes: { x: { title: "value" }, y: true } })
  .flow(spread({ by: "group", dir: "y", anchor: "baseline", spacing: 64 }))
  .mark(
    layer({ h: 60 }, [
      slab({ x: value, fill: SLAB }),
      interval({ x: value, levels: [0.66, 0.95], fill: INK }),
    ])
  );

A raincloud is a layer of a slab, an interval and a jittered scatter of the raw rows. The column is plain, so the slab and the interval fold each species' rows to their empirical Dist, and the slab smooths it with the Silverman bandwidth:

chart(penguins, { w: 560, axes: { x: { title: "Body mass (g)" }, y: true } })
  .flow(spread({ by: "Species", dir: "y", spacing: 20 }))
  .mark(
    layer([
      slab({ x: "Body Mass (g)", fill: SLAB, opacity: 0.7 }),
      interval({ x: "Body Mass (g)", levels: [0.66, 0.95], fill: INK }),
      chart()
        .flow(
          scatter({ x: "Body Mass (g)", overlap: Overlap.jitter() }).translate({
            y: -16,
          })
        )
        .mark(circle({ r: 1.5, fill: "Species", opacity: 0.6 })),
    ])
  );

The raincloud was not rendered for this page. Its rain sits below the line, so it meets the same question as side start: the layer has to place the rain relative to the slab's origin.

Summary of what needed information outside the group

Piece What it needs How this page handles it
slab normalize: "all" one Z for every slab the chart's shared size scale, for free
slab side start and both the group's line, shared with others not expressed: the layer aligns boxes, not origins
interval along every step of the tier a mark at the root that splits by along
interval Band.depth the joint draw key across steps defined in comments, not implemented
dots bin width the value axis's pixels per unit two options the author matches by hand

Uncertainty visualization literature triage: 2024

Scope: the IEEE VIS 2024 Workshop on Uncertainty Visualization, plus uncertainty papers in the IEEE VIS 2024 main conference (full papers, short papers, TVCG and CG&A presentations). Judged against GoFish issue #980 (Dist<T>) and the design note apps/docs/docs/internals/design/distributions.md.

1. Coverage

Sources I got.

  • Workshop accepted-papers page: https://tusharathawale.github.io/UncertaintyVis-Workshop/accepted-papers.html. The 2026-style URL (uncertainty-vis-workshop-2024/) returns 404. The 2024 site is the older UncertaintyVis-Workshop/ path, which the 2026 site links from its archive. It lists 12 items: 5 long papers, 6 short papers, 1 poster.
  • Workshop session on the VIS program: https://www.ieeevis.org/year/2024/program/session_workshop5.html.
  • The full VIS 2024 program as JSON: https://ieeevis.org/year/2024/program/papers.json. It has 436 entries: 201 full (197 VIS full, 12 CG&A, the rest TVCG), 66 short, 114 workshop and 55 associated-event papers. All 12 workshop papers (w-uncertainty) are in it with abstracts and preprint links. I keyword-filtered every full and short abstract for uncertainty, distribution, ensemble, interval, Bayesian, quantile, forecast, variance and so on (about 65 hits). Then I read the abstracts of the real candidates by hand, and checked the session lists for "Flow, Topology, and Uncertainty", "Judgment and Decision-making", "Perception and Cognition", "Model-checking and Validation" and "Short Papers: Perception and Representation".
  • Full text read: VMC (arXiv 2408.16702, sections 4.1 to 4.4); Fygenson & Padilla on PDF vertical scaling (OSF preprint, introduction, background and the results summary); Tominski et al. on uncertainty in sets (arXiv 2302.11575, framework section). For UADAPy I read the README and the Distribution class source on GitHub. For UASTL I read the README of the authors' code repo (kraketm/UASTL).

What I could not access.

  • UASTL full text (Krake et al., TVCG, doi 10.1109/TVCG.2024.3364388). There is no arXiv copy. Semantic Scholar lists the IEEE PDF as CC-BY open access, but IEEE refused my fetch tools (HTTP 418). My notes on it rest on the abstract and the code README. It is the paper most worth reading in full by hand.
  • Workshop papers with no preprint (Accelerated Surface Boxplots, FunM²C, Entropy-Based Test Framework, Wildfire Ensembles, Decision Systems with Surrogates, UADAPy's 3-page paper). I read abstracts only.
  • Everything rated Low or None: abstract only.

3. Deep notes

M1. VMC: A Grammar for Visualizing Statistical Model Checks (High; full text read)

What is new. VMC is a declarative grammar (an R package) whose spec is a tuple: Sampling_spec × Data_transformation × Visual_representation × Comparative_layout.

  • Sampling_spec = (Quantity, Predictor_values, Ndraws). The number of draws belongs to the sampling step, not to the mark.
  • The sample data type is <Predictors, Response_var, Draw_id>[]. Each draw is a full replicate of the dataset (a posterior-predictive draw), so draws are shared across rows by construction.
  • Visual_representation is a list of (Target, Mark, Group_samples?). Target is model or data, so one chart lists separate representations for the model draws and the observed data.
  • Marks fall into three classes by how they show a distribution: EXTENT (densityline, slab, violin, histogram, interval), VISUAL VARIABLE (gradient, heatmap) and COUNTABLE (quantile dots, line, point).
  • Group_samples is one of four options. Write q⁽ⁱ⁾ for draw i over all n rows, with r draws in total.
    • collapsing: one mark takes all of q⁽¹⁾ … q⁽ʳ⁾ (a line plus ribbon over all draws).
    • individualizing: r marks, each taking one q⁽ⁱ⁾, overlaid in one coordinate system (the spaghetti of posterior-predictive densities).
    • animating: r marks, one per time frame (HOPs).
    • aggregating: apply a statistic π: ℝⁿ → ℝ to each draw, then one mark shows π(q⁽¹⁾) … π(q⁽ʳ⁾) (for example a histogram of per-draw means).
  • Comparative_layout is superposition, juxtaposition, nested juxtaposition (side by side inside one coordinate system) or explicit encoding (residual, Q-Q, detrended Q-Q, or a custom (Samples, Data) → Samples function). The authors stress that making these one type departs from the grammar of graphics.

How it bears on #980.

  1. It refines the noise/signal split into four cases, and the fourth is missing from the design note. In GoFish terms: collapsing is "signal" (consume, section 5b); individualizing is "noise" with the z (overlay) direction; animating is "noise" with the time direction. Aggregating is neither. It is a map of a row fold over draws (Dist<Table> → Dist<T> by the functor), followed by consuming the resulting Dist<T>. The design note covers a row fold (field("y").mean()) and a cell summary (section 5c, open question 6), but not "fold rows inside each draw, then show the distribution of the fold". VMC shows this is a common, named model-check pattern (histogram of replicated means). Suggestion: add "aggregate per draw" as a third use beside lift and consume. Its typing is clean: it needs shared draws (a draw is a whole replicate), because folding rows within draw i means nothing under independent cells. That gives one more case where the dependence model is decided by the operation, which supports open question 2.
  2. Shared draws as the default for model output. VMC's sample type is exactly rvar's layout (Draw_id on every row of a replicate). For posterior-predictive and forecast-ensemble data (the main #980 use case besides "a cell with an error"), the natural input is a long table with a draw column, and the shared-draws model follows. Suggestion: treat "long table with a declared draw key" as the shared-draws constructor and "a column of per-cell distribution objects" as the independent-cells constructor. Two input shapes, two dependence models, no default to guess.
  3. The direction choice is per representation, not per chart. In one VMC chart the model can be collapsing while the data is not grouped. The design note's draw direction is a property of the operator that consumes the draw key. VMC agrees in effect, and adds that layering a collapsed slab plus a collapsed interval plus raw data dots (a raincloud) is a list of representations of the same draws. In GoFish this is layer over marks that share one Dist field, which needs no new primitive.
  4. Comparative layout. "Nested juxtaposition" is GoFish's x-dodge of model vs data inside one slot. "Explicit encoding" (residual, Q-Q) is a derive that subtracts or pairs quantiles. VMC shows these are a menu over one source and target pair. Nothing new to build, but the four layouts make a good story set for #980.
  5. Ndraws. VMC puts the draw count in the sampling spec. That answers part of open question 10: n is an argument of the sampling step (sample(n)), not a chart option.

Contradiction or refinement. VMC does not forbid stacking draws, but it has no "stack" grouping option either. Its four options match ggdibbler's directions except that ggdibbler's x (dodge) is VMC's nested juxtaposition (model vs data), not draws side by side. VMC never dodges draws against each other. That is weak extra evidence that dodging draws is rare in practice.

M2. The Impact of Vertical Scaling on Normal PDF Plots (High; preprint read in part)

What is new. Two preregistered experiments (n=600 and n=401) show that when two normal density curves are drawn with different vertical scalings (100%, 75% or 50% of equal-area height), readers misjudge which one puts more probability in a range. Readers tend to compare height or total area. Keeping the vertical scale consistent, which means equal pixel area under every curve, gives the most accurate comparisons. Adding a y-axis helps in some contexts, while SD annotations did not help in the same way. The background section notes that for ridgeline plots, unequal scaling is "canonically (and mathematically) incorrect".

How it bears on GoFish.

  • #980 slab marks. A density slab's height is a density measure (probability per x-unit). Two slabs side by side are comparable only if they share that measure's scale. This is the same rule GoFish already enforces for a shared y-axis, applied to the slab thickness channel. ggdist's normalize option defaults to "all" (one scale), with "panels", "xy", "groups" and "none" as alternatives. This paper is evidence for making the shared scale the default and per-group normalization the explicit opt-out.
  • #1091 lints. A lint "slabs that are juxtaposed or stacked in one layout use per-group normalized density" is checkable from types if the slab thickness carries the "density of field X" measure. Two slabs with different normalizations of the same measure have different units, which is the kind of disagreement the units-as-types rule already turns into a type error.
  • #971 violin and ridgelines. The same rule applies to violins and ridgelines. Ridgeline overlap is a layout choice; per-row rescaling is a scale choice. Keep them separate options.

M3. UASTL: Uncertainty-Aware Seasonal-Trend Decomposition Based on Loess (Medium; would be High; abstract and code README only)

What is new (from the abstract and README). The input is a discrete Gaussian process: a time series as one multivariate Gaussian with a full covariance matrix. STL is a composition of loess smoothers and differences. The README computes an explicit "loess matrix" (loessmtx.m), so the whole decomposition is a linear map. A multivariate Gaussian pushed through a linear map stays Gaussian in closed form (x ~ N(μ, Σ) gives Ax ~ N(Aμ, AΣAᵀ)). The output is one "global Gaussian distributed variable" holding the trend, seasonal and residual components and the data, with a global covariance. The abstract says this keeps the stochastic quantities that the components share, gives "STL-consistent samples", and supports exploring correlation between and within components.

How it bears on GoFish.

  • A third representation besides samples and quantiles. The design note follows ggdibbler in accepting samples only. UASTL shows the case where a parametric joint (mean vector plus covariance) is better than samples: it is exact, it is small for moderate n, and it keeps correlation (the shared-draws property) without storing draws. Draws are still available by sampling the joint. This is TFP's event shape in practice. Suggestion: the representation menu for open question 1 should be {samples with a draw key, independent per-cell parametric, joint Gaussian over a set of cells}. The third one crosses the Python bridge as one mean column plus one covariance (or a low-rank factor).
  • #635 data-space smoothing. Loess is a linear smoother ŷ = Ly. For y with covariance Σ, the band is diag(LΣLᵀ) exactly, with no bootstrap. If GoFish's loess is written as a linear operator, then Dist-in, Dist-out loess is a one-line extension, and the confidence band in #635 becomes the consume step of that output.
  • Shared draws. The joint covariance is the correlation that a connecting mark needs. "Ensemble lines need shared draws" (design note) holds for a joint Gaussian too: sample paths of the joint are valid lines.
  • Caveat. I could not read the paper's visualization section, so I do not know their specific technique for "highly correlated components". Read it before citing details.

W12. UADAPy (Medium; README and source read)

What is new. A Python Distribution class wraps either a scipy.stats object or a NumPy array of samples. Given samples, it builds a Gaussian KDE and uses it for pdf and sample. Passing name="Normal" fits a multivariate normal to the samples. It exposes sample(n, seed), pdf, cdf (not for sample-based distributions), mean and cov, and is multivariate (n_dims). A TimeSeries class builds on it. The package's distinctive part is operators that take distributions and return distributions: UAPCA (projects Gaussians), a GMM variant for non-Gaussians, UAMDS and UASTL.

How it bears on GoFish.

  • It is the closest Python precedent for what a GoFish Python user will hand over. Its constructor dispatches on input shape (scipy object or sample array), which matches the design note's plan. Note what it does not have: no draw key, so no shared draws across separate Distribution objects. Each object is internally multivariate (a joint within one object) and independent from the others. That is a fourth dependence pattern: "joint within a cell group, independent across groups". It is the TFP batch and event split again.
  • Uncertainty-propagating operators (PCA, MDS, STL) are a class GoFish will meet once derive accepts Dist columns. A linear derive can stay closed-form on Gaussians; a nonlinear one must fall back to samples. A capability for this would be "Linear op on Gaussian stays Gaussian".

M4. Visualizing Uncertainty in Sets (Medium; framework section read)

What is new. A framework crossing set data facets (set membership, set attributes, element attributes) with three levels of uncertainty: certain (U = 0), undefined uncertainty as a binary fact (U > 0: we know a value is uncertain but not how much), and defined uncertainty as a quantifiable measure (U = p, for example a membership probability or a distribution). It then walks through how implicit membership (inclusion in a region) and explicit membership (links, matrix cells) can be varied to show each level. Fuzziness, location, color value and arrangement are the visual variables it draws on (from MacEachren et al.).

How it bears on GoFish.

  • Dist<T> covers U = 0 (the point mass) and U = p. It has no value for U > 0, "flagged as uncertain, unquantified". Real data has this a lot (estimated, imputed, provisional, "preliminary" flags). Suggestion: decide whether this is in scope. If so, it is a separate small type (Flagged<T> or a boolean "uncertain" trait on a cell), and its only operations are "show the value" and "mark it as uncertain". It should not be modeled as a Dist with unknown spread, since there is no spread to draw.
  • Open question 8 (uncertain categories). Uncertain set membership is Dist<Bool> per element per set, or Dist<Category> when sets partition. The paper's examples (fuzzy region boundaries, line-style or opacity on links) are more options for the "signal" side of a Dist<Category> key, beyond probability bars. They also show the implicit case, where the uncertain thing is containment in an enclosure. That ties to GoFish's enclose and the bubble-set work: an enclose over a group whose membership is uncertain needs a soft boundary, which is a new requirement there.

M6. The Backstory to "Swaying the Public" (Medium; abstract only)

A design space for two-party election forecasts along data transformations, visual channels and animated narratives, with ten prototypes evaluated qualitatively (13 participants) and six insights on how the public reads them. Use for GoFish: a source of hard real-world examples for #980 stories (two-candidate win probability, vote-share distributions, animated draws). I have not read the design-space table, so I cannot list its dimensions in detail.

M7. Inclusion Depth for Contour Ensembles (Medium; abstract only), with W5 and W11

What is new. Data depth gives a center-outward order on whole ensemble members. With it you can define a median member, a 50% band (the envelope of the deepest half) and outliers, even when the members are contours or curves and have no total order. Inclusion Depth uses only inside and outside relations between contours, generalizes functional half-region depth, and costs O(n²) instead of O(n³) for contour band depth. W5 speeds up surface boxplots with a neural network; W11 applies contour band depth to fire fronts.

How it bears on #980. ggdibbler rejects quantiles because they "need an order" and "do not extend to more than one dimension". Depth is the standard answer from functional data analysis: it orders whole draws, so a "functional boxplot" of ensemble lines is a consume-mode mark over a Dist<Curve> under shared draws. Suggestion: when GoFish adds signal marks for line ensembles, a depth-based band (envelope of the deepest k% of draws) is a better default than the pointwise quantile ribbon. The pointwise ribbon shows the marginal at each x; the depth band shows the shape of the most typical paths. These differ in what they claim, and a chart should say which it shows. This also gives a third option for "which draws set the axis extent" (open question 5): the envelope of the deepest k%.

M8. Beware of Validation by Eye (Medium; abstract only)

People judge regression slopes better when they fit a line themselves than when they accept a shown line. They are biased toward slopes that are too steep, which matches orthogonal-distance regression rather than ordinary least squares. Adding confidence intervals, bounding boxes or error lines did not improve accuracy; error lines reduced bias. For #635: a confidence band is not a validation aid by itself. If GoFish offers a regression overlay with a band, residual segments (error lines) are a cheap option worth having (a connect from each point to its fitted value). It also says the band must be labeled with what it is (the mean's CI vs a prediction interval), since readers do not read it as an accuracy check.

W3. Effects of Forecast Number, Order, and Cost (Medium; abstract only)

When people choose how many forecast lines to view, they view more when the outcome is ambiguous. With no cost, order hardly matters. With a cost per forecast, people decide from the first one, and an extreme first forecast leads to worse decisions. For #980: in the overlay and animation directions, draw order is part of the picture's meaning. A HOP that starts on an extreme draw biases a viewer who stops early. Suggestion: the default order of draws in animation should not be "the order in the input" when the input may be sorted. A seeded shuffle is a reasonable default. This also adds to open question 10: a fixed seed for reproducibility is needed anyway.

W10. Entropy-Based Test Framework for Uncertainty Modeling (Medium; abstract only)

They fit uniform, Gaussian, histogram and quantile models to the same ensemble and compare each model's entropy with the entropy computed on the ensemble itself. Models matching the ensemble's shape match its entropy. Few-bin histograms work well, and quantile models with many bins approach the accuracy of the data. For #980: this is evidence for a representation menu {samples, parametric, histogram, quantiles} with a fidelity score, and gives a concrete test for "did summarizing samples into quantiles lose information" (#1231, the quantile-rows shortcut). The entropy comparison is cheap and could be a unit test on a Dist conversion.

M10. Zoomable Level-of-Detail ChartTables (Low/Medium; abstract only)

A table of rows (cases) and columns (variables), where each cell holds a distribution and shows a mini-chart whose detail rises with zoom (a summary at low zoom, the full distribution at high zoom). For GoFish: the level of summary on a Dist cell (point, interval, slab, dots) is a single ordered axis of representations. That fits the "discrete presets in one tunable space" principle: one Dist mark with a detail level, not separate mark types per level.

4. Top takeaways for GoFish (2024)

  1. Add "aggregate per draw" as a third use of Dist, beside lift and consume (VMC). Folding rows within each draw and then showing the distribution of the fold is a named pattern. It types cleanly as functor map over a whole-table draw, and it requires shared draws. VMC's four grouping options (collapse, individualize, animate, aggregate) map onto signal, z-overlay, time and this new case. That is a ready vocabulary check for section 5 of the design note.
  2. Let the input shape fix the dependence model, and accept a joint Gaussian as a third representation (VMC, UASTL, UADAPy). A long table with a draw key means shared draws. A column of per-cell distributions means independent cells. A mean plus covariance over a set of cells is a joint without draws, and linear operators (loess, PCA, a stack sum) keep it closed-form. This answers open question 2 without a default and gives #635 exact loess bands as diag(LΣLᵀ).
  3. Slab density shares one scale by default; per-group normalization is opt-in and lintable (Fygenson & Padilla). Treat slab thickness as the "density of X" measure so mismatched normalizations disagree as units (#1091, #971, ridgelines).
  4. For line and contour ensembles, offer a depth-based band beside the pointwise quantile ribbon (Inclusion Depth, surface boxplots, band depth). It answers ggdibbler's claim that quantiles do not extend past 1D for whole-draw objects, and it gives another rule for which draws set the axis extent.
  5. Two smaller design inputs. "Uncertain but unquantified" is a real third level (Tominski et al.) that Dist<T> does not model, so decide whether it is in scope. Draw order in HOPs and overlays changes decisions (Matzen et al.), so use a seeded shuffle, not input order.

Uncertainty visualization at IEEE VIS 2025: triage for GoFish

Date of triage: 2026-10-30. Scope: the 2025 IEEE Workshop on Uncertainty Visualization ("Unraveling Relationships of Uncertainty, AI, and Decision-Making", Vienna, 2 Nov 2025) and uncertainty-related papers in the IEEE VIS 2025 main program (full, short, and TVCG presentations).

1. Coverage

Workshop. The URL pattern worked: https://tusharathawale.github.io/uncertainty-vis-workshop-2025/accepted-papers.html. It lists 9 papers (4 long, 5 short) with titles and authors but no links. I found arXiv preprints for 6 of them by searching titles. Proceedings DOIs use the prefix 10.1109/UncertaintyVisualization68947.2025.000NN; I confirmed two (.00010 for wGMM-UAPCA, .00012 for Layers of Doubt). Gaps:

  • "Visualizing Uncertainty from Meteo-Oceanic Ensemble Data" (Bañgate et al.): no preprint, abstract, or DOI found. Rated from the title only.
  • "Layers of Doubt" and "Visualizing Diagnostic Uncertainty in Tabular Data": no full text; I read only the abstract-level descriptions on the TU Wien, Newcastle, and Sabancı repository pages.
  • The ieeevis.org workshop session pages (https://study.ieeevis.org/year/2025/program/session_w-uncertaintyvis-1.html) list no papers.

Main conference. I downloaded the program's data file https://ieeevis.org/year/2025/program/papers.json. It holds 309 entries: 131 full papers, 80 short papers, 75 TVCG/CGA presentations without a type, 12 arts program items, and 11 CG&A items. Each entry has its abstract. I cross-checked against the session list https://ieeevis.org/year/2025/info/program/papers_list. I keyword-filtered every title, abstract, and keyword list (uncertain, ensemble, distribution, probabilistic, Bayes, interval, confidence, risk, forecast, posterior, stochastic, variability, quantile, sampling, noise, decision) and read the abstracts of every hit. Then I kept the ~22 papers with real uncertainty content and dropped the false hits (such as "distribution" in the HPC sense, or "ensemble" meaning tree ensembles). VIS 2025 had no dedicated uncertainty paper session. The uncertainty papers are spread across SciVis, perception, and decision-making sessions.

Full text read: Zhao & Berger (arXiv HTML) and wGMM-UAPCA (arXiv HTML). Everything else is rated from its abstract (plus arXiv/repository summaries where noted). The "Uncertainty-Aware PCA Revisited" PDF link in the program returned 404.

Adjacent, out of scope: Correll & Harrison, "A Priest, a Rabbi, and an Atheist Walk into an Error Bar" (alt.VIS 2025, arXiv:2509.08213), a provocation that says the field reduces too many kinds of uncertainty to error bars around an estimate. I did not read it. Also, "GoFish: A Grammar of More Graphics!" itself appeared in the Authoring and Design session.

W = workshop, F = VIS full paper, S = VIS short paper, T = TVCG paper presented at VIS.
