3. Deep notes (High and Medium)

3.1 Zhao & Berger: animation versus small multiples for judging correlation across draws (High, full text read)

What it is. Each stimulus is a distribution over 2D scalar fields: a zero-mean Gaussian process on a grid. The background covariance has a length scale σₗ. A planted elliptical region is strongly correlated across draws, with strength σᵥ² from 1.5 to 3. The viewer must find the region that "moves together" across draws, which no single draw shows. They compared two displays (with 88 Prolific participants, between-subjects color scale):

  • an animation (HOP) of 30 draws, each shown twice, at 6 fps for 10 s, with no pause or scrub;
  • small multiples of 10 draws in a 2×5 grid, shown for 10 s.

Both used Viridis or Blues.

Results.

  • Small multiples gave better recognition (how often the region was present), but only for the larger target.
  • Animation gave better localization: higher IoU when the viewer brushed the region, mostly because small multiples caused more translation error.
  • The multi-hue scale (Viridis) helped recognition at weak correlation, but made viewers underestimate the region's size. Blues made them overestimate it.

The authors state no explicit guidelines. The two displays also showed different numbers of draws (30 versus 10), so the comparison is confounded, and the authors say so.

Why it matters for GoFish (#980: which operators give a draw a direction; shared draws).

  1. It is the first evidence I found that the draw direction changes which judgment is easy. Time (animation) is better for where. Space (juxtaposition, which in ggdibbler terms is a facet/x-dodge of the draw key) is better for whether/how often. So the choice of draw direction in GoFish should be a user choice with no single default, not a heuristic. This fits the "uniform rule + explicit override" principle. The design note can cite this when it argues that the draw key is an ordinary by key that any spread, or time, can consume.
  2. The task only exists when cells share draws. A correlated region is invisible if every grid cell is an independent Dist. ggdibbler's default assumes separate distributions are independent; that assumption would make this whole class of chart impossible. This is a strong argument for the rvar / shape-C representation (a long table with one row per draw, keyed by draw ID) as the base layer, with independent per-cell distributions as the special case. It is a concrete visualization that a design built on independent cells cannot draw. Add it to the hard examples for #980.
  3. Suggested story: a GP field ensemble drawn both as spread({ by: "draw" }) small multiples and as animate({ by: "draw" }), from one spec, changing only the step that consumes the draw key. This puts the paper's comparison into one GoFish spec.

3.2 wGMM-UAPCA: Gaussian mixtures as a representation with closed-form linear pushforward (High, full text read)

What it is. Each class (in their experiments) or each uncertain point (in principle) is a Gaussian mixture: weights, means, and covariances. PCA is linear, so they project the density itself. Each component maps to N(Pᵀμ, PᵀΣP) with the same weight. The projected mixture is exact and needs no numerical integration. The projection matrix comes from a weighted uncertainty-aware covariance. Per-distribution importance weights τ (set with sliders) change the projection live. Output: highest-density isolines at chosen mass levels (25/50/95%), computed numerically by sorting grid cells by density and summing mass, because mixture contours are not ellipses. They argue that samples clutter the view. Against a sample+KDE reference, their density was closer (lower KL divergence) in 10 of 17 datasets.

Bearing on #980 (samples-only versus a richer representation). ggdibbler rejects densities because "a density does not have the same domain as the data, so it cannot feed the plot's statistic". This paper refines that. For affine stages (position scales, unit changes, PCA, and any linear derive), a parametric family that is closed under affine maps (Gaussian, a Gaussian mixture, or a point mass) can go through the pipeline exactly. The design note's map section already says "an affine map of a normal is a normal; otherwise we fall back to draws". The concrete suggestions:

  • Add mixtures as a third representation next to samples and named families. A mixture is also the natural result of bind over a discrete variable, and a monadic Dist already produces it. It is closed under affine map, and it is the shape that the "Shape B: a field annotation over existing columns" sketch would produce from per-row mean+sd columns grouped by class.
  • A highest-density-region contour is a slab-family member in 2D. It is the 2D analogue of ggdist's interval at several mass levels. If GoFish's signal-mode marks (interval/slab) accept 2D Dist<Point>, the HDR isoline at levels [.25,.5,.95] is the mark. Computing it by sorting a grid and summing mass works for any density, from parameters or from a KDE of draws. That gives one implementation for both representations.
  • The weights τ are just the mixture weights over a grouping key. They need no new concept.

3.3 Friesecke et al., "Uncertainty-Aware PCA Revisited" (Medium, abstract only)

Claim. PCA is discontinuous: when eigenvalues are close, a small perturbation can swap eigenvectors. So small uncertainty in the data can give huge uncertainty in the projected points. Uncertainty in the data also creates uncertainty in the projection basis itself. They give a closed form for eigenvector uncertainty and a 3D glyph that tells you whether a fixed-basis UAPCA (like #3.2) is enough, or whether you must re-run PCA per draw.

Bearing on #980 (visual-convergence test). ggdibbler's Theorem 3.1 says that as variance goes to 0, the picture of the uncertain data converges to the picture of the exact data, because the plot is a continuous function. This paper names a common pipeline stage that is not continuous. Others in GoFish are sorting or ordering by value (bar order, treemap tiling order), argmax/label choice, binning boundaries (#48), and greedy label placement. Two concrete consequences:

  1. A visual-convergence test should be stated per stage, and stages should declare whether they are continuous in their data inputs. A typeclass-style capability such as "continuous in field f" would let the type system say where convergence holds and where a lint (#1091) should warn: "this operator is discontinuous in a distribution-valued field; the draws may reorder".
  2. There are two kinds of lift. One fixes the layout's derived parameters (projection basis, sort order, bin edges) from the point estimate and maps draws through it. The other re-derives them per draw. ggdibbler's "each statistic runs once per draw" is the second kind. wGMM-UAPCA uses the first. GoFish should make the choice explicit, because for discontinuous stages the two give visibly different charts. This bears on the "which operators give the draw a direction" question. An operator that re-derives structure per draw (sort, pack, bin) is different from one that only places each draw.

3.4 Evers & Weiskopf, "Uncertainty-aware Spectral Visualization" (Medium, abstract only)

Claim. A Gaussian-process time series pushed through a Fourier or continuous wavelet transform gives spectra whose distribution is a weighted mixture of non-central chi-squared distributions, which is not normal. They draw it with percentile-based bands in 1D and 2D (wavelet), and add views for correlation, sensitivity, and signal-to-noise.

Bearing on GoFish. It is a clean example of a nonlinear map (power spectrum = squared magnitude) where the closed form exists but leaves the family. Two points:

  • It supports the design note's fallback rule. The quantile interface (percentile bands) is the one stable interface the mark consumes, whether the distribution came from a named family, a mixture, or draws. So the signal-mode marks should consume quantile/cdf methods, not a particular representation.
  • It is also a correlated-cells case. Neighboring frequencies and wavelet coefficients are correlated, and the paper visualizes those correlations, so this is more weight against assuming independent cells.

3.5 Potter et al., temporal and categorical uncertainty on timelines (Medium, abstract only)

Claim. In an 81-person study on activity-recall timelines, accuracy was better when uncertain times were encoded with transparency rather than dashing. Participants' preferred encoding did not match their best-performing one.

Bearing on #980 (the missing transparency direction) and #256 (time datatype).

  • ggdibbler's z-direction is transparency over overlaid draws. This paper is evidence for a related but different thing: transparency as an encoding of an interval-valued position, the "signal" mode for a Dist<Time> on a timeline. It shows the transparency/opacity channel is worth having in both modes. One implementation might serve both, since overlaying N draws at alpha 1/N gives a fading band.
  • For the time datatype: an uncertain event start/end is a Dist<Instant>, and the mark that consumes it is a rect whose ends fade. This is a small, concrete signal-mode example to add to the #980 examples.
  • Their preference-versus-performance mismatch is a reason not to choose a default encoding from user preference.

3.6 Markant et al., "Correcting Misperceptions at a Glance" (Medium, abstract only)

Claim. People saw survey data on the opposing party's views. Seeing Mean+Interval (the middle 75%) gave a weaker correction than seeing Mean-only or Mean+Points. Seeing Mean+Points gave the best later recall of the actual distribution.

Bearing on GoFish. This is an evaluation result that implies a design rule: when the data are draws or individual responses, showing them beats summarizing them as an interval. In GoFish terms, the lift (noise) mode, or a dotplot-style signal mark, should be as easy to write as an error bar, and an error bar should not be the shortest path. It supports making the Dist path primary rather than bolting errorbar onto summary columns. It also argues against keeping the #1231 quantile-row shortcut as the recommended spelling.

3.7 Mehta, Kindlmann & Kale, "Designing for Disclosure" (Medium, abstract only)

Claim. A content analysis of 425 visualization techniques gives a taxonomy of "disclosure tactics", classified by how they change the data representation behind a chart: what they reveal, distort, or hide. The paper says this frame applies to authoring tools and to uncertainty communication.

Bearing on #1091 (lint rules checkable by type). GoFish's operators are exactly the "changes to the data representation" this taxonomy classifies. If each operator (group → aggregate, bin, derive summary, filter) carries a disclosure tag, then "this chart collapses a Dist or a group to a point and shows no spread" becomes a lint that can be checked. The paper may supply the category names. Worth reading in full before writing #1091's rule list. I have not read it, so I don't know whether its categories line up with operators.

4. Top takeaways for GoFish (2025)

  1. Shared draws are not optional. Zhao & Berger's task (finding a spatially correlated region across draws) cannot be drawn if cells are independent Dist values. Evers & Weiskopf also visualize correlations between spectrum cells. This is the strongest 2025 argument for making the rvar/draw-keyed long table the base representation, with independent cells as the special case. It goes against ggdibbler's independence default.
  2. Draw direction should be the user's choice, and it changes the task. Animation is better for localization and juxtaposition for recognition (Zhao & Berger). Transparency beats dashing for interval-valued time (Potter et al.). Keep the draw key an ordinary by key that spread, animation, or opacity can consume, with no built-in preference among them.
  3. Add Gaussian mixtures as a representation closed under affine maps. wGMM-UAPCA shows exact linear pushforward of mixtures and draws highest-density isolines at chosen mass levels. Use samples for nonlinear stages. Signal-mode marks should consume a quantile/cdf/density interface so they don't care which representation they get.
  4. Visual convergence needs continuity, and real stages lack it. "UAPCA Revisited" shows that PCA's discontinuity breaks small-noise reasoning. GoFish operators that sort, bin, pack, or choose labels are discontinuous too. Decide, per operator, whether a lift re-derives structure per draw or fixes it from the point estimate. Let the type system flag discontinuous stages in a distribution-valued field (a #1091 lint).
  5. Showing points beats showing intervals for communicating a distribution (Markant et al.). This supports making the noise/dotplot path at least as short to write as an error bar, and retiring the #1231 quantile-row shortcut.

Uncertainty visualization at IEEE VIS 2026: triage for GoFish

Date: 2026-10-30. Scope: the VIS 2026 Workshop on Uncertainty Visualization (Nov 9, Boston), and uncertainty-related papers in the VIS 2026 main conference (full papers, short papers, TVCG and CG&A presentations).

1. Coverage

Sources:

  • Workshop accepted-papers page: https://tusharathawale.github.io/uncertainty-vis-workshop-2026/accepted-papers.html. It lists 15 items: 8 papers for full presentation, 4 lightning talks, and 3 demos. The page has no PDF links. The workshop program page says only "TBA".
  • VIS 2026 "Presented Research" listing: https://ieeevis.org/year/2026/program/content/. I fetched the whole listing (488 entries: 145 full, 68 short, 59 TVCG, 11 CG&A, 12 Uncertainty Workshop, plus other workshops, posters and challenge entries) and filtered titles and sessions by uncertainty keywords (uncertain, distribution, ensemble, probabilistic, Bayesian, interval, confidence, forecast, noise, sampling, density, and others). I also read every paper in the sessions "I'm not so certain" (7 papers) and "Can We Trust This Chart?" (8 short papers). For 38 candidate papers I fetched the per-paper page (abstract, authors, links).
  • The old URL ieeevis.org/year/2026/info/program/papers_list/ now redirects to the home page, and the aa.ieeevis.org mirror does the same.
  • Per-paper pages carry signed PDF links. I downloaded and read these:
    • In full, or the method and discussion sections: Petek et al. (arXiv 2508.00937v4), Mason et al., and Piccolotto et al. (jk-plots).
    • The parts named in each note: Zou et al. (weighted MFV), Fygenson et al. (mixed uncertainty), Sarma (mCCDF), Brooks (optical operations), and Counterbalance.
    • Also read: the UADAPy Distribution class source (github.com/UniStuttgart-VISUS/uadapy, uadapy/distribution.py).
    • Everything else is from the abstract only, and the table says so.

What I could not get:

  • The 3 workshop demos (UADAPy, pid-depth, CESET) are not on the ieeevis.org listing. The VIS site shows 12 workshop papers, which are the 8 talks and 4 lightning talks.
    • UADAPy: I used the 2024 toolbox paper (arXiv 2409.10217) and its source code.
    • pid-depth: I found only the underlying method paper ("Probabilistic Inclusion Depth for Fuzzy Contour Ensemble Visualization", arXiv 2512.15187, same authors).
    • CESET: I found nothing online beyond the title and authors.
  • No 2026 workshop paper has a public PDF beyond the signed VIS links.
  • ggdibbler itself is not a 2026 paper. Its authors' 2026 VIS full paper is "Colour Blinded by the Noise". That paper builds on ggdibbler and uses ggdibbler to make its stimuli.

Posters were out of scope. Two related ones I noticed but did not read: "Visualizing Dengue Forecasts for Public Health Preparedness" (Xing et al.) and "A Declarative Specification for Clinical Reference Range Number Line Visualizations" (Fisk et al., a DSL that compiles to Vega-Lite).

3. Deep notes (High and Medium)

M1. Petek, Nabergoj, Štrumbelj: "A General Approach to Visualizing Uncertainty in Statistical Graphics" (TVCG, presented at VIS 2026). Rating: High

What is new.

  • The base visualization is a function. A chart M maps one realization θ of the uncertain quantity to an image. Uncertainty in θ then gives a distribution over images. The paper draws n samples, renders each with the unchanged base visualization, and averages the images pixel by pixel. Their library bootplot (https://github.com/davidnabergoj/bootplot) is a Python reference implementation for IID data. Its draws come from a nonparametric bootstrap: it resamples the rows with replacement and reruns the user's matplotlib code on each resample.
  • Coverage theorem (Sec. 3.3).
    • A mark that varies along one axis: the range of the n drawn positions is an interval with coverage (n−1)/(n+1), whatever the distribution. So 9, 19, 39, 199 and 1999 draws give 80%, 90%, 95%, 99% and 99.9% intervals.
    • Many points, each varying on its own parallel axis (a regression line): the envelope is a pointwise band with the same coverage.
    • A region chosen before looking: if no draw enters it, a Jeffreys Beta(n+½, ½) bound gives a test.
    • Confidence intervals and bands therefore appear without being computed.
  • Certain and uncertain elements (Sec. 5.2). Elements that do not depend on the draw (axes, labels, a bar drawn from the full data) appear crisp. Elements that do depend on it blur. Mixing the two gives new idioms. For example, a certain bar with an uncertain point gives a bar with an error bar. A certain small pie over an uncertain pie gives an anchored, ambiguated pie.
  • Layout consistency (Sec. 4.1.6). Every draw must use the same axis limits, ticks, category order, color mapping and anchoring. Otherwise the averaged image is unusable. They say this is the main practical burden.
  • Intensity (Sec. 4.1.5). Plain averaging with weight 1/n makes small marks over a large range fade away, so more draws look like less uncertainty. They fix this with a monotone, point-symmetric Beta-CDF transform of each pixel's frequencies (defaults τ = 0.3, k = 2.5).
  • Failure cases (Sec. 5.4, 5.4.1).
    • Dependent quantities (pie slices, stacked bars, Sankey) make the interior segments hard to read. Only the anchored end segments stay clear.
    • Bubble charts mix position and area uncertainty.
    • A continuous color scale (three or more colors, gradients, transparency) is not injective under averaging. A certain mid-gray looks the same as an even mix of light and dark.
    • Two colors are fine, because the blend becomes an implicit probability scale.
  • The output is raster only.

How it refines or contradicts the ggdibbler and ggdist framing.

  • Sec. 6.1 compares itself to Mason et al. and ggdibbler by name. Its claim is that working in image space with a black-box base visualization keeps complex joint distributions, applies to any mark, and comes with coverage guarantees. ggdibbler "forces the user to explicitly manage distributions and overlay rules". The paper's Table 3 marks ggdibbler as "×" for most examples.
  • Read through GoFish's design note, this is the "lift" mode (5a) with z/transparency as the only direction, and with the draw source fixed to "resample the rows". The two points that matter:
    1. Resampling rows gives shared draws for free. Draw i is one bootstrap replicate of the whole dataset. Every statistic computed in draw i (a mean per category, a regression line, a stacked total) therefore belongs to the same joint outcome. That is why lines and stacks stay valid in their examples.
    2. The coverage theorem gives n a meaning.
  • It does not contradict "stacking draws on the measured axis is a type error". Their stacked bars stack categories within one draw, which the design note already allows. Their "dependent quantities" problem is the perceptual side of the same fact.

Which GoFish questions it bears on.

  • #980 Q2 (default dependence) and section 4, "Independent cells versus shared draws".
  • #980 Q5 (axis domain).
  • #980 Q9 (z direction).
  • #980 Q10 (visual convergence and how many draws).
  • #1091 (lint rules).
  • #1231 (quantile-rows shortcut).

Concrete suggestions.

  1. A draw source that is not a field type. Add a flow step such as resample({ n }), a bootstrap over rows, beside sample() from a Dist field. It produces a draw key whose draws are shared by construction. Downstream derive, aggregates, line and stack-within-draw are then all legal under the existing rules, with no rvar input needed.
    • This answers the shared-draws question for the common case. Here the uncertainty is sampling uncertainty of statistics, not uncertainty given per cell.
    • It is a design decision for the user: it adds a second way to make draws. Report it as a proposal, not a settled answer.
  2. Give n a coverage spelling. For example sample({ coverage: 0.95 }) resolves to n = 39, using n = (1+c)/(1−c). This also answers Q10's "how many draws does a story need". A convergence story with fixed n has a stated interval meaning.
  3. Q5 axis domain. Their layout-consistency rule says the domain must be shared across draws. In GoFish that means domain inference runs once over the union of all draws (which GoFish's one-pass domain inference does naturally), never per draw. The extent is then the extreme draws, which is exactly the implied (n−1)/(n+1) interval. So the axis extent and the coverage of the picture agree.
  4. The z direction (Q9), draws drawn in one frame with group, needs more than opacity 1/n. Give it a monotone opacity transform (their Beta-CDF, or a simple floor) so that small marks stay visible as n grows. Without it, the visual-convergence test can pass for the wrong reason: marks fade as n grows.
  5. Lint candidates for #1091, each checkable from types:
    • A Dist lifted into a continuous color channel under the z overlay is ambiguous (not injective). Warn, or require a two-color or ordinal palette.
    • Lifting into a stack within draw is legal, but interior segments are unreadable. Suggest anchoring with a certain overlay. This could be a hint, not an error.
  6. A vector-output alternative. GoFish emits SVG, so pixel averaging is not available as such. The GoFish version of their method is "lift, then overlay the n SVG marks with the opacity transform". This is equivalent for opaque marks. It differs where marks overlap within one draw, because SVG alpha compositing is not averaging. Note this as a known gap. It is also a case for #980 Q11 (a canvas path).

M2. Mason, Rogers, Kleffner, Cook: "Colour Blinded by the Noise" (VIS 2026 full). Rating: High

What is new.

  • The test. An implicit, Ishihara-style test of "uncertainty as noise" on choropleth plates. Each region holds a normal distribution. A digit is visible only if the signal is statistically real. Visibility is compared with the power curves of a t-test and Moran's I.
  • Five maps compared:
    • plain choropleth,
    • value/variance bivariate,
    • VSUP,
    • "pixel" (each region subdivided into a grid of draws, made with ggdibbler),
    • "transparency" (the same draws overlaid at alpha 1/n, made with ggdibbler; only the position adjustment differs).
  • Results:
    • The bivariate map is statistically the same as the plain choropleth: variance has no effect on whether the signal shows. "Treating uncertainty as a second variable, independent of the estimate, will not facilitate the suppression of false signals."
    • VSUP does suppress, but not in line with the tests. It also needs global knowledge of the data's range, which they call a form of data snooping.
    • Pixel and transparency roughly track the test power curves (too visible at high variance, not enough at low) and are not different from each other.
  • Their reading:
    • Suppressing false signals needs a full-distribution representation (samples, and in theory quantiles, PMF or PDF).
    • The position adjustment for draws does not change the statistical signal, as long as draws are equally weighted. This is the empirical support for ggdibbler's "draws are perpendicular in the grammar" claim.
    • "When multiple random variables are fed into the system at once, transparency is the only viable position adjustment."
  • Further notes:
    • The plot set is restricted to maps whose zero-variance limit is the plain choropleth. That is the visual convergence property.
    • Reading skill matters, with large differences between participants.

Which GoFish questions it bears on.

  • #980 Q9 (z direction: new operator or not; does the pixel map need a subdivide operator).
  • #980 Q10 (convergence test).
  • The design note's lift section (5a).
  • #1091.

Concrete suggestions.

  1. Draw placement is a free strategy choice. The result supports treating it as a strategy family whose members (x dodge, z overlay, t animation, subdivide) are interchangeable for validity. That fits the one-operator-with-a-strategy pattern (Overlap.*-style).
  2. Make z the default when more than one channel is lifted at once. In that case it is the only valid choice. Subdivide (the pixel map) adds no validity over transparency, so it can be deferred. That narrows Q9.
  3. Keep convergence to the plain chart as the admission rule. The paper uses it to choose its plots. It confirms the planned capture-diff convergence test as the right acceptance test for every lift strategy.
  4. Lint (#1091). Mapping field(x).sd() to a separate channel (bivariate) does not convey uncertainty as noise. If GoFish ever distinguishes noise and signal intent, it can warn that the bivariate form behaves like no uncertainty at all. VSUP needs a global, not local, scale domain. If GoFish adds a VSUP palette to the Color family, its domain must be the global (value, uncertainty) extent. That is a typed check.
  5. Equal weighting is the stated condition. This links to M4: weighted draws need weighted opacity.

M3. Piccolotto, Pahr, Lotteraner, Öztank, Bögl: "Adaptive Uncertainty Visualization With jk-Plots" (VIS 2026 short). Rating: High

What is new.

  • Kruschke-style plots, generalized to a family. A Bayesian intercept-only model (brms/Stan, Normal for continuous data, categorical for discrete) gives posterior draws of the model parameters. Each draw is a whole reference distribution, such as Normal(μᵢ, σᵢ).
  • Common idioms drawn from those reference models, above the raw observations: pie, histogram, box plot, CDF and PDF.
  • Three summaries of the many reference models:
    • subset: draw k models at random,
    • bands: 50/80/95% intervals over the models,
    • gradient: fade away from the main trend.
  • Adaptive by construction. With n = 10 the jk-CDF is a wide band. As n grows it converges to the empirical CDF. It never shows the staircase artifacts of an ECDF on little data.
  • Implementation detail. The jk pie chart draws each posterior draw as one concentric ring: stacked bars in polar coordinates, one ring per draw.
  • Limitation. If the model is wrong, the picture misleads. A Normal model hides Gamma skew that the empirical box plot shows.

How it refines the framing. This is neither pure noise nor pure signal. Each element of the outer distribution is itself a distribution, drawn as a signal mark. It is Dist<Dist<T>>: lift over the outer draws, consume the inner distribution.

Which GoFish questions it bears on.

  • #980 section 4 (Dist is a monad).
  • #980 Q1 (parameters versus draws).
  • #980 Q10 (convergence).
  • #971 (violin), #1231.

Concrete suggestions.

  1. A case for the monad section. Use the jk-CDF as the worked example in section 4: a field of type Dist<Normal> (posterior over parameters). sample({ n: 50 }) lifts the outer level and gives a draw key. A consume mark (slab / CDF line) then reads each inner distribution.
    • This needs the parameters representation. One draw is (μ, σ), not a sample.
    • So it is a concrete argument that samples alone are not enough. The cell representation should allow "a sample of parameter vectors of a named family". That is samples of parameters, a mixed representation.
  2. New placement: draw spread on the radial axis in polar. The jk pie places draws by spread({ by: "draw", dir: "r" }) under Coord.polar(), with categories stacked within each draw ring. This is legal under the existing rules: stack within draw, spread across draws. It is a good story to test the rules.
  3. A summary-strategy family. "subset / bands / gradient" is a strategy choice for how a set of draws is shown, and it matches ggdist's interval/gradient split. It may be the same family as the lift directions, or a sibling one. This is a naming question for the user.
  4. A second convergence axis. As the data sample size grows, the jk-plot converges to the empirical plot. This is convergence in data size, not in variance. A story pair (n = 10 and n = 1000) is a good regression check.

M4. Zou, Wu, Fygenson, Wang, Correll, Padilla: "Seeing Through the Forecast Clutter" (VIS 2026 full). Rating: Medium

What is new.

  • Two preregistered experiments (N = 480, 900) on climate forecasts.
  • Multiple-forecast visualization (MFV, a spaghetti of model runs) beats summary CI plots for perceiving the distribution's shape. It also reduces the false assumption that the distribution is normal.
  • A downsampled MFV of 9 lines, weighted by opacity (mapped to 0.1 to 1) or by linewidth (0.5 to 1.4 scaled), preserves the perceived distribution. So weights can carry a second attribute (here a consensus weight) without distorting the distribution.

Which GoFish questions it bears on.

  • #980 section 7.1 (declaring a distribution-valued field).
  • #980 Q1, Q9.
  • The z-direction rule "alpha = 1/n".

Concrete suggestions.

  1. Weighted samples. The cell representation should allow weighted samples (draws plus weights): importance-sampling output, model-averaging weights, ensemble weights. This is a third representation beside equal-weight samples and quantiles.
  2. Weights in the lift. Under the lift, a weight becomes a per-draw opacity or stroke width. This study is evidence that this keeps the perceived distribution. M2's equal-weight condition then becomes "opacity proportional to weight".
  3. Downsampling. Downsampling to about 9 lines is acceptable for distribution-shape tasks. This bears on #980 Q11 (SVG size). A small n can be a fine default for line ensembles. Note that M1's coverage meaning at n = 9 is only an 80% interval, so coverage and n should both be offered.

M5. Fygenson, Padilla, Matzen: "Mixed Uncertainty in One View" (VIS 2026 full). Rating: Medium

What is new.

  • Three preregistered studies (N = 923) on time-series forecasts with a CI band plus a qualitative, expert confidence level (low, medium, high).
  • Text, blur glyphs, hue glyphs, hue overlays and transparency overlays on the CI all shift decisions in the same way. Low confidence makes people less decisive near the threshold.
  • Hue is read as "danger", not "uncertainty". Blur and transparency read as uncertainty, more so when stronger.
  • "Medium" adds little beyond "high".
  • Blur changes the perceived size and position of a band. So they designed a gray, blurred stroke outside the band edge, which keeps the CI extent.

Which GoFish questions it bears on.

  • #980 section 7 (field declaration).
  • #1091.
  • Any blur or fuzz effect in GoFish.

Concrete suggestions.

  1. Qualitative confidence is not a Dist. It is an ordinal field beside the Dist. The type design should not try to fold it into Dist<T>. It maps to an opacity, or to a blurred halo on the interval or ribbon mark.
  2. An effect rule. A blur or fuzz effect must not change the geometry that layout and axes read. Render it outside the mark's bounds, as a halo, and keep it out of the bbox. This is a design rule for any future blur paint option.
  3. A lint hint for color. Hue mapped to an uncertainty field reads as hazard. This is weak evidence, so make it a hint, not an error.

M6. Sarma: "Adapting CCDF Plots for Visualizing Ordinal Regression Results" (VIS 2026 short). Rating: Medium

What is new.

  • For Likert outcomes from cumulative-link (probit) models, the paper plots a modified CCDF: Φ⁻¹ P(y ≥ k) for each rating level k, one line per posterior draw.
  • The rating where the draws cross 0.5 is the median.
  • Differences between conditions read as horizontal shifts.
  • The same takeaways people usually get by treating ordinal data as metric (means) can be read from the plot without that assumption.

Which GoFish questions it bears on.

  • #980 section 4 ("What Dist<T> can do depends on T").
  • #980 Q8 (uncertain categories).

Concrete suggestions.

  1. A new row for the "what Dist<T> can do" table. Dist<Ordinal> has cdf, quantile and median but no mean. Its natural consume mark is a CCDF line on a probit-scaled y axis. field("rating").mean() on an ordinal should be the type error the design note sketches. This paper is its motivating counterexample.
  2. A test of coordinate transforms. The probit axis is a nonlinear scale on probabilities. It is a good test of the claim that "a scale acts on the distribution by map".

M7. Long, Chang, Wu, Kale, Kay: "Visual Decoding Operators" (VIS 2026 full). Rating: Medium (abstract and practitioner note only)

What is new.

  • Reading PDF and CDF charts is broken into five reusable, chart-agnostic perceptual operators, each with a bias and variance fitted by a hierarchical Bayesian model.
  • Composed operators predict performance on a different experiment (scatterplot mean estimation) with no refitting.
  • Practitioner takeaway: reading a median off a CDF is two chained projections, so reference lines and direct annotation target the actual error.

Which GoFish questions it bears on. The signal marks (5b) and #1091.

Concrete suggestion. When GoFish ships CDF or slab signal marks, the default chrome could include a reference line at the quantile asked about (an interval level or a 0.5 line). In the longer term, operator-level error is a candidate scoring function for a lint or recommendation pass. That is research, not an action now.

W13. UADAPy (workshop demo; 2024 toolbox). Rating: Medium

What is in the code.

  • Distribution(model, name, n_dims) wraps either a scipy.stats frozen distribution or an array of samples.
  • Samples get a Gaussian KDE for pdf and sample, unless name == "Normal", in which case the samples are fit to a multivariate normal.
  • It provides sample, pdf, cdf, mean, cov, skew, kurt, and marginal(dims) (KDE fallback).
  • One Distribution is multivariate: a whole vector with covariance.

Which GoFish questions it bears on. #980 Q1 (what crosses the bridge) and Q2 (dependence).

Concrete suggestion. This is direct Python precedent that "parameters or samples behind one interface" is workable. It also shows another answer to dependence: a joint distribution over a vector of cells (one object with covariance), as opposed to rvar's shared draw index. M10 MAGIC and W6 Jacobi sets model correlation the same way (a multivariate Gaussian). If GoFish's Python side accepts scipy-like objects, a multivariate one should map to shared draws when sampled, not to independent cells.

4. Top takeaways for GoFish (2026)

  1. Bootstrap over rows is the missing draw source, and it gives shared draws for free. Petek et al. (M1) make every uncertainty picture by rerunning the plain chart on resampled data. The usual case, "uncertainty of a statistic computed from rows", needs no Dist column: a resample({ n }) flow step makes a draw key whose draws are joint by construction. Then lines, regressions and stacking within a draw are all valid under the existing rules. This would cover much of #635 (confidence bands for smoothers) and #1231 without per-cell distributions. It needs the user's decision on whether it is a second draw source or a constructor of Dist.
  2. n has a precise meaning, and the axis domain follows from it.
    • n draws give an (n−1)/(n+1) interval: 39 draws for 95%.
    • Offer coverage as a spelling of n. Compute the domain over the union of draws, never per draw.
    • This settles #980 Q5. It gives Q10 its fixed n: the convergence story has a stated interval meaning.
  3. The z (transparency) direction is validated but underspecified.
    • Mason et al. (M2) show transparency and subdivide are statistically equivalent. Transparency is the only valid choice when several channels are lifted at once. So z should be the default, and subdivide can wait.
    • The opacity rule for draws in one frame (#980 Q9) needs an opacity transform so small marks do not fade (M1). It also needs per-draw weights (M4).
  4. The representation needs more than "samples or quantiles".
    • Weighted samples (M4).
    • Samples of parameter vectors of a named family, that is Dist<Dist<T>> (M3 jk-plots, a good monad test case).
    • Joint multivariate objects whose sampling gives shared draws (W13 UADAPy, M10).
    • Qualitative confidence is a separate ordinal field, not part of Dist (M5).
    • Dist<Ordinal> has cdf but no mean, and its consume mark is the mCCDF (M6).
  5. New typed lint rules for #1091 with empirical backing:
    • Value plus sd() on separate channels behaves like no uncertainty for noise intent (M2).
    • A lifted Dist on a continuous color channel under overlay is not injective (M1).
    • VSUP needs a global, not local, scale domain (M2).
    • A blur effect must not change the geometry layout reads (M5).
    • A connecting mark across draws, with no split by the draw key, stays an error, and M1's resampling sidesteps it legitimately.

ggdibbler and the GoFish proposal

ggdibbler, by Harriet Mason, Dianne Cook, Sarah Goodwin and Susan VanderPlas, turns a ggplot2 plot into its uncertainty counterpart. You keep the plot code, replace a variable with a random variable from distributional, and add _sample to the geom. ggdibbler draws from each random variable and places the draws with a nested position. As the variance goes to zero, the plot converges to the plain ggplot2 plot. It works with most ggplot2 geoms and with any aesthetic.

The GoFish proposal for #980 takes this idea as its noise half. This tab puts the two side by side, then shows plots that ggdibbler's own issues ask for. The ggdibbler images are real renders from ggdibbler 0.6.8.9000 (GitHub main, commit 6bca10a) with distributional 0.9.0. The GoFish images are drawn with today's GoFish on draws precomputed in JavaScript, because dist.* and sample do not exist yet. So the two columns show different random draws of the same estimates.

Draws and samples. A draw is one random value taken from a distribution. A sample is a collection of draws. sample({ n }) takes n draws per row and adds a draw key that says which draw each new row is. With shared draws, draw i is one consistent world across rows: draw 3 of every row comes from the same run of a model. With independent draws, each row is drawn on its own, so the shared index is only a label.

The R scripts, the data and the GoFish stories are in proposals/uncertainty-specs/ggdibbler-r/ and packages/gofish-graphics/stories/proposals/uncertainty/Ggdibbler.stories.tsx. The bar, scatter and map images on the GoFish side are the Noise tab's renders.

How the proposal maps ggdibbler's ideas

  • A random variable in a column, such as dist_normal(mean, se), becomes a dist.* expression over the parameter columns, dist.normal({ mean: "mean", sd: "se" }). A column that already holds draws, as dist_sample does, is declared with schema: { value: { draws: "draw" } }.
  • times becomes the flow step sample({ n }). It turns each row into n rows with a draw key, which plays the role of ggdibbler's drawID.
  • A nested position main_draws becomes steps in the flow. identity_dodge is spread({ by: "draw", dir: "x", spacing: 0 }) after sample. stack_dodge is that spread with a stack inside each draw.
  • Overplotting with _identity and a low alpha becomes today's group({ by: "draw" }), which splits by the draw key and places nothing, so the draws land on top of each other. The mark sets the low opacity. The specs write it as opacity: field("draw").share(), the draw's share of the total weight, so 1/n for n draws.
  • position_subdivide becomes a small grid of draws in each region, table({ by: "draw", numCols }). This works for rectangles only. GoFish cannot cut a map polygon into cells yet.
  • A HOP, which ggdibbler makes with gganimate's transition_states(after_stat(drawID)), becomes time.sequence({ by: "draw" }).
  • Convergence holds for the same reason as in ggdibbler. The draws are placed by ordinary operators, so as every sd goes to zero, each spec draws the plain chart.
  • Two things differ. The proposal checks the draw key, so it rejects stack by draw (ggdibbler leaves stack out as a draw position for a similar reason, #34). It also draws a distribution directly with marks such as slab and interval, which ggdibbler leaves to ggdist.

Part 1. Side by side

Each card shows ggdibbler on the left and the proposed GoFish spec on the right. The Python form of the GoFish spec is under each card.

Uncertain bar chart

Five estimates, each a normal. Twenty draws per bar are dodged inside the bar's slot. geom_col_sample uses stack_dodge by default, which is a plain dodge here because there is no fill.

ggdibbler
bars |>
  mutate(value = dist_normal(mean, se)) |>
  ggplot(aes(x = category, y = value)) +
  geom_col_sample(times = 20)
GoFish, proposed
const value = dist.normal({ mean: "mean", sd: "se" });
chart(bars)
  .flow(
    spread({ by: "category", dir: "x" }),
    sample({ n: 20 }),
    spread({ by: "draw", dir: "x", spacing: 0 })
  )
  .mark(rect({ h: value }));
Python
value = dist.normal(mean="mean", sd="se")
chart(bars).flow(
    spread(by="category", dir="x"), sample(n=20), spread(by="draw", dir="x", spacing=0)
).mark(rect(h=value))

Uncertain counts, stack_dodge

From the ggdibbler README. In uncertain_mpg, each car's class and drv are categorical random variables. Each draw is a full stacked bar, and the draws are dodged within each class.

ggdibbler
ggplot(uncertain_mpg, aes(class)) +
  geom_bar_sample(aes(fill = drv), position = "stack_dodge")
GoFish, proposed
// mpgDraws: one row per car and draw, from sample_expand(uncertain_mpg)
const draws = { schema: { class: { draws: "draw" }, drv: { draws: "draw" } } };
chart(mpgDraws, draws)
  .flow(
    spread({ by: "class", dir: "x" }),
    spread({ by: "draw", dir: "x", spacing: 0 }),
    stack({ by: "drv", dir: "y" })
  )
  .mark(rect({ h: field("drv").count(), fill: "drv" }));
Python
# mpg_draws: one row per car and draw, from sample_expand(uncertain_mpg)
schema = {"class": {"draws": "draw"}, "drv": {"draws": "draw"}}
chart(mpg_draws, schema=schema).flow(
    spread(by="class", dir="x"),
    spread(by="draw", dir="x", spacing=0),
    stack(by="drv", dir="y"),
).mark(rect(h=field("drv").count(), fill="drv"))

Needs: a categorical random variable per row, so uncertain_mpg could be read as it is. The proposal's dist.categorical({ level, p }) reads its levels across rows, not a probability vector in one cell. So this spec reads the draws that ggdibbler's sample_expand(times = 10) writes. The GoFish image uses those draws, counted per draw.

Uncertain counts, stack_identity

The same data. The draws of each class are drawn in one slot at low opacity, so the boundaries between drv levels blur where they are uncertain.

ggdibbler
ggplot(uncertain_mpg, aes(class)) +
  geom_bar_sample(aes(fill = drv), alpha = 0.15, position = "stack_identity")
GoFish, proposed
chart(mpgDraws, draws)
  .flow(
    spread({ by: "class", dir: "x" }),
    group({ by: "draw" }),
    stack({ by: "drv", dir: "y" })
  )
  .mark(
    rect({
      h: field("drv").count(),
      fill: "drv",
      opacity: field("draw").share(),
    })
  );
Python
chart(mpg_draws, schema=schema).flow(
    spread(by="class", dir="x"), group(by="draw"), stack(by="drv", dir="y")
).mark(rect(h=field("drv").count(), fill="drv", opacity=field("draw").share()))

field("draw").share() gives each draw opacity 1/10. The GoFish image uses today's group with opacity 0.15, as the ggdibbler call does.

Uncertain scatter

Twelve points whose x and y are both normal, with thirty draws each. Each point becomes a cloud.

ggdibbler
points |>
  mutate(x = dist_normal(x, xSe), y = dist_normal(y, ySe)) |>
  ggplot(aes(x = x, y = y, colour = group)) +
  geom_point_sample(times = 30, alpha = 0.3)
GoFish, proposed
const x = dist.normal({ mean: "x", sd: "xSe" });
const y = dist.normal({ mean: "y", sd: "ySe" });
chart(points)
  .flow(sample({ n: 30 }), scatter({ x, y }))
  .mark(circle({ r: 3, fill: "group", opacity: 0.3 }));
Python
x = dist.normal(mean="x", sd="xSe")
y = dist.normal(mean="y", sd="ySe")
chart(points).flow(sample(n=30), scatter(x=x, y=y)).mark(
    circle(r=3, fill="group", opacity=0.3)
)

Uncertain forecast

Ten forecast steps, each a normal whose sd grows with the horizon, as in the fable example in ggdibbler's how-to guide. ggdibbler joins draw k at every step into one line.

ggdibbler
forecast |>
  mutate(value = dist_normal(mean, sd)) |>
  ggplot(aes(x = h, y = value)) +
  geom_line_sample(times = 30, alpha = 0.3)
GoFish, proposed
const value = dist.normal({ mean: "mean", sd: "sd" });
chart(forecast)
  .flow(
    sample({ n: 30 }),
    group({ by: "draw" }),
    scatter({ by: "h", x: "h", y: value })
  )
  .mark(line({ opacity: field("draw").share() }));

Today, on the precomputed draws (story ForecastLines):

chart(forecastDraws)
  .flow(group({ by: "draw" }), scatter({ by: "h", x: "h", y: "value" }))
  .mark(line({ stroke: "#333", strokeWidth: 1.5, opacity: 0.3, curve: Curve.linear() }));
Python
value = dist.normal(mean="mean", sd="sd")
chart(forecast).flow(
    sample(n=30), group(by="draw"), scatter(by="h", x="h", y=value)
).mark(line(opacity=field("draw").share()))

This is the multiline chart with the draw as the series. It follows LineEndLabels in packages/gofish-graphics/stories/forwardsyntax/LabelPlacement.stories.tsx, which splits by symbol, then scatters by date, then draws a line. Here group({ by: "draw" }) splits the rows into one line chart per draw and lays the draws over each other in one frame, as the Today code does. Each line stays inside one draw, so the checker allows it. Without the split, one line would run across all 30 draws, and that is the error. The image uses opacity 0.3, as ggdibbler does. field("draw").share() would give 1/30.

There is one caveat. Each step here is its own normal, and sample draws each step on its own, so draw 3 at one step has no tie to draw 3 at the next. The lines show the steps moving independently, which the data does not say. So they zigzag more than real forecast paths would (compare the shared draws in Part 2). ggdibbler draws the same lines. Open question 1 asks whether that needs a warning.

Uncertain map, subdivided

An 8 by 6 grid of square regions stands in for a map. Each region holds a normal whose sd grows to the east. Sixteen draws fill a 4 by 4 grid in each region, as in Vizumap's pixel map. The noisy eastern regions look grainy.

ggdibbler
tiles_sf |>
  mutate(value = dist_normal(mean, sd)) |>
  ggplot() +
  geom_sf_sample(aes(geometry = geometry, fill = value),
    times = 16, linewidth = 0
  ) +
  geom_sf(aes(geometry = geometry), fill = NA, colour = "white", linewidth = 0.7) +
  scale_fill_distiller(palette = "Blues", direction = 1) +
  theme_void()
GoFish, proposed
const value = dist.normal({ mean: "mean", sd: "sd" });
chart(tiles, { color: Color.gradient(["#f7fbff", "#6baed6", "#08306b"]) })
  .flow(
    table({ by: { x: "col", y: "row" }, spacing: 2 }),
    sample({ n: 16 }),
    table({ by: "draw", numCols: 4, spacing: 0 })
  )
  .mark(rect({ fill: value }));
Python
value = dist.normal(mean="mean", sd="sd")
chart(tiles, color=Color.gradient(["#f7fbff", "#6baed6", "#08306b"])).flow(
    table(by={"x": "col", "y": "row"}, spacing=2),
    sample(n=16),
    table(by="draw", num_cols=4, spacing=0),
).mark(rect(fill=value))

Needs: a single-key table({ by, numCols }) in flow form, which the proposal already lists. A real map also needs a way to cut a polygon into cells, as position_subdivide does. GoFish has none. Here the regions are squares, so a grid is enough.

Part 2. Beyond ggdibbler today

Each card is a plot that ggdibbler cannot make yet. Most come from an open ggdibbler issue. One follows from ggdibbler's core idea. Each shows the GoFish render, the proposed spec, and what ggdibbler would need.

Correlated x and y

ggdibbler #31 ggdibbler #9 #31 asks for joint distributions, starting with the x and y of one point. #9 left this as a later task. Today ggdibbler draws x and y independently.

GoFish, proposed
const p = dist.mvnormal({
  mean: { x: "x", y: "y" },
  sd: { x: "sdX", y: "sdY" },
  cor: "rho",
});
chart(points)
  .flow(sample({ n: 40 }), scatter({ x: p.x, y: p.y }))
  .mark(circle({ r: 2.5, fill: "cor", opacity: 0.35 }));

Eight points, each a bivariate normal with correlation 0.85, −0.85 or 0. p.x and p.y are the two halves of one joint Dist, so sample draws them together and each cloud tilts. ggdibbler would need: a joint distribution mapped to two aesthetics and drawn once for both. In #31, Harriet notes that a multivariate normal in distributional can already hold the across-column case.

Shared draws across rows

ggdibbler #31 ggdibbler #22 #31 discusses correlation between rows, such as a forecast path, and suggests a seed or ID that links two distributions.

GoFish, proposed
// paths: one row per draw and step, from a model's simulated sample paths
chart(paths, { schema: { value: { draws: "draw" } } })
  .flow(group({ by: "draw" }), scatter({ by: "h", x: "h", y: "value" }))
  .mark(line({ opacity: field("draw").share() }));

Thirty random walk paths. Each step has the same normal as in the Part 1 forecast, but the steps of one path move together, so every line is a possible future. As in Part 1, group splits by draw, so each line stays inside one draw. Here the draws are shared, so the lines do not zigzag as Part 1's do. The image uses opacity 0.3. field("draw").share() would give 1/30. ggdibbler would need: a way to say that rows share draws. Its seed argument (#39) keeps the same draws across layers, not across rows. #31 also argues the other side: that rows should stay independent, and a dependent table should be split into a fixed part and a random part. Open question 1.

Uncertain dates on a time axis

ggdibbler #49 #49 asks for nested scales beyond the continuous and discrete ones, "so you can have random time objects and stuff".

GoFish, proposed
// milestones: [{ milestone, date, sd }], with sd a duration in milliseconds
const date = dist.normal({ mean: "date", sd: "sd" }); // Dist<Instant>
chart(milestones, { schema: { date: Unit.time(), sd: Unit.duration } })
  .flow(
    spread({ by: "milestone", dir: "y" }),
    sample({ n: 30 }),
    scatter({ x: date })
  )
  .mark(circle({ r: 3, opacity: field("draw").share() }));
ggdibbler today
# date: one dist_sample of Dates per milestone
ggplot(milestones, aes(x = date, y = milestone)) +
  geom_point_sample(times = 30, alpha = 0.25)

Five project milestones whose dates are uncertain, thirty draws each, on a date axis. The proposal types dist.normal over an Instant with its sd as a duration. Unit.time() and Unit.duration exist today, and the date axis is today's. scatter puts the draws of a milestone on one row by itself, so no group step is needed. The image uses opacity 0.25. ggdibbler today: dist_normal does not take a Date, and a dist_sample of dates is drawn on a continuous scale, so the axis shows day numbers (the ggdibbler image, from the same draws). ggdibbler would need: the nested scale for time that #49 describes.

Two levels of uncertainty

ggdibbler #55 #55 proposes a dibble() wrapper for any layer. A comment there asks to nest it, dibble(dibble(plot, times = 10), times = 50), for scenario uncertainty on top of model uncertainty.

GoFish, proposed
const mu = dist.normal({ mean: "mean", sd: "tau" });
const value = dist.normal({ mean: mu, sd: "se" }); // Dist<Dist<number>>
chart(estimates)
  .flow(
    spread({ by: "category", dir: "x" }),
    sample({ n: 8 }), // draws the outer level, mu
    spread({ by: "draw", dir: "x" }),
    sample({ n: 10, as: "inner" }), // draws the inner level
    scatter({ y: value })
  )
  .mark(circle({ r: 2.5, opacity: field("inner").share() }));

Three estimates. The mean of each is itself uncertain (sd tau), and each value scatters around that mean (sd se). Eight outer draws are dodged, and each holds ten inner draws, which scatter draws in one column. A wide spread of column centers means model uncertainty. Tall columns mean noise within the model. Needs: the proposal shows one level per sample only (the jk-CDF in the Proposal tab, where a cdf mark reads the inner level). Two sample steps need a rule that the first draws the outer level and the second the inner, and a name for the second draw key. as was in an earlier draft and was dropped. ggdibbler would need: the nested dibble() from #55.

Uncertain treemap

natural extension A treemap's layout depends on every value, so one draw moves every tile. Placing the draws with the layout operator is the same move as ggdibbler's nested positions. The README lists "integration with the ggplot2 extension ecosystem" as a plan, and #55's dibble() would wrap an extension layer such as treemapify.

GoFish, proposed
const value = dist.normal({ mean: "mean", sd: "se" });
chart(budget)
  .flow(
    sample({ n: 6 }),
    spread({ by: "draw", dir: "x" }),
    treemap({ by: "item", size: value })
  )
  .mark(rect({ fill: "item" }));

Six budget lines, each a normal, six draws side by side. Swapping spread for time.sequence({ by: "draw" }) gives a HOP. The image clamps each draw at 0.5, because a size cannot be negative. Needs: a positive Dist for sizes, such as a truncated normal, which the proposal does not have. ggdibbler would need: a draw position for a layout extension. Today sample_expand with group = drawID gets the draws to treemapify, but not a nested position.

Open questions for Harriet

  1. Lines over independent draws. ggdibbler and the proposal both join draw k at each x into one line per draw. With independent cells, the lines show the steps moving independently, a joint the data does not give. Your comment on #31 argues that the unit of an uncertainty visualization is an independent distribution. For a fable forecast, where each step is a marginal, should the line carry a warning or a note in the docs, or is it fine as it is?
  2. Default opacity. ggdibbler leaves alpha to the user and warns about times (#59). In the proposal the mark sets the opacity, for example opacity: field("draw").share(), which is 1/n. Should 1/n instead be a default for marks under a draw key, and should the legend then use full opacity by itself (#67)?
  3. Blending. Alpha compositing favors the last draw. Would an order-free blend, as in ggblend (#48), be a better default for draws drawn in one frame?
  4. Subdividing regions. GoFish cannot cut a polygon into cells yet. Of the grid, random points with Voronoi cells (#16) and a noise fill (#23), which would you build first?
  5. Signal and noise in one system. #40 settled on samples only, partly because quantiles do not extend to several random variables. The proposal also has marks that read the whole distribution. Does having both in one system risk the mix-up that the philosophy vignette warns about?

Harriet's answers (4 November)

Harriet answered questions 1 to 4 by email. Question 5 is still open. She also sent the final version of the ggdibbler paper, which is due out in JCGS in the next few days. Each answer below is a short summary of hers, then what it means for the proposal.

  1. Lines over independent draws. Her answer. A forecast like y_t = a·y_(t−1) + e_t has one random part, e_t, and those parts are independent. fable gives the distribution of y_t instead, which depends on y_(t−1). Asking users to rebuild the model from a and e_t is not reasonable, and having ggdibbler assume the conditional structure would be a bad default. The smooth ribbons of ggdist do not come from a correct joint either: they come from the false structure of quantiles. The paper's geom_abline example (Section 4.2.4, Figure 6) is narrower than this. A quantile band of one random input is fine. The false structure appears when the quantiles of two random inputs are paired by level: the lines then all cross at the wrong point. She puts the problem in the data structure. ggdibbler wants a distribution of tables (one whole table per draw), but distributional gives a table of distributions (one distribution per cell). Going from the second to the first needs assumptions such as independence. The two differ in what you condition on: the draw ID or the distribution ID. That choice also sets what the plot tends to as the uncertainty goes to zero: a rug for a table of distributions (ggdist), or the plain density for a distribution of tables (ggdibbler). Figure 3 of the final paper shows the two limits. The words about conditioning are from her email, not the paper. Her preferred fix is that ggdibbler takes a distribution of tables and warns about independence when it gets a table of distributions. Today a table of distributions is the only input, so it would warn every time. Her second choice is that distributional records the dependence between cells and draws from it.
    What it means for GoFish. The proposal already takes both forms. A table of distributions is a column of Dist values that sample draws (the forecast card in Part 1). A distribution of tables is rows that already carry a draw column (the shared paths card in Part 2). So GoFish can do what Harriet would like ggdibbler to do, and the warning fires only on the case that needs it. A possible rule: sample on independent cells gives each cell its own draws. A mark that joins rows along the draw key, such as a line grouped by draw, then claims a joint that the data does not give. That gets a lint, and the fix is to supply joint draws, either as a draw column or as a joint Dist over the rows. Her point about conditioning maps onto group: grouping by draw reads one drawn table at a time (ggdibbler), and grouping by the row key reads each cell's distribution (ggdist). This is the same kind of check as the stability rule: drawn data flowing into a structure the picture shows.
    After reading the final paper and UASTL. The rule is too narrow if it fires only on lines. The general test is whether one draw group holds draws from more than one source cell. A line through draw k does, but so does the density of each drawn table (the paper's own Figure 1c), a stack total per draw, a smooth (Figure 7) or a treemap. UASTL (Krake et al. 2025) shows the harm reaches the numbers: an STL decomposition run on independent draws averages white noise, so the trend band comes out too narrow and the residual too wide. A quantile level used as a joining key is the same error with a perfectly positive joint in place of an independent one. Applied in full, the test would flag most ggdibbler style specs, which is Harriet's "it would warn every time". So a better form may be for sample to state the independence assumption once, where it is made, rather than a lint on each use. Decisions for Josh: state it at sample or lint each use (either way not an error, since the paper treats independence as a stated default), and whether a joint Dist over rows (her second choice, and the direction Section 6 of the paper plans) belongs in the first version.
  2. Default opacity. Her answer. A default position adjustment is planned. She plans to rebuild ggdibbler on a custom ggplot_build(), and she thinks those limits are specific to R and ggplot2. The legend should use full opacity, because it stands for one unit of data, one cell of the table, and should not change with n. But opacity does not add, so the draws do not add up to the legend color. That is one reason she prefers subdividing a region: there the legend stays tied to the data.
    What it means for GoFish. This supports the limits tab. group({ by: "draw", blend: "mean" }) draws each draw at full opacity and averages them. When the variance goes to zero, every draw is the same color, so the average is the legend color. For position and text the legend then needs no special case: it shows one cell at full opacity, and the picture converges to it. For fills this holds only in the limit. Until then the mean of the drawn colors is usually not a palette color (answer 3). The field("draw").share() opacity and its 0.05 floor go away, as the limits tab already proposes.
  3. Blending. Her answer. She is not sure. ggblend treats color as paint, where mixing every color gives black. Optics treats it as light, where mixing every color gives white. Her work on bias and variance suggests the honest result is an area in color space, but she does not know what that area should look like. She does not think alpha is right, though the order problem matters less in ggdibbler because every layer comes from the same distribution.
    What it means for GoFish. The mean blend is neither paint nor light. It is an average, so it stays inside the colors that were drawn and does not depend on order. It shows the expected color, and so it collapses her "area" to one point, its center. Showing the area itself would need a second channel, such as a texture, or a subdivision (answer 4).
    After reading the final paper and the Annual Review. Figure 9d of the paper shows that transparency on a color-mapped Dist gives a color that is not on the palette and cannot be read from the legend. A mean blend fixes the order bias but not this. The Annual Review adds that averaging color draws in one place tends to the mean map, its "ignoring uncertainty" failure. Both depend on the shape of the palette's path through the space where the blend is done. The draws are points along that path, and their mean is the center of Harriet's "area". On a straight path, such as a lightness ramp, which is nearly straight in OKLab, the mean stays on the path and reads as the color of the mean value, so the spread disappears. On a curved path, where the hue turns, the mean falls inside the bend toward gray. In OKLab, hue is an angle and chroma a radius, so the mean is the circular mean: the hue averages, and the chroma shrinks by how tightly the hues cluster. Wild hues give gray. That works like a value-suppressing palette, but it comes out of the math instead of a direct mapping. It is still off the palette, so the legend cannot decode it.
    Proposed: average in OKLab, which does the circular mean for free (linear RGB mixes like light and brightens). Lint a blended fill whose draws differ only in color, and suggest spreading the draws in space (subdivide or dodge) or in time, which Section 4.3.1 of the paper recommends for color. Decision for Josh: whether a curved palette makes the blended fill acceptable, with a legend that shows blends.
  4. Subdividing regions. Her answer. Voronoi cells first, or a hexagonal grid; ggincerta implements several subdivisions. She would probably never build the noise fill, because it maps uncertainty straight onto an aesthetic, and section 5.3 of the paper argues that no aesthetic should be mapped that way. She left #16 and #23 open in case she changes her mind.
    What it means for GoFish. Main now has Bin.hex and Bin.voronoi, which cut the plane into polygons, and the region operators from #1229 and #1287, which intersect regions. Cutting a map region into cells could be the croissant pattern: intersect the region with a hex or Voronoi partition, then color each cell by one draw. That needs a check against a real map before it is promised. Her view on noise fill matches the limits tab, which has no blur, fuzz or fade channel. Section 5.3 of the final paper is more hedged than her email. It says direct mapping "is not unreasonable, and it might be possible to implement in the future", and that samples are "the safest approach" for now. Section 6 leaves the question open. That fits the theory tab's verified direct renders better than a flat ban.
  5. Signal and noise in one system. No answer from Harriet yet. The final paper answers part of it. Section 4.2.6 says ggdist and ggdibbler are different visual functions and "both packages are required if we want a complete picture". The mix-ups it warns about are specific: a summary drawn next to samples makes readers ignore the uncertainty (4.2.2), a whole-distribution value fed into a statistic leaves the data's domain (4.2.3), and quantile levels used as joints (4.2.4). For GoFish this means keeping the outputs of interval, slab, cdf and dots terminal, so they never feed a split, a stack or a sum. It also means rule 1's error text for a Dist in rect h should offer "draw it" first, and say that "show it as an interval" makes a different plot, whose limit is a rug.

Three papers read (4 November)

Josh shared three PDFs: the final JCGS version of the ggdibbler paper (dated 31 October 2026), UASTL (Krake, Klötzl, Hägele and Weiskopf, IEEE TVCG 2025), and an Annual Review article by Harriet's group. What each adds beyond the answers above follows.

ggdibbler, final version

  • The figures are renumbered. A raster figure (5) and the geom_abline figure (6) are new, and the proof about just noticeable differences moved to Appendix 10.2. The theory tab's citations are updated.
  • Section 4.3.1 puts the draw positions on four axes: x is dodge, y is stack, z is transparency and t is animation. A HOP, a pixel map and a VSUP are the same plot placed by animation, subdivision and transparency. A facet, and the lineup protocol, place draws between plots. In GoFish these are spread, stack, group with a blend, and time.sequence, with table between plots.
  • Every render is random (Section 5.3), so GoFish should seed by default and pick n so that two renders look alike. ggdibbler's default is 10.
  • Figure 3 gives a test to run in CI: every sample-based story must render the plain chart when every sd is 0.

UASTL: a joint Gaussian over a whole series

UASTL treats a time series as one joint normal over all its steps, with a covariance matrix across time built from kernels or estimated from an ensemble. Non-robust STL is linear, so the paper carries the normal through exactly. Robust STL is not, and it falls back to sampling. It draws a mean line, 50% and 99% pointwise bands, and 8 to 12 sample paths, some in distinct colors so the eye can follow one draw across the trend, seasonal and residual panels. It shows correlation between time steps three ways: a heat map, one column of the correlation matrix drawn as a strip under the plot (experts liked this one best), and a correlation length per step. Figure 3 is the key picture for answer 1: three kernels with the same variance give identical bands and very different paths.

For GoFish it argues for a joint Dist over rows as a value of its own, such as dist.mvnormal({ mean, along: "t", cov }) with a Kernel family whose members can be added. The covariance matrix cannot live in a column. Sampling stays the meaning, and exact propagation through a linear step is a later speedup for operators that expose their matrix. Also missing: a correlation summary across draws, and a way for two charts to reuse the same seeds, which the paper's sensitivity view needs.

"The Noisy Work of Uncertainty Visualization"

Mason, Cook, Goodwin, Tanaka and VanderPlas, Annual Review of Statistics and Its Application (2027). The thesis is signal modulation: a display should keep a strong signal and let a weak one disappear, in proportion to the confidence. It sorts displays into four approaches. Ignoring uncertainty fails. Uncertainty as a statistic (a variance map, an exceedance map) is not uncertainty visualization, because the uncertainty becomes the signal. Uncertainty as a variable (line width from the SE, a point and interval, VSUP) fails. Uncertainty as a distribution (spaghetti, pixel map, quantile dots, HOPs) succeeds, and that is the proposal's sample path.

  • Names. In the article, "uncertainty as signal" names a failure. Its dividing line is "two separate statistics" against "one integrated distribution", not samples against whole distributions: the quantile dot plot succeeds and the point with interval fails, though both are ggdist marks. The proposal should stop calling its two paths signal and noise.
  • Variability is not uncertainty. Rows folded into a Dist are variability; a draws key marks uncertainty. The docs should say which is which.
  • Shared domains. Figure 4 shows a signal seeming to vanish only because two maps use different color domains. Domains taken from draws are wider than domains taken from means, so a low and high uncertainty pair must share one.
  • Not yet spelled in GoFish: an exceedance map (d.prob(x > 27) on a continuous Dist), a two-variable color scale and VSUP, curves from parameter draws evaluated over an x grid, a pixel map clipped to real county outlines, a shadow for missing values (naniar), and lineups.
  • Lineups. A lineup is several panels, each one draw of a whole table from a null model, with the real data hidden at a random slot. That is a distribution of tables placed with table({ by: "draw" }). It needs a null generator (such as permute), a way to place the real table as one draw, and a way to hide the answer. The article proposes lineups as a test of signal modulation, which fits design question 10.
  • Prior work our review lacks: Buja et al. 2009 (lineup and Rorschach protocols), Majumder, Hofmann and Cook 2013 (the power of lineups), Kinkeldey, MacEachren and Schiewe 2014 (intrinsic and extrinsic displays), Correll and Gleicher 2014 ("Error bars considered harmful"), Kale et al. 2018 (HOPs against overlaid draws, our z against t question), Lucchesi et al. 2021 (Vizumap pixel maps), Hullman and Gelman 2021 (graphical inference), Tierney and Cook 2023 (naniar).

Decisions these add

  1. Independence: state it once at sample, or lint each use where one draw group spans several cells.
  2. A joint Dist over rows (dist.mvnormal with a Kernel family) in the first version or later.
  3. Blended fills: average in OKLab, and lint when draws differ only in color on a straight palette; whether a curved palette with a blend legend is acceptable.
  4. Rename the two paths away from signal and noise.
  5. Seed by default, and add the zero-sd convergence check to CI.

Theory: continuity, symmetry and types for distribution-valued data (#980)

This page is research, not a plan. It reads the ggdibbler paper (Mason, Cook, Goodwin and VanderPlas, "A Mathematical Framework and Software Implementation for Uncertainty Visualisation", arXiv:2606.24217, June 2026) against GoFish's type system and the proposal in proposal.md and types.md. It asks three questions. Can the paper's continuous mapping theorem become a type rule, and how does it relate to Kindlmann and Scheidegger's algebraic design? How does the proposal answer each hurdle in the paper's Section 4? Can types help render blur directly instead of by sampling? It lays out options, marks the ones that feel strongest, and ends with open questions.

Every claim about GoFish cites a file and line. Paths are relative to the repository root. Code paths are on this branch, which is main after #1237.

Summary

  • The continuous mapping theorem (CMT) and Kindlmann and Scheidegger (K–S) are two halves of one idea, but not the same idea. K–S asks that the picture respect the data's symmetries, as an exact equation. CMT asks that the picture respect the data's topology, as a limit. Both say "the visual mapping is a structure-preserving map". In category theory terms, K–S says the encoding is a natural transformation between two group actions, and CMT says the probability functor sends continuous maps to continuous maps. These two phrasings are exact. Calling CMT "the K–S square for distributions" is only an analogy, because CMT is a continuity property and not a commuting square. One square does hold exactly: the point mass is natural, so the uncertain plot of a certain value is the certain plot.
  • Most of GoFish's existing checks are exact K–S instances. The CanAdd check, the unit clash, the instant-has-no-sum rule and the proposed stack-by-draw error are each a hallucinator ruled out for a named data symmetry. Unit inference is Kennedy's units of measure, so its invariance is Kennedy's free theorem. Two checks fit only loosely, and one does not fit at all.
  • One new rule unifies the draw checks. A placement of draws is meaningful when it is a function of the distribution. That holds when the picture does not change if you permute the draws or repeat every draw the same number of times. Stacking by draw fails the second test, a mean passes it, and an additive overlay passes both. This is the "colimit of finite samples" construction of Fritz and Perrone.
  • Section 4's hurdles mostly fall out of GoFish. Nested positions, per-draw statistics and the single surface (no *_sample suffix) come from GoFish's existing nested splits. Stacking draws, quantiles on categories and domain mismatches need the proposal's new rules. Three things stay open: nonlinear scales under a density, color mixing under overlay, and two uncertain channels that want different placements.
  • Types can make the direct blur safe. The direct render is the limit of the sampled render, so its kernel is the pushforward of the distribution through the whole visual mapping, not through the mark alone. A type that tracks whether that path is affine, monotone or discontinuous decides when a closed form exists. Two concrete problems in the proposal came out of this analysis. An opacity floor for draws drawn in one frame, as the earlier draft's max(0.05, 1/n), stops the picture from converging as the number of draws grows. And the proposal's two fades for the uncertain timeline converge to different pictures unless the blend mode is chosen to match.

Terms

  • Metric space. A set with a distance d(a, b) that is zero only for equal points, is symmetric, and obeys the triangle inequality.
  • Continuous map. A map that sends points that are close enough to points that are as close as you like. Lipschitz with constant L means d(f(a), f(b)) ≤ L·d(a, b), a quantitative form of continuity.
  • Convergence in distribution. Random values X_n converge to X when every bounded continuous summary of X_n converges to the same summary of X. Shrinking a normal's standard deviation to 0 makes it converge to its mean.
  • Continuous mapping theorem (CMT). If X_n converges to X and f is continuous (except on a set that X lands in with probability 0), then f(X_n) converges to f(X) (Mann and Wald 1943).
  • Pushforward. For a map f and a distribution μ, f_*μ is the distribution of f(X) when X has distribution μ. It is what a scale does to a distribution (the paper's Definition 4.1).
  • Wasserstein distance W1. The least average distance you must move probability mass to turn one distribution into another. It makes distributions a metric space.
  • Category, functor, natural transformation. A category has objects and arrows that compose. A functor maps one category to another and keeps composition. A natural transformation η between two functors F and G is a family of arrows η_x such that every square G(f) ∘ η_x = η_y ∘ F(f) commutes.
  • Group action and equivariance. A group acts on a set when each group element moves the points, and composing elements composes moves. A map v is equivariant when moving then mapping equals mapping then moving.
  • Probability monad (Giry monad). The functor P that sends a space to the space of distributions on it and a map f to f_*, with pure(x) = δ_x (all mass at x) and bind (draw, then draw again). Giry (1982) defined it on measurable spaces. Fritz and Perrone (2019) defined the Kantorovich monad on complete metric spaces with Lipschitz-1 maps, using W1.
  • Homomorphism. A map that keeps an operation: e(a + b) = e(a) ⊕ e(b).
  • Parametricity and free theorems. A function that is generic in a type cannot look inside values of that type, so it obeys laws for free (Reynolds 1983, Wadler 1989).
  • Sensitivity type. A type that records how much a function can amplify a change in its input, as in Fuzz (Reed and Pierce 2010).
  • Refinement type. A type with a logical predicate attached, such as "an affine map" (Liquid types: Rondon, Kawaguchi and Jhala 2008).
  • Effect system. A type system that records what a computation does besides return a value, such as drawing random numbers (Lucassen and Gifford 1988).

Which formal tool fits which question

Question Formal tool Fit GoFish hook

A channel needs a trait, and the encoding must keep it Typeclass (trait) laws and homomorphisms (Mackinlay 1986; Bartonicek et al. 2025) exact for laws checkSummable, measure.ts:142
