Q1. The continuous mapping theorem and algebraic visualization design

1.1 What the paper's theorem says, exactly

The paper writes a plot as V = A ∘ O ∘ G ∘ S ∘ M (Definition 3.2): scales M, statistic S, geometry G (a geometry E after a position adjustment P), coordinates O, and aesthetics A. With random cells, S becomes "sample t draws, then S" and P becomes a within-sample adjustment followed by a between-sample one (Definitions 4.2 to 4.4). The paper then asks that V obey the CMT (Theorem 3.1). The metric on pictures is pulled back through A, so A is an isometry by definition (Section 3.2.1). The real content is that O ∘ G ∘ S ∘ M is continuous.

Two different convergences hide under the one name "visual convergence".

  1. Shrinking uncertainty. As a cell's variance goes to 0, its distribution converges to its value, and the uncertain plot should converge to the plain plot (Section 3.3). This is the CMT. It needs V continuous at the limit point.
  2. Growing samples. For a fixed distribution, t draws drawn on top of each other should look the same on every render once t is large (Section 5.3). This is a law of large numbers for the empirical distribution of the draws. It needs the way draws are placed and blended to have a limit as t grows.

GoFish should name and test these separately. The first constrains the visual mapping. The second constrains the opacity of draws drawn in one frame, sample and the axis extent, and Q3 shows that the proposal currently fails it in two places.

1.2 Kindlmann and Scheidegger, and its relatives

Kindlmann and Scheidegger, "An Algebraic Process for Visualization Design", IEEE TVCG 20(12), 2014, write data D, representations R, and pictures V, with r: D → R and v: R → V. Their equation (1) is a commuting square:

        r1        v
   D ──────▶ R ──────▶ V
   │                   │
 α │                   │ ω            v ∘ r2 ∘ α  =  ω ∘ v ∘ r1
   ▼                   ▼
   D ──────▶ R ──────▶ V
        r2        v

A data symmetry α must match a visual symmetry ω. Three principles follow. Representation Invariance: α = 1 must give ω = 1, and a failure is a hallucinator. Unambiguous Data Depiction: ω = 1 must give α = 1, and a failure is a confuser. Visual-Data Correspondence: important α match obvious ω, and the failures are jumblers and misleaders.

The relatives, and how each relates:

  • Stevens (1946), "On the theory of scales of measurement", Science 103. A scale type is defined by its group of allowed transformations (permutations, monotone maps, affine maps, scalings). A statistic is permissible when it commutes with that group. K–S's α are exactly these groups. Velleman and Wilkinson (1993, The American Statistician 47(1)) is the standard critique of using the four types as a fixed list, which matches GoFish's choice of composable traits over a fixed list (apps/docs/docs/internals/design/distributions.md:45).
  • Mackinlay (1986), "Automating the design of graphical presentations of relational information", ACM TOG 5(2). Expressiveness: the picture expresses all the facts in the data and only those. GoFish already cites this as its type principle (apps/docs/docs/internals/design/api-design.md:72).
  • Ziemkiewicz and Kosara (2009), "Embedding information visualization within visual representation". Readability: each visual element corresponds to a distinct data element.
  • Vickers, Faith and Rossiter (2013), "Understanding visualization: a formal approach using category theory and semiotics", IEEE TVCG 19(6).
  • Demiralp, Bernstein and Heer (2014), "Learning perceptual kernels for visualization design", IEEE TVCG 20(12). K–S call distance-based design "a quantitative specialization of our Correspondence Principle" and note that metrics cannot express everything, since partial orders are not symmetric. The CMT sits on this metric side.
  • McNutt and Kindlmann (2018), "Linting for visualization: towards a practical automated visualization guidance system", VisGuides workshop. A matplotlib linter whose rules come from algebraic design. Chen et al.'s VizLinter (IEEE TVCG 2022) is a later linter. Wongsuphasawat et al.'s CompassQL and Voyager (2016) encode expressiveness as rules for recommendation. I could not find a visualization-linting paper by Ostrander, so it is left out.
  • Correll, Li, Kindlmann and Scheidegger (2019), "Looks good to me: visualizations as sanity checks", IEEE TVCG 25(1). It tests whether data flaws (an α such as duplicated rows or missing values) produce visible changes (an ω). This is Unambiguity tested on people.
  • McNutt, Kindlmann and Correll (2020), "Surfacing visualization mirages", CHI. It uses metamorphic testing: perturb the data or the spec, and check the picture changes as it should. This is a test that a K–S square commutes.
  • Bartonicek, Urbanek and Murrell (2025), "No more, no less than sum of its parts", JCGS 34(3). A stacked plot is valid when its statistic is a monoid homomorphism.
  • Aizenman, Vejdemo-Johansson, Caswell and Grossberg (2026), "Mind your νs and Qs: a categorical framework for specifying visualization components", GUGOG-VIS workshop (PDF). Data are fiber bundles (a base space for connectivity and a fiber for field values), containers are sheaves, and an "Artist" is structure-preserving when it is a natural transformation: actions on data and graphic are equivariant, so changes to data and graphic commute (their Section 5). They split an artist into an encoder ν per field and a compositor Q. Their stated goal is K–S's three principles applied to library architecture rather than to design. Their example in Figure 5 is a Dirichlet distribution whose color map commutes with binning.

Aizenman et al. sit closest to this page. They say "natural transformation" where K–S say "commuting diagram", and they apply it to components, as GoFish would. Two differences matter for GoFish. They model connectivity (lines, surfaces) as topology on the base space, which GoFish has as relational marks and along. And their fibers carry field structure, which GoFish has as column traits. Neither paper treats distributions as values, so the CMT is not in either.

1.3 Phrasing "a natural transformation between the morphisms", precisely

Josh's tentative phrasing was "some natural transformation between the morphisms, or some equality between the typeclasses". Here are three precise versions, with a verdict on each.

Phrasing A: the encoding is natural with respect to the symmetries (exact). A group G is a category with one object, whose arrows are the group elements. A group acting on a set is a functor from that category to sets. Take the group of data symmetries G acting on D, and let it act on V through a matching φ: G → G_V that sends each α to its ω. Then K–S's equation (1) with r1 = r2 is exactly the naturality square, and the encoding v ∘ r is the natural transformation:

   D ──v──▶ V
   │        │
 α │        │ φ(α) = ω         v(α · x) = φ(α) · v(x)
   ▼        ▼
   D ──v──▶ V

So the natural transformation is not between the morphisms. The morphisms are the symmetries α and ω, and the encoding is the transformation. When the α and ω are invertible and the solving pairs form a group, the three principles become statements about φ:

  • Invariance: φ is a function (each α has one ω, and 1 goes to 1).
  • Unambiguity: φ is injective (only α = 1 goes to ω = 1).
  • Correspondence: φ is an isomorphism onto the visual symmetries a viewer reads.

GoFish example, a centered Likert stack. The data symmetry is "reverse the order of the levels about the midpoint" (agree swaps with disagree). The visual symmetry is "reflect the bar about the center line". A stack centered on a HasMidpoint column (packages/gofish-graphics/src/ast/graphicalOperators/spread.tsx:246 to :275) is equivariant for this pair. A stack from the left edge is not: reversing the levels moves a different segment to the baseline, and no reflection matches it.

GoFish example, a draw spread. spread({ by: "draw", dir: "x" }) is equivariant for the pair "permute the draws" and "permute the thin bars". It is not invariant: the picture moves. Section 1.5 shows that this matters, and when sorting the draws fixes it.

Phrasing B: the encoding is a homomorphism for each trait the channel needs (exact for the algebra, with one correction). A trait is a set of operations with laws. A channel needs some traits, and the data type provides some. The precise statement is not "equality between the typeclasses". It is two inclusions and a law:

  • Only the facts (no misleader): every trait the channel's reading implies is a trait the data type has. A bar length is read as a ratio, so it needs CanAdd and an origin. A temperature has neither, so a bar of temperatures is a misleader.
  • All the facts (no jumbler): every trait the task needs from the data is kept by the channel. An ordered type drawn with unordered hues loses its order.
  • The law: for each kept trait, the encoding is a homomorphism. For CanAdd, that is e(a + b) = e(a) ⊕ e(b) and e(0) = 0.

The first two are what a type checker does: instance resolution. The third is a law that a type checker cannot see. It is checked by construction (GoFish's stack really adds lengths) or by property tests. Each trait also determines its group of symmetries, the maps that keep its operations. That is the bridge between Phrasing B and Phrasing A, and it is Stevens' idea: a trait set and its symmetry group are two descriptions of one thing. So "equality between the typeclasses up to the encoding" is Mackinlay's expressiveness, and it is the same as K–S Correspondence restricted to the symmetries those traits generate.

GoFish example, stack as a monoid homomorphism. stack lays its parts end to end and adds their amounts (packages/gofish-graphics/src/ast/constraints/distribute.ts:340 to :361). It is a homomorphism from the data's (+, 0) to the picture's "concatenate lengths, empty length". checkSummable refuses the stack when the unit has no CanAdd (packages/gofish-graphics/src/ast/measure.ts:142 to :162), which is the "only the facts" inclusion.

Phrasing C: the CMT is the same square for the probability functor (partly exact, partly analogy). Three statements are exact.

  1. Functoriality. The uncertain version of a visual mapping v is P(v) = v_*. Because P is a functor, (w ∘ v)_* = w_* ∘ v_*. So the paper's V = A ∘ O ∘ G* ∘ S* ∘ M acting on a random matrix is the pushforward through each stage in turn, which is the paper's "change of variable" for scales (Definition 4.1) applied to every stage.

  2. The point mass is natural. v_*(δ_x) = δ_{v(x)}. This is a commuting square:

      D ──v──▶ V
  │        │
δ │        │ δ          the uncertain plot of a certain value is the certain plot
  ▼        ▼
 P D ─v_*─▶ P V

    This is the paper's remark that deterministic graphics are the special case where every cell is degenerate (Section 4.4). In GoFish it is pure in the proposal: "a plain value is a Dist with all its mass at one point" (proposal.md:371 to :373).

  3. Equivariance lifts. If v is natural for a symmetry (Phrasing A), then v_* is natural for the same symmetry, because P maps commuting squares to commuting squares. So every hallucinator GoFish rules out for plain data is ruled out for distribution data with no extra check. The design note says this informally ("the unit rules from #773 carry over without change", apps/docs/docs/internals/design/distributions.md:283 to :288). This is the reason it is true.

The CMT itself is not a commuting square. It says P sends continuous maps to maps that are continuous for convergence in distribution. Its quantitative form is exact and useful: if v is L-Lipschitz then W1(v_*μ, v_*ν) ≤ L · W1(μ, ν). In the Kantorovich monad this is just the statement that P is a functor on metric spaces with Lipschitz maps. For a normal cell with standard deviation s around value x, W1(N(x, s²), δ_x) = s·√(2/π). So a position channel whose scope maps σ pixels per data unit (apps/docs/docs/internals/core/underlying-space.md:110 to :115) moves its mark by at most σ · s · √(2/π) pixels on average. That is a rate of visual convergence, and GoFish already knows σ after layout.

Verdict. Say it this way: the encoding is a natural transformation between the data's symmetry action and the picture's symmetry action (K–S, and Aizenman et al.'s Artists). Its uncertain version is the pushforward, which keeps that naturality because the probability monad is a functor, keeps the point-mass square because the unit of the monad is natural, and keeps convergence because the functor preserves continuous maps (Mann and Wald) and Lipschitz maps (Kantorovich). Phrasing A and the three numbered statements are exact. "CMT is naturality" is an analogy.

1.4 Where the analogy between CMT and K–S breaks

  • A constant picture satisfies the CMT. The CMT forbids jumps. It does not ask that any change be visible. A plot of only the means converges perfectly as the variance shrinks. The paper bans point estimates for this reason (Section 4.2.2), but that ban is K–S Unambiguity, not the CMT: the data symmetry "change the variance, keep the mean" has ω = 1, so it is a confuser. The final paper (Section 4.2.6) says point estimates converge "trivially" to constants "but prevent visual convergence to other random plots", so it frames this as a failure of convergence. The mean map is continuous, so we read it instead as a failure of Kindlmann and Scheidegger's Unambiguity.
  • K–S symmetries are exact and global. CMT perturbations are small and local. A small perturbation is not a symmetry, and small perturbations do not form a group.
  • Discontinuity can be the point. A hypothetical outcome plot (HOP) that sorts bars by each draw's value changes order when two values cross. That map is discontinuous, yet Hullman, Resnick and Adar (2015) show it helps people judge how reliable a ranking is. So "continuous" cannot be a hard rule for every channel. It is a rule for the channels whose reading is a magnitude.
  • The human metric is not a metric. The paper proves that perception with a just noticeable difference breaks the triangle inequality (Proposition 10.1). So the CMT holds in pixel space, and the human side needs a separate threshold, as in Q3's invisibility lint.

1.5 Can the CMT condition be a type rule?

Option 1A: a Continuous trait on each encoding. Mark each operator and field op with whether it is continuous from the data metric to pixels. Every map out of a discrete space is continuous, so categorical keys pass for free. The trouble is that almost nothing interesting is fully continuous. Bins, thresholded color palettes, collision layouts and sorting by value all jump somewhere. A binary trait would reject them all.

Option 1B: three continuity classes, plus a runtime check at the limit point (strongest). The CMT needs continuity only where the limit lands with positive probability (Mann and Wald). So use three classes:

Class Meaning GoFish examples
lipschitz(L) continuous with a known rate a position or size channel in a σ-scope; stack of free parts (1 per part); .mean() (1/n per row); sorting the draws themselves by value (the sorted list is Lipschitz-1)
continuousExcept jumps only on a named thin set .bin() and Bin.step (at cell edges, packages/gofish-graphics/src/ast/fieldExpr.ts:77 to :83); a thresholded color palette; sort({ by }) of category labels by a value (where two values tie, fieldExpr.ts:64 to :74); filter on a value; niceContinuous (at a nice tick, packages/gofish-graphics/src/ast/underlyingSpace.ts:360)
discontinuous jumps on a set of positive size, or depends on the draw count Overlap.separate() collision layout (packages/gofish-graphics/src/families/overlap.ts:50); stack by draw; an axis extent taken from the largest draw

The static check composes classes along the flow. A continuousExcept step then needs a runtime check: is the limit point on the thin set? For shrinking uncertainty the limit is the data value, so the check is "does a value sit exactly on a bin edge or a nice tick". This matters in practice. Rounded data often sits on bin edges, and a percentage of exactly 100 sits on a nice tick. Then the uncertain plot does not converge: draws just above 100 make niceContinuous jump the axis to 120, and the whole chart rescales.

This is Chaudhuri, Gulwani and Lublinerman's continuity analysis ("Continuity analysis of programs", POPL 2010) applied to a chart: data types get metrics, branches are where continuity can fail, and the checker proves continuity piece by piece.

Option 1C: sensitivity types in the style of Fuzz. Reed and Pierce ("Distance makes the types grow stronger", ICFP 2010) type a function as !k τ ⊸ σ, meaning it amplifies changes by at most k. DFuzz (Gaboardi et al., POPL 2013) lets k depend on sizes such as n. This gives exact numbers, and one GoFish rule falls out of it: a fold over the draw key must have sensitivity that does not grow with n. A mean has sensitivity 1/n per draw and 1 in total. A sum has 1 per draw and n in total, so the stacked total of n draws moves n times as far as one draw. That is rule 3's reason (proposal.md:1190 to :1195) in sensitivity terms. The cost is high: a full sensitivity type system is far heavier than GoFish needs. A table of constants per operator, as in 1B's first row, gets most of the value.

Option 1D: draws as a monad, the flow after sample as a Kleisli arrow. This explains the paper's Figure 7 exactly. sample lifts a table into the monad. A statistic S must run inside, as fmap S, and only then are the draws flattened. Running S on the flattened draws (S ∘ join) gives the paper's wrong single smooth with too-small error. The two agree only when S is a homomorphism of the flattening, which for counts and sums means the answer grows with n. GoFish already gets fmap S from its nested splits: a mark or operator after a split runs once per leaf (packages/gofish-graphics/src/ast/marks/createOperator.ts:1218 and :1284 to :1286). So the draw key in a split is exactly fmap. The typing rule to add is "after sample, the draw key must be in the split before any statistic that is not draw-invariant".

Option 1E: sampling as an effect. sample makes everything after it random. An effect system would mark the flow as random until a seed handles the effect, the way an effect handler interprets an effect (Plotkin and Pretnar 2009; for probability, Ścibior, Ghahramani and Gordon, "Practical probabilistic programming with monads", Haskell Symposium 2015). The proposal already gives sample a seed shared by JS and Python (types.md:213 to :216). The effect view adds one useful rule: a chart whose random effect is not handled by a seed is not a pure function of its data, so capture-diff and the parity tests must refuse it. This is cheap to add as a flag and does not need a real effect system.

1.6 The placement test: is a placement of draws a function of the distribution?

This test unifies the draw rules, and it follows from Fritz and Perrone ("A probability monad as the colimit of spaces of finite samples", TAC 34(7), 2019). They build the space of distributions from samples of size n, glued along two moves: permuting a sample, and repeating every draw the same number of times. Both moves keep the empirical distribution. So a picture made from draws is well defined on distributions exactly when it does not change under these two moves (in the limit, for the second).

Placement of n draws Permute the draws Repeat every draw k times Verdict
a mean or quantile over the draws same same a function of the distribution
group by draw, additive blend at weight 1/n same same exactly a function of the distribution
group by draw, "over" blend at opacity 1/n, one color same same in the limit converges to a fixed tone map (Q3)
group by draw, opacity with a 0.05 floor same changes: darkens without bound does not converge in n (Q3)
spread by draw, draws in sampled order slots permute slots split a random picture whose law is fixed
spread by draw, draws sorted by value same same in the limit (a quantile curve) a function of the distribution
time.sequence by draw (HOP) frame order changes duration changes fixed only as "a random frame"
stack by draw same total grows k times not a function of the distribution: error
a line per draw (split by the draw key), shared draws same same in the limit a function of the joint distribution
a line per draw (split by the draw key), independent cells same same in the limit a random picture of a joint the data lacks
a line across draws (no split by the draw key) changes changes not a function of the distribution: error

The three line rows need one more idea. For independent cells, the law of the whole table of draws does not change if you permute the draw index separately in each cell. For shared (joint) draws, only permuting whole draws keeps it. A line per draw does not change when you permute whole draws, the same relabeling in every cell. So with shared draws it is well defined, and each line is a possible path. A line across draws changes even under that move, so it is an error. That is all rule 4 (proposal.md:1197 to :1208) checks: "a connecting mark needs a split by the draw key".

With independent cells, a line per draw is allowed, but it has a caveat that should be stated plainly. Permute the draws of one cell alone and the lines rewire. The law of the picture stays the same, because sample draws each cell on its own, so the picture is a fair random draw of the table under independence. But independence is a choice that sample makes, not a fact in the data. The data gives each cell's marginal and says nothing about how the cells move together. So lines over independent marginal draws show a joint that the data does not specify. For a forecast whose real paths are smooth, they zigzag more than any real path would. The checker cannot know the real joint, so this is a caveat for the reader and not an error. Shared draws are the way to show real paths. This is the same split as TFP's batch shape and event shape, and Fritz's Markov categories ("A synthetic approach to Markov kernels, conditional independence and theorems on sufficient statistics", Advances in Mathematics 370, 2020) give the general theory of which draws are independent.

A pseudo-type rule:

  key k has HasDraws { joint }          fold f over k
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── (draw-fold)
  f is allowed  iff  f(permute(xs)) = f(xs)  and  f(repeat(xs, m)) = f(xs)
  sum, count, stack-by-k         →  error (grows with m)
  mean, quantile, mixture, group by k at 1/n (additive)  →  ok

This also shows something the channel kinds already encode. A size channel sums, so it keeps the "split one amount into parts" move. A pos channel averages, so it keeps the "repeat every row" move (packages/gofish-ir/src/frontend/descriptors.ts:41 to :48; packages/gofish-graphics/src/ast/channels.ts:289 to :299). The proposal's new dist channel folds into a mixture, which also keeps "repeat every row". So the channel kinds are grouped by which re-representations of a group of rows they ignore, and draws provide only the pos-like moves.

One silent case is not caught by any of the five proposed rules. After sample, if a split puts several draws in one leaf and a pos channel folds them, the channel takes their mean. That is legal and it converges, but it erases the uncertainty. In K–S terms it is a confuser. A sixth check could warn: "circle cy averages 20 draws of value; the draws are not shown. Add spread({ by: "draw" }) or group({ by: "draw" }), or write value.mean() if a point estimate is what you want."

const value = dist.normal({ mean: "mean", sd: "se" });

// Silent today: each category's leaf holds 20 draws, and cy averages them.
// The chart converges, but it shows only the means (a confuser).
chart(estimates)
  .flow(spread({ by: "category", dir: "x" }), sample({ n: 20 }))
  .mark(circle({ cy: value, r: 3 }));

// What the warning would point to: place the draws.
chart(estimates)
  .flow(
    spread({ by: "category", dir: "x" }),
    sample({ n: 20 }),
    group({ by: "draw" })
  )
  .mark(circle({ cy: value, r: 3, opacity: field("draw").share() }));

1.7 Existing GoFish checks as K–S squares

For each check: the data symmetry α, the visual symmetry ω, and how well it fits.

Check α (data) ω (picture) Fit
CanAdd at a sum (checkSummable, measure.ts:142; a size channel, channels.ts:213; .sum(), fieldExpr.ts:460; a stack, distribute.ts:361) change of origin, such as Celsius to Kelvin (α = 1, a new representation) should be 1; a stack of n parts moves its top by n · 273.15 Exact. Summing is equivariant under scalings but not under shifts, so a type with no origin gives a hallucinator. The error text names this case: "temperatures, prices, rates" (measure.ts:155 to :160).
Instants have no sum, but their differences do (INSTANT and DURATION, measure.ts:73 to :86; the advice, measure.ts:147 to :153) move the epoch a bar from the epoch changes length Exact. This is Stevens' interval scale: differences are invariant under shifts, so "subtract a reference first" removes the hallucinator.
Two units on one axis (MeasureClash, measure.ts:531; unit inference, measure.ts:9 to :17) rescale one column's unit (USD to cents) and not the other one set of marks shrinks against the other Exact, and a free theorem. Unit variables that unify are Kennedy's units of measure ("Relational parametricity and units of measure", POPL 1997). Kennedy shows unit-generic programs are invariant under scaling each unit. Sharing an axis forces two unit variables to be equal, which is what the clash enforces.
One unit, two definitions (meetUnitClasses, measure.ts:196 to :217) none none Does not fit. This is coherence (one trait instance per type), a well-formedness rule about the spec, not about pictures.
Centered stack over HasMidpoint (spread.tsx:246 to :275), and its nonnegative-parts error (distribute.ts:363 to :371) reverse the level order about the midpoint reflect about the center line Exact for the centered layout. The error for a negative part is closer to Phrasing B: a centered stack's sides are magnitudes, so a negative one has no reading.
Channel kinds by aggregation (descriptors.ts:41 to :48) split one row into parts that sum (size); repeat a row (pos) the mark should not change Exact, as Section 1.6 shows. This is the cleanest instance: each kind is defined by which re-representation of a group it ignores.
Proposed: stack by a draw key (rule 2, proposal.md:1182) and a sum over draws (rule 3, proposal.md:1190) repeat every draw (n to 2n draws of the same distribution) the stack doubles Exact. A hallucinator under a change of sample size. This is a firmer reason than "the sum has no meaning".
Proposed: a line across draws (rule 4, proposal.md:1197) permute whole draws the path through the draws reorders Exact. A line per draw passes. Over independent cells it still rewires under a permutation inside one cell. That is the caveat of Section 1.6, a joint the data lacks, and not an error.
Proposed: summaries need a trait of T (rule 5, proposal.md:1209; types.md:94 to :101) relabel the categories (a symmetry of a nominal type) the "median category" changes Exact. Stevens' permissible statistics.
Proposed: a Dist in a one-value channel (rule 1, proposal.md:1173) change the spread, keep the center none, if GoFish silently took the mean Loose. The rule prevents a confuser by refusing to pick an implicit summary. It is a type error more than a symmetry.
HasVector for stack (research only) reorder the parts reorder the segments, top fixed Loose. HasVector is not in the code; a search of packages/ finds no match. It comes from the October 2026 "space as traits" research. The rule is about the net amount, which is invariant under reordering, so it fits only for the top of the stack.
No check today: a nominal key with an ordered placement permute the categories permute the slots A gap. A by key without HasOrder (packages/gofish-graphics/src/ast/schema.ts:89 to :94) gets slots in order. The picture permutes when the labels do, which is fine, but a viewer may read the order. This would be a lint, not an error.

One more invariance came up while checking these. GoFish fits a σ-scope to the data, so multiplying a column by k should leave the geometry unchanged and only relabel ticks. That holds for powers of ten, but niceContinuous rounds the domain to nice ticks (underlyingSpace.ts:360), and nice ticks do not scale by arbitrary factors. A tallest bar of 1.37 gets a domain of about 1.4, and the same bar in units three times smaller, 4.11, gets about 4.5. So changing centimeters to inches changes the empty space above the bars. It is a small hallucinator, and it is the kind of thing metamorphic testing finds: render a story with one column multiplied by 2.54 and run capture-diff.

Q2. Section 4's hurdles and the proposal

Classes: (a) falls out of GoFish's semantics today, (b) needs a new piece already in the proposal, (c) needs a change to GoFish's core semantics, (d) unsolved.

Hurdle (paper section) How the proposal answers it Class
Scales must act on distributions by change of variable (4.1, Definition 4.1) Draws are plain rows (types.md:150 to :151), so each draw goes through the σ-affine map like any value (underlying-space.md:110). A consumed density (slab) under an affine scale only rescales. Under a nonlinear scale or coordinate system (Scale.probit, proposal.md:470; Coord.polar), the density needs the Jacobian of the map, and the proposal does not say whether slab draws density per data unit or per pixel. (a) for draws; (d) for densities under nonlinear maps
The axis domain of a distribution (4.1, the scale's domain) Open decision 8 (proposal.md:1313). Section 1.5 adds a constraint: the extent must be a continuous summary of the distribution, such as fixed quantiles. The largest of n normal draws grows like s·√(2 ln n), so an extent from draws never converges as n grows. (b), with a rule the proposal lacks
A point estimate hides the uncertainty (4.2.2) GoFish allows summaries on purpose, since the signal marks need them, but only when written (value.mean()). Rule 1 refuses a Dist in a one-value channel with no lift (proposal.md:1173). The silent case of Section 1.6, a pos fold over several draws after sample, is not caught. (b), plus the new warning of 1.6
Probability functions change the domain (4.2.3: a density does not live on the data's scale) Signal marks take the whole Dist in the new dist channel kind (types.md:179 to :190) and draw a new axis whose unit is a density or a share (types.md:100). Feeding a probability into a channel of the data's unit is then a unit clash, which GoFish raises today (measure.ts:531). So the mismatch the paper fears is a type error. GoFish does not have to ban densities, which is why it can keep both signal and noise. (b) dist channel; the clash itself is (a)

Quantiles do not extend to several dimensions (4.2.4, Figure 6) dist.mvnormal is read through its marginals p.x and p.y (proposal.md:405 to :406), so a joint quantile is never formed. For whole curves, Band.depth (proposal.md:1035 to :1046) orders curves from the center outward by band depth, which is one principled multivariate order the paper does not consider. Joint quantiles for points stay out. (b) for curves; (d) for joint regions
Samples only, with the draw id in the group (4.2.5, Figure 7) sample adds a draw key (proposal.md:402 to :403), and draws are placed by operators split by that key. Statistics then run per leaf, because marks and later steps run once per leaf of a split (createOperator.ts:1218). So the per-draw grouping is GoFish's existing nested split, Option 1D's fmap. The bootstrap example's derive(fitLine) "runs once per draw" for this reason (proposal.md:906 to :907). GoFish keeps signal marks too, which the paper excludes for EDA; the two coexist because the type says which one a channel is. (a)
Flatten the draw array with equal weights (4.3) Draws are rows, so there is nothing to flatten (types.md:150). Unequal weights come from the column record's weight (types.md:126 to :141). (a) for rows; (b) for weights
Nested position adjustments, within and between draws (Definition 4.3, 4.4) A flow is already a nest of operators, so spread(region), sample, spread(draw), stack(product) is ggdibbler's stack_dodge (proposal.md:784 to :794). x is spread, t is time.sequence and z is group, all today. group({ by: "draw" }) places nothing, so the draws share one frame (proposal.md:512 to :520). Only the opacity rule is new. The pixel map's subdivide is a single-key table in flow form, a small new piece. (a) for x, t and z; (b) the opacity rule, single-key table

The best adjustment depends on the aesthetic; color with transparency leaves the palette (4.3.1, Figure 9) No answer. Draws in one frame over a fill that reads a Dist blend palette colors into colors that are not in the legend. A type knows both facts (the channel is color, the draws share one frame through group), so a lint could suggest table or spread by draw instead. See Q3 for the exact limit color. (d), easy lint
Several uncertain channels want different adjustments (end of 4.3.1) sample draws every Dist channel of a drawn row together ("any Dist read on a drawn row reads that draw", proposal.md:402 to :403), so there is one draw key and one placement for all of them. Placing position draws in one frame and color draws by subdivision would need two independent draw keys, one per channel. The proposal dropped sample's as option (proposal.md:400 to :401), which is what that would need. (c): a draw key per channel, a product of samples
The *_sample suffix because ggplot2 is not polymorphic (5.1) Not a hurdle in GoFish. The same mark reads a Dist once sample is in the flow, so there is one surface. (a)

How many draws (5.3, the times argument) sample({ n }) or sample({ coverage }). The design note's question 10 asks for a convergence test (distributions.md:734). Q3 below proposes one. (d)

Q3. Emergent blur versus direct blur

3.1 What the limit of an overlay is

Overlay n draws of a mark, each at weight 1/n, with additive blending. Each pixel's ink is then the average over draws of "does this draw cover the pixel". As n grows this converges to the expected image, E[render(V(X))]. For each channel kind, that expectation has a plain form.

  • Position (blur). A dot whose center is uncertain. Rendering a dot at p is its footprint K shifted to p. So the expected image is K convolved with the pushforward of the distribution through the position map. If the map is affine with σ pixels per unit and the cell is N(m, s²), the pushforward is N(σm + o, (σs)²), and the blur is a Gaussian of standard deviation σ·s pixels.
  • Size (fuzziness). A circle whose radius R is uncertain. A pixel at distance d from the center is covered when R ≥ d. So its expected ink is P(R ≥ d), the complement of the radius's CDF. This is not a convolution. It is a radial gradient.
  • A box end. A bar from 0 to H. A pixel at height y is covered when H ≥ y, so the fade is P(H ≥ y). A bar from start to end gives P(start ≤ t ≤ end), which is the formula in the proposal's uncertain timeline (proposal.md:1112 to :1116).
  • Color. A fill whose value is uncertain. The expected color is the mean of the palette colors in the blending space, weighted by probability. It is usually not a palette color, which is the paper's Figure 9 problem.

This explains the paper's ggdist "cliff" (Figure 14). The blur there is attached to the dot's raw standard error. But a dotplot's dot position comes from binning and stacking, and the pushforward of a normal through a bin and a stack count is discrete: a dot jumps between stacks. No smooth kernel equals that. The blur was attached to the wrong map.

Two facts follow for the proposal.

  1. The 0.05 floor breaks convergence. With "over" compositing of one color at opacity a, a pixel covered by k of the draws shows 1 − (1 − a)^k. With a = 1/n and k = p·n this tends to 1 − e^(−p), a fixed function of the coverage probability p. So the over-composited overlay converges, to a tone map of p. With a floor, such as the earlier draft's max(0.05, w / Σw), any n above 20 uses a = 0.05, and 1 − 0.95^(p·n) tends to 1 for every p > 0. Every pixel any draw touches goes fully dark as n grows. The floor exists because 8-bit alpha cannot hold 1/n for large n, so the fix is in the renderer (accumulate coverage at higher precision), not a floor. Open decision 5 (proposal.md:1290) keeps this for both of its options.
  2. The proposal's two fades converge to different pictures. The uncertain timeline gives a gradient with opacity P(start ≤ t ≤ end) and a noise form of 50 overlaid draws (proposal.md:1112 to :1125). Over-composited, the noise form tends to 1 − e^(−P), not P. They agree only if the draws blend additively, for example by accumulating coverage in an SVG mask with plus-lighter blending and then filling through the mask, or if gradient applies the same tone map. The note in noise.md that compositing is order-dependent (noise.md:24 to :26) is true across colors. For one color the result depends only on the count, so this limit is well defined.

There is a clean way to say which blend is right. Fritz and Perrone show that the algebras of the Kantorovich monad are closed convex sets with affine maps, so "take the expected value" is exactly an algebra map. Images under additive blending form such a convex set, so the additive overlay's limit is the algebra map applied to the pushforward. "Over" compositing is not affine, so it is not an algebra. It still has a limit, but only after a nonlinear tone map.

Also, types.md:269 to :270 says "one Dist end follows its density". By the limits above, a box with one uncertain end fades as the complement of the CDF, not the density. The density belongs to a different picture: a single uncertain point drawn as a band (ggdist's gradient interval). The proposal should keep the two apart, and the channel kind decides which one is meant: a Dist in a point's position channel gives a density band, and a Dist in a box's end gives a CDF fade.

3.2 Options for direct rendering

Option 3A: no direct form, always sample. This is ggdibbler's choice (Section 5.3). It is correct by construction and needs no new theory. It costs n times the nodes (design note question 11, distributions.md:738), it is grainy at small n, and it renders differently on every run unless seeded.

Option 3B: a direct form as an optimization of sample plus group (strongest). The user writes the noise form. The meaning is the limit. When the type says a closed form exists, the renderer replaces the draws with it, as a compiler rewrite rule replaces code with faster code that means the same thing. The type needs four facts per channel, and GoFish has three of them already:

Fact Where it comes from
the source Dist and its family the DistAccessor (types.md:155 to :169)
the map from the Dist to pixels: affine, monotone, continuous, or discontinuous the σ-scope (underlying-space.md:110) plus Option 1B's classes for every step on the path
dependence between Dists that meet HasDraws { joint } (types.md:138 to :140)
the blend and its limit tone map the opacity rule and the blend mode (open decision 5)

A pseudo-type rule:

  channel c reads X : Dist<T>, X = normal(m, s)       path(X → c) is affine with σ, o
  c is a point position                                the draws blend additively
  ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── (blur-normal)
  direct render = mark footprint ⊛ Gaussian(σ·m + o, σ·s)   (an SVG feGaussianBlur)

  c is a box end, path monotone with inverse g        ───────────── (fade-end)
  direct render = gradient with opacity 1 − F_X(g(px))

  path(X → c) contains a discontinuous step            ───────────── (no-direct)
  direct render = sample, n chosen by the convergence check

In the proposal's spelling, the user writes only the noise form. The first chart below meets blur-normal: cy is a position in a σ-scope, and nothing between the Dist and the pixels jumps. The second chart meets no-direct, because Overlap.separate() moves each dot by where the others landed, so it is sampled.

const value = dist.normal({ mean: "mean", sd: "se" });

// blur-normal: rendered as one dot per category with a Gaussian blur of σ·se px.
chart(estimates)
  .flow(
    spread({ by: "category", dir: "x" }),
    sample({ n: 200 }),
    group({ by: "draw" })
  )
  .mark(circle({ cy: value, r: 3, opacity: field("draw").share() }));

// no-direct: a collision layout sits on the path, so the draws are drawn.
chart(estimates)
  .flow(
    sample({ n: 200 }),
    group({ by: "draw" }),
    scatter({ x: value, overlap: Overlap.separate() })
  )
  .mark(circle({ r: 3, opacity: field("draw").share() }));

A concrete closed form that the rule finds: the uncertain stacked bar (proposal.md:796 to :812). Each boundary is a running sum of the parts below it. With independent normal parts, the boundary after k parts is normal with mean Σ m_i and variance Σ s_i². If the parts are never negative, the chance that height y shows color k is F_{k−1}(y) − F_k(y), the difference of two normal CDFs. So the overlaid stacked bar has an exact direct render as a stack of gradients. With joint draws, the variances come from the draws instead, and the same formula holds.

What 3B cannot do: correlated (x, y) positions need a rotated kernel, which SVG blurs cannot express without a transform; a heavy-tailed family under a log scale has no Gaussian form; color under overlay has a closed form that is still off-palette.

Option 3C: blur as a user aesthetic (ggdist's blur). Short to write, and it is what users ask for. The paper's cliff shows the cost: the user picks the kernel, and nothing ties it to the map the uncertainty actually goes through. If GoFish offered it, the type from 3B could at least check it: "blur on cy is the standard error of value, but cy is placed by a stack of counts, so the blur does not describe where the dot could be".

Option 3D: verified direct renders. Keep 3B's rewrites, and test each one: render the direct form and the sampled form at increasing n with a fixed seed, and check that the image distance shrinks like 1/√n. This is the paper's visual convergence (Definition 3.4) as a CI test, and it is design note question 10. capture-diff compares normalized geometry, not pixels, so this test needs a pixel comparison, which GoFish's visual baselines already have.

Option 3E: an invisibility lint. With σ known after layout, the pixel standard deviation of each uncertain channel is σ·s. When it is below about one pixel, the uncertainty cannot be seen, which is the paper's just noticeable difference turned into a confuser warning: "the draws of value vary by 0.3 px on this axis; the uncertainty will not show". This needs only 3B's first two facts.

The strongest combination looks like 3B with 3D as its guard and 3E as a lint. The user writes the noise form, the system renders the limit exactly when the type proves a closed form exists, and CI checks that the closed form really is the limit.

Related questions Josh did not ask

  1. Is "visual convergence" one property or two? It is two: shrinking uncertainty (the CMT) and growing samples (a law of large numbers for the overlay). They fail in different places.
  2. Does the axis extent converge? Not if it comes from the largest draw, since that grows with n. It must come from a fixed summary such as quantiles (open decision 8).
  3. Does the opacity rule for draws in one frame converge as n grows? Not with a 0.05 floor, and the blend mode decides what it converges to.
  4. Should a pos fold over several draws after sample warn, since it silently shows a point estimate?
  5. Does equivariance lift from plain charts to uncertain charts for free? Yes, because the probability monad is a functor. Is that worth a property test per existing check?
  6. Is a dodge by draw a function of the distribution? Only if the draws are sorted by value within the slot. Should spread({ by: "draw" }) sort by default?
  7. Should sorting categories by a sampled value (a bar order per HOP frame) be allowed? It is discontinuous, but the discontinuity is what shows rank uncertainty.
  8. Does filter on a Dist break continuity? A threshold is a jump, so a per-draw filter is continuousExcept and needs the limit point off the threshold.
  9. Can metamorphic tests (multiply a column by a constant, permute draws, repeat draws) become a standard story check under capture-diff?
  10. Is the density of slab measured per data unit or per pixel under a nonlinear scale?
  11. Two uncertain channels with different placements need two draw keys. Is a product of samples worth the complexity?
  12. Selection over draws: Bartonicek et al. find stacking is the safe direction for selection, and draws forbid stacking. Does the replication test predict where highlighting works on an uncertain stacked bar?

Open questions

  1. Is Phrasing A (the encoding as a natural transformation of symmetry actions) worth writing into api-design.md as the one-line meaning of "meaning lives on the data type"?
  2. Should continuity classes (Option 1B) live on operator descriptors, next to the channel kinds, so the Python side gets them for free?
  3. Should the draw-fold rule of Section 1.6 replace rules 2 and 3 with one rule, "a fold over a draw key must ignore permutation and repetition"? That would also cover future folds without a new rule each.
  4. Should the direct render (Option 3B) be in #980's first step, or wait until sampling is built and the convergence test exists?
  5. Should draws in one frame blend additively in a mask so that their limit is the expected image, or keep "over" compositing and give gradient the matching tone map? Where the blend is set, on the mark or on group, is part of the question.
  6. Is the sample effect worth tracking as an effect, or is a "has an unseeded sample" flag on the chart enough?
  7. How far should GoFish follow Aizenman et al.'s split into encoders and compositors? It matches GoFish's channels and marks, and it may give a place to hang the per-channel continuity class.
